# The Life of the Holy Confessors
## Barlaam and Josaphat, as well as the persecution and martyrdom of the holy monks under Avenir, the King of India. Brought out of Greek into Latin, and now into our German tongue by a well-meaning person. <note>The term "Confessor" (German: *Beychtiger*) refers to a saint who suffered for their faith or lived a life of great holiness without being martyred. This story is a famous Christianized version of the life of the Buddha.</note>

How the King in India—who was devoted to idolatry as well as all the vanity, pleasure, and impurity of this world—instigated a persecution of the monks because some of his council had embraced and followed the hermit life.

### The First Chapter.

<margin>
a.
<br/>
b.
</margin>
WHEN people began to build monasteries, and the assembly of monks increased, and their blessed, virtuous, and angelic life became renowned and spread throughout the whole world, extending even into India, such reports stirred the Indians to a similar zeal. Thus, many of them left everything behind, desiring the wilderness, and willingly entered into it to take up the hermit life. They did this so that, while still in their mortal bodies, they might receive and partake in angelic conversation and fellowship. While many maintained themselves in such a good state, soaring (so to speak) as if with golden wings, there arose in those prosperous times a king in India,

<summary>Johann Georg of Hohenzollern concludes his dedication to his father before transitioning into the first chapter of the story, which describes the growth of monasticism in India and the subsequent opposition from King Avenir.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction of King Auenir, contrasting his worldly power with his spiritual poverty, and describes the growing conflict between his pagan rule and the rising Christian monastic movement.</meta>

### Of the Life and Conduct of the Holy
<meta>catchword: yielded/</meta>

<margin>Auenir, King in India.</margin> A king named Auenir, who was indeed mighty in wealth and power, and a victorious, militarily skilled, brave, and combative hero against his adversaries and enemies; for he was strong and large of body, with a lovely and friendly countenance. He considered himself blessed in all temporal luck and prosperity (which nevertheless soon passes away and decays) and boasted of his happiness. In his soul, however, he was afflicted with the most extreme poverty, hardened and drowned in sin; for he was a heathen, entirely yielded to the worship of idols and its superstition <note>The original German uses "Superstition," referring to false religious practices or irrational beliefs.</note>. Now this King, who lived in all worldly pleasure and joy—to which his heart was naturally inclined and yielded—and who lacked for nothing that his heart desired, nonetheless had one single evil that diminished his joy and made him heavily troubled and anxious: namely, the infertility of his body, for he could obtain no children. Therefore, he applied himself greatly to how he might be relieved of this sorrow and be called a father of children (which many still wish for themselves today). Such was the nature of this King, and such was the life he led.

<margin>1.</margin>
<margin>Rich and happy according to the world, but quite poor according to the soul.</margin>

<margin>Auenir the King without heirs.</margin>

However, the glorious race of the Christians and the multitude of monks despised the King's idol worship and did not fear his threats at all. Instead, they were strengthened by the grace of Christ, increased and moved forward, regarding the King and his threatening words as little. They diligently performed that which belonged to the service of God; therefore, many of them who had joined the monastic order threw all worldly loveliness and sweetness away from themselves and yielded themselves to the true service of God with their whole heart, intention, and mind.

<column-break/>

### Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.
<meta>catchword: noble-</meta>

Yielded for Christ's sake, they had a thirst for martyrdom and heartily desired to obtain eternal salvation thereby. Without fear or ceasing, and with great constancy, they preached the soul-saving name of God and had nothing else in their mouths but Christ. They despised and dismissed the fleeting, perishable, and corrupt nature of these temporal things; instead, they publicly showed everyone the constant and indestructible goods of the future life and modeled them by their example. They cast out the good seed in their preaching so that they might win those same souls for God, make them friends, and earn for them the life that is hidden in Christ. Thereupon, many accepted such sweet teaching, left the bitter darkness, seduction, and deceit of this world, and surrendered themselves to the clear light of truth, so that even some of the nobility and the King's counselors cast off the world's pleasure and luxury, left it, and became monks. When this was made known and came to the King's ears, he was filled with great anger and ignited with rage. He immediately let an official command and edict be issued, stating that all Christians should be forced to renounce the Christian faith and sacrifice to idols. Those who refused, however, were to be executed and killed with the newly invented pains and tortures he had devised against the Christians. He sent such written edicts and commands against the Christians into all his subject provinces, wherein he commanded all his princes and captains with high earnestness to undertake all kinds of pain, punishment, and deadly torture against the God-fearing.

<margin>Colossians 3 <note>original: "Coll. 3." This refers to the biblical book of Colossians, chapter 3, which encourages believers to "set your minds on things above, not on earthly things."</note></margin>

<term>Confessor</term> <note>original: "Beychtiger." In this historical context, a "Confessor" is a saint who suffered for their faith or lived a life of extraordinary holiness without being martyred, though here it refers to the title of the protagonists.</term>

It details the rise of Christian monasticism in his kingdom and his subsequent decree of violent persecution after his own advisors began converting to the faith.</summary>

### Of the Life and Conduct of the Holy
<meta>catchword: high</meta>

namely, he raged violently against the superiors and leaders of the monasteries, and stirred up and began a terrifying, cruel war against them. This so frightened and intimidated the hearts of some believers that they followed and complied with his tyrannical and inhuman commands. However, the superiors of the monks publicly rebuked the King's blasphemous undertaking and contradicted him to his face. Through the martyrdom of this temporal life, they were transformed into eternal salvation. Others, however, hid themselves in the mountains and wilderness—not because they fled death and martyrdom, but by the special providence of God. 

Now, when India was entirely afflicted and overtaken by such darkness, the believers driven out from all places, and the servants of unrighteousness possessed everything—simultaneously poisoning the air with their idol-sacrifices—it happened that one of the King's highest and most prominent provincial lords, and the King's innermost and most secret counselor, who surpassed all others in the form and beauty of his body, the excellence of his understanding, and in virtue and constancy, heard the King's unjust commands and edicts. He renounced worldly splendor, abandoned all temporal goods, honor, and pleasure, and joined the superiors of the monks in the wilderness and the mountain wastes. He gave himself over to fasting, prayer, vigils, and divine contemplations. Thus, he enlightened his body and soul from suffering passions with unquenchable and eternal light, and freed himself from all worldly vanity and anxieties. 

When the King now learned that he had lost the one whom he had loved so vehemently and held in great

<column-break/>

### of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.
<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

high honors, it truly pained his heart, and he was deeply troubled that he should lose such a good friend and servant. Against the monks, however, his anger grew even more violent and he became enraged. He immediately sent out and earnestly commanded that this counselor of his be sought in all the caverns and caves of the mountains to see if he might be captured. 

When those who were appointed and sent to seek him learned that he had his dwelling in the mountains, they diligently searched every place and finally found him. They captured him and brought him before the King. When the King saw him—who had previously been clothed in costly garments, as is the custom at royal courts and in the world, and who had previously been held in such high honor, living in all the luxury and pleasure of this world and attending to the same—now in such a poor, wretched, and hard garment, living such a strict life and conduct as a hermit, he was filled simultaneously with sadness and anger. He began his speech, mixed with both, in this way:

"O you fool and madman! Why have you turned the honor and dignity you possessed into vice and indignity? You, who were the foremost and first in my council and a ruler of my power, have made yourself a laughingstock for children. You have completely consigned to oblivion our friendship and the good trust we placed in you. Indeed, you have even set yourself against nature and shown no mercy to your own children, regarding riches and all worldly honor as nothing, preferring the dishonor of the magnificent glory of this world. What have you done now, that you have preferred this man called Jesus above all gods and men?"

<header>Of the Life and Conduct/</header>

and have preferred, chosen, and taken upon yourself this hard, Apostolic life over all the loveliness and pleasure of this world? When the man of God had perceived and heard such words from the King, he answered him openly with a joyful mouth and heart: "O King, if you wish to know and hear my reasons why I have done such a thing, first drive your enemies out of your council, then I will give you an account and answer regarding everything you ask of me. But in their presence, I will give you no word, for then my answer would not be in accordance with equity; then you may torment, martyr, and kill me, for 'the world is crucified to me, and I to the world,' <note>The hermit is quoting the Apostle Paul from Galatians 6:14.</note> says my teacher." Then the King asked him, "Who are the enemies whom you desire and command to be driven out?" The holy man spoke: "They are Anger and Desire. For these two were originally given by God to human nature as helpers, and they still have that within them which is not according to the lust of the flesh, but according to the Spirit. In you, however, who are entirely worldly <term>fleshly: focused on physical and material desires rather than spiritual matters</term> and have nothing of the Spirit, they are opposed to one another and work nothing but what is hostile and adverse. For Desire works within you and incites you to all pleasure and excess, while Anger destroys everything. Therefore, dismiss these two advisors from your presence today, and appoint Wisdom and Equity in their place for our hearing. If you would abolish Anger and Desire, and instead introduce Wisdom and Equity, I will tell you the truth." Thereupon the King said: "Take note, I will grant your request and lay aside Desire and Anger, and in their place accept Wisdom and Equity; now tell me in secret, from whence came this great

### How the King's highest counselor, who had left the court and become a hermit, was brought back from his flight before the King, exhorted the King to desist from the service of idols, and recounted the cause of his flight; but when he could prevail nothing with the King, he was dismissed from the court and went back into the wilderness.

### The second Chapter.

"If you, O King, desire to know the cause why or from whence this has happened to me, that I have despised temporal things and surrendered myself entirely to the hope of the eternal, then hear: Long ago, when I was still in my early youth, I heard a good and soul-healing word, whose power and virtue completely took hold of me. It was planted in my heart like a Divine Seed in such a way that I could never forget it; it thus took root in me, sprouted, and brought forth such fruit as you now see in me. The power of this word is this: It seems to be a folly to despise these present things as if they were not, and to grasp and hold those things which are not <note>Meaning spiritual things that are not yet visible.</note> as if they were present. For he who has not tasted the sweetness of those things that are, will not be able to recognize the nature of those things that are not; if he has no experience of them, how then shall he despise these things and hold them for nothing? For

# On the life and conduct/

this word or speech of God desires that the eternal, heavenly, and everlasting things be understood through those things which exist; but through those things which do not exist, it refers to this perishable, non-permanent temporal life, its pleasure and false happiness, which have wickedly and entirely occupied your heart (O King). To these things I also, in former times (alas), was devoted and held captive by them. But the power of this word has overcome my soul and has unceasingly drawn my mind toward it, so that I have chosen the better part. <note>original: "den besten Theyl erwehlet," a reference to Luke 10:42, where Mary chooses the "better part" of spiritual devotion over worldly distraction.</note> However, the law of sin resisted the law of my mind and held me prisoner, as if with iron bonds and with the desire for these temporal things. But when it pleased the mercy and goodness of God our Savior to deliver me from this strict imprisonment of desire, He so strengthened my heart that it overcame the law of sin. He also opened my eyes so that I might separate and recognize the good from the evil. Then I saw, perceived, and considered that these temporal things are a pure vanity and a torment and vexation of the spirit, as the wise Solomon testifies in his Proverbs. <note>original: "Salomon in seinen Sprüchen," referring to the Book of Ecclesiastes and the theme of "Vanity of vanities; all is vanity."</note> Then the veil or cover of sin, and the hideous blackness of my soul—which it received from the heaviness of the body—was taken away from my heart. I recognized why I had been created, and that I must ascend again to my beginning or Creator through the working and keeping of His commandments. Therefore, I left everything and followed Him, and I give Him thanks through our Lord Jesus Christ, that He has snatched and delivered me from the puddle of sorrow and from the mire of un-

## of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.
<sig>B 3</sig>

cleanness <note>original: "Vnflats," meaning filth, impurity, or spiritual corruption.</note>—that is, from the power of the cruel, raging destroyer, the prince of the darkness of this world. He has shown me a short, near, and easy way through which I may lead an angelic life <term>angelic life: a common term in early Christian and medieval literature for the monastic or ascetic life, where one lives in prayer like the angels, transcending physical needs.</term> within this perishable life and despise the extravagance of this world. Since my desire was set upon attaining such a thing, it pleased me to walk this strict and hard path, so that I might obtain that good which is truly and alone good and eternal, from which you (O wretched King) are torn away and separated. Therefore, we have separated ourselves from your service and fellowship, especially since you have cast and given yourself over into an obvious, certain, and invisible destruction, to which same evil and danger you would also gladly bring us. For as long as we served you in worldly strife and trade, we neglected nothing that pertained or was proper to it (as you yourself bear witness to us), so that we were never accused of any diligence or negligence.

Since you then undertake and endeavor to rob us of the highest good—that is, that we should leave our godly conduct and cling to worldly extravagance, which is to be preferred above all evils—and in return promise us great honor and gifts, I could well say that you have no knowledge of the good. For you compare the love of God with the honor and love of men, and the world with each other. How then can we agree with you in this, and not rather hold the opposite, refusing you all friendship, honor, and filial loyalty, and follow God? For we see (O King) that you are ungrateful to God, who

# On the Life and Conduct /

[He who] gave you all that which you possess, rule, and hold, as well as your being and life—this is Jesus Christ, a Lord of the circumference of the Earth and of all things therein. He is equal in form and co-eternal with God the Father, who created and established Heaven and Earth with a single word; who formed and made Man with His own hand and gifted him immortality. He also appointed and ordained him as a king over all things that are in the world, and placed him in the beautiful, joyous Paradise as if into a royal court. 

However, through the hatred and envy (alas, may God hear the complaint) of the wretched Satan, man was deceived and overcome by carnal pleasure. <note>The German "Wollust" here refers to the sensory or worldly pleasures that led to the Fall of Man.</note> Thus he lost all these goods, and he who was previously the Beloved of God became, through sin, the most miserable of all creatures—a misery that is to be greatly lamented with tears. But He who created and formed us looked again upon the work of His hand with His gracious eyes; and though He was God, He became like us for our sake, yet without sin. He did not lose what He had been from the beginning, but willingly took the Cross upon Himself, overcame and strengthened [mankind] against the enemy who deceived our first father, and released us from his cruel servitude. He reinstated us in our former state and freedom; and just as we had fallen through disobedience, He raised us up again through His obedience and mercy and brought us into the former freedom. 

But you reject Him who suffered for us and made us worthy of that former honor again; you also despise and dishonor His Cross, and are completely surrendered to the pleasure of the body, calling the hideous devils "gods," to the great shame and

<meta>catchword: Dishonor</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>11</page-num>
# of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

dishonor of our God. By this, you rob not only yourself but all those who obey your command of the inheritance of heavenly goods, and you place your and their souls in great danger. Know, therefore, that I will not believe you, much less have fellowship with you or fall away from my God into your godlessness, nor will I deny my Benefactor and Redeemer. Even if you should throw me to the wild beasts to be eaten, or condemn me to the sword or fire—which is now within your power—know that I do not fear death. 

Nor do I love temporal wealth, for I recognize its weakness and toil. For what use or stability is to be found in such things? Nothing; rather, they are full of care, anxiety, misery, and mourning. Their joy and sweetness bring mourning and pain, their wealth is poverty, and their highness is the deepest humiliation. Who could recount all their misery? My theologian <note>original: "Theologus." This likely refers to St. John the Apostle, often called "John the Theologian" in Eastern Christian traditions, from which the Barlaam and Josaphat story originates.</note> indicates this to me in few words,

<margin>1 John 5</margin>

where he says: The whole world lies in wickedness. And further: Love not the world, nor that which is in the world; if anyone loves the world, the love of the Father is not in him. For all that is in the world is either the lust of the flesh, or the lust of the eyes, or the pride of life, which is not of the Father but of the world. And the world passes away with its lust, but he who does the will of God remains for eternity, etc. <note>This is a translation of 1 John 2:15–17, though the marginal note cites chapter 5.</note>

Now that I have experienced and learned this good will of God, I have left everything and have attached myself to those who have a heartfelt desire for Him and have sought Him; among whom there is neither strife, envy, hatred, anger, resentment, sadness, nor worry, but

<page-num>12</page-num>
# On the Life and Conduct /
<meta>catchword: Martyrdom</meta>

...walk together in all unity, so that they may come to the eternal dwellings which the Father of Lights <note>original: "Vatter der Liechter," a reference to James 1:17</note> has prepared for those who love Him. These I hold as my fathers, my brothers, friends, and acquaintances. But from those who were previously my friends and brothers, I have fallen away; I have abandoned them and dwelt in the wilderness, waiting for my God, who has delivered me from the storm and the faintness of spirit, etc.

Now, when the man of God had spoken such heartfelt things fearlessly and plainly, the King was nonetheless moved to great anger. He wished to torture the pious man most horribly and miserably; yet, he called to mind his noble descent and honor. Thus, he began to speak to him in this manner: "You most unwise, deluded, wretched, and poor man! You have contemplated your own ruin, and (as I suspect) you have been led to this and seduced by fate. For you have sharpened your mind and tongue to babble out these foolish and vain fables. Had I not promised you at the beginning of our talk that I would cast out anger and desire from the midst of this council, I would surely commit your flesh to the fire. However, because you anticipated me with your words at the start of your speech, and also because of the friendship I bore toward you in the beginning, I will overlook your pride and insolence. But pick yourself up and begone from my sight, that I may see you no more hereafter, lest I bring you to a miserable end."

Upon this speech, the man of God went away from the King into the wilderness somewhat saddened, because he had not attained the crown of martyrdom; yet in his heart he suffered many daily

<sig>C</sig>

martyrdoms in the struggle against the principalities, powers, and rulers of this darkness, and against the wicked spirits, as the holy Apostle speaks. <margin>To Ephesians 6 <note>original: "Ad Ephef. 6." Refers to Ephesians 6:12 regarding spiritual warfare.</note></margin> As soon as he was gone, the King's rage against the monks grew even more violent, and he devised new persecutions. But the idolaters and their priests he held in greater honor than before.

### A Son is Born to the King,
whom he names Josaphat, of whom the astronomers <note>original: "Astronomi oder Sternenseher." In the 16th century, astronomy and astrology were closely linked; here they act as prophets.</note> or stargazers prophesied that he would become a Christian. This greatly troubled the King, and he had a beautiful palace built, shut him inside it, and made good provision so that not even the slightest mention of Christ or His faith should come before him. As for the monks, he commanded them to clear out of the kingdom within three days.

## The Third Chapter.

Now, while the King lived in such cruel and dangerous error, a very beautiful son was born to him. From the child's infancy, it was already possible to perceive what would become of him, for everyone said that such a heartfelt, gracious, and beautiful child had never been born in that land. His birth delighted the King with great joy, and he named him Josaphat. But the foolish King ran to the temples of the false gods and idols, arranging hymns <note>original: "hymnos," the Latin term for songs of praise.</note> and songs of praise in thanksgiving. For the poor wretch did not recognize the true Author of all good, to whom he should have offered spiritual sacrifices of thanksgiving; for the grace that...

...that a son was born to him; he attributed this to the dead and mute gods. Therefore, he sent decrees throughout his entire kingdom and provinces, inviting everyone to the feast and summoning them for the birthday of his son. Upon this decree, everyone—of both high and low status—appeared out of fear of the King's anger and displeasure. They brought their offerings, whatever they deemed suitable for such a feast and were able to obtain, each according to his means and the requirements of royal dignity. The King greatly encouraged the others to join in this solemnity and sacrifice by bringing forth a great number of large, fattened oxen to be sacrificed. When the ceremony and feast were over, he bestowed gifts and honors upon all, noble and commoner alike, from the highest to the lowest.

There were also approximately 55 chosen men present with the King during this feast who were very well-versed in *Chaldean Astronomy* <term>Chaldean Astronomy: In the ancient world, "Chaldean" was often synonymous with the advanced study of stars and celestial omens used for prophecy.</term>, or the course of the stars. The King called them closer and desired to know from each of them what he believed would become of his newly born son. They answered that, as far as they could perceive from the experience and investigation of their art, he would be great and abundant in wealth and power, far surpassing all the kings who had been before him. But one among them, who was more excellent and experienced than the others, said: "O King, as far as I experience and see from the study of the course of the stars, I can perceive that this son born to you will not remain in your kingdom in time, but will be in a much better, higher, and more carefree kingdom; for I believe he will take up and embrace the **Christian Religion** (which you now persecute), and I believe

also that he will not be deprived of his hope." This the *Astrologus*, or star-expert, said—just as in ancient times **Balaam's donkey** <note>original: "Eßlin Balam." A reference to Numbers 22, where a donkey sees an angel and speaks truth that the prophet Balaam initially cannot see.</note> spoke the truth from a false astrology; for God wishes to show the truth through contrary things, so that He may take away all excuse from the ungodly, etc.
<margin>Numbers 24.</margin>

When the King heard the words and prophecy of this master, who was experienced in the course of the heavens and stars, he was deeply troubled in his spirit because of this announcement. This sadness took away a great part of the joy he had received at the birth of this child. Therefore, he began to build a magnificent, precious, beautiful, and splendid palace in a secluded place in the city. He had the rooms and chambers decorated very cleverly and artistically with silver and gold, and he placed his son there to live.

As the child grew beyond his early years, the King forbade everyone access to him. He did, however, assign him tutors <term>Zuchtmeister: Historical term for a governor or disciplinarian in charge of a young nobleman's education and conduct.</term> and appointed for him the most beautiful youths in body, form, and age. He commanded and ordered them to show his son nothing of the world’s course, nor to tell him anything that might make him sad—neither of sickness, death, old age, nor poverty—nor of other things that might grieve him and diminish his joy. Instead, they were to set before him all the joy, pleasure, happiness, and wealth of this world, so that he would neither think of nor learn about anything in the future that might happen to him. In addition to this, and most importantly, the King commanded with great earnestness that not the slightest thing should be said to him about the Christian faith, and that nothing of it should come to his ears.

When the King later learned that several monks were still present here and there in his kingdom—for he had believed they had all been driven out—he was moved to great anger and wrath. He sent his heralds throughout the entire kingdom and its cities to publicly proclaim that all monks still present must leave the realm within three days. If any were found after the appointed time, they were to be miserably executed by the sword and fire; "for these," he said, "are the ones who persuade the people to worship a crucified God."

Now, there was in this kingdom a prominent governor and counselor who was King Auenir's most beloved, trusted friend and innermost advisor. He was the highest official at court, a kind and gentle man, and zealous in the Christian faith. He took care for the salvation of his soul and was diligent in it, yet he did all this secretly before the King. However, several of the King's court servants, who also had access to the King,

### This spiritual man understands that the King spoke to him with a deceitful heart; therefore, he has his hair shorn and puts on a sackcloth, thus stilling the King's wrath: Likewise, how the King captures two monks during a hunt and has them burned.

### The Fourth Chapter.

When the King heard this speech and answer from his innermost counselor and beloved friend, he was greatly terrified; yet, he concealed his fury and anger as much as possible, and spoke no further with him. But the pious, zealous man, being a sensible, wise, and ingenious man, soon understood that the King had taken his words heavily and harshly, and that he had sought him out in a deceitful manner. He therefore went back to his house in great sadness and was heavily troubled, wondering by what means he might reconcile with the King and still his wrath, so that he might escape the impending danger. And as he lay awake the entire night, the poor injured man whom he had in his house, and who had presented himself as a <term>Word-Doctor</term> <note>original: "WortArtzet"; a healer of speech or one who uses eloquence to mend difficult situations</note>, came to his mind. He summoned him and spoke to him thus: "I recall that you said, 'I am a Word-Doctor and an advocate

for those who have spoken poorly.'" To this he answered: "Yes, I stand by it; and if you have need of me, I will let you see my art." Then the counselor told him from the beginning what great love and trust the King had for him, and also what a small, deceitful conversation the King had held with him, and how well and fittingly he had answered him—which the King had taken in the worst possible way. He could tell from the change in the King's face the anger and fury that he had internally conceived against him.

When the poor sick man heard this, he considered it for a little while and then said: "You should know, gracious Lord, that the King never had a suspicion that you desired his kingdom or were plotting for it; rather, he spoke these things to you to test you. Therefore, arise, shear your head <note>Cutting the hair was a traditional sign of penance or preparation for monastic life</note>, lay aside your costly garments, and clothe yourself in a <insert>garment of hair</insert> <note>original: "härenen Kleid"; a hairshirt or sackcloth used for mourning or religious devotion</note>, and in the early morning appear before the King. And when he asks you, 'What does this clothing signify?' give this answer: 'O King, regarding your speech yesterday, behold, I appear before you and am ready to follow the path you desire to go. Although the delights of this world are to be loved because they are full of joy, it does not befit me to enjoy them after you and without you. I know that the path of virtue which you wish to walk is rigorous and hard; but as long as I am with you, it shall be light, smooth, and pleasant to me. For since you have had me as a friend and companion in these present joys and benefits, so you shall also have me as a companion in sadness, so that I may also share with you and have the reward of future blessings.'"

# Of the Life and Conduct

When the noble and spiritual Lord had heard these words of the sick man <note>referring to the "Word-Doctor" mentioned on the previous page</note>, he followed his advice; he had his hair shorn and appeared before the King in the early morning, dressed in a **hair shirt** <note>a garment made of coarse animal hair, worn close to the skin as a sign of penance or mourning</note>. When the King saw him dressed in such a manner and heard all his words (as told above), he was greatly terrified, shocked, and amazed. He realized that this man, his most secret counselor, bore a true love and friendship toward him, and that those who had accused him had presented untruths and lies. Therefore, the King gained greater trust and friendship toward him and elevated him to a higher **dignity** <note>original: "Dignitet"; high rank or office</note>.

The King went out to hunt once again, and there he saw two monks walking through the wilderness. He immediately commanded that they be seized and brought before him. He looked upon them fiercely, and from his eyes, fiery rays of anger seemed to appear. He spoke to them: "O you vagabonds and deceivers! Did you not hear my henchmen proclaim that within three days, no one of your **superstition** <note>original: "Superstition"; here the King uses the word to insult the Christian faith as a false or irrational belief</note> and false faith should be found in the cities and lands under my power? Those who are found shall be miserably put to death by the sword and fire." To this, the monks answered: "Very well, we are following your command and are departing from your cities and kingdom. However, because we have a long journey to reach our brothers, we have purchased a small amount of food so that we might not faint or die of hunger on such a long road." To this, the King replied: "Those who fear death do not concern themselves with food or trade." Upon this, the monks answered the King:

<column-break/>

23
# of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.
D 2
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

"King, you speak correctly of this; for those who fear death are very concerned with what ways and means they might escape it. But who are these people other than those who are entirely devoted to these fragile things and hope to find nothing good in the future? In those whom temporal things are deeply rooted, they fear death. But we, who long ago hated the world and what is worldly, and for **Christ’s** sake lead this hard and strict life, are those who do not fear death, nor love these temporal things. Rather, we desire only what is to come. If death is inflicted upon us by you, it is to us a testimony of a better and eternal life; therefore, it is not terrifying to us, but sweet and lovely."

But the King wished to catch the monks in their own arguments and said to them: "Did you not say a moment ago that you were departing because I commanded it? What is that other than an empty boast? If you do not fear death, why have you taken to flight?" To this, the monks answered: "We do not flee because we fear the death you threaten us with, but for your sake—that you might have mercy on us, so that you do not fall into greater and heavier **damnation** <note>original: "Verdamnus"</note> on our account. For this reason, we decided to withdraw, and not at all out of fear of your threats." At this, the King became enraged and ordered them to be burned; thus, the servants of **Christ** obtained the **martyr’s crown** <note>original: "Marterkron"; a spiritual reward given to those who die for their faith</note> through fire. The King then issued a command throughout his entire land that wherever monks were found, they should be executed without any questioning. Thus, no more were found in these lands, except for those hidden in the rocks and caves.

## How the King’s son learned from his tutor the reason why his father kept him thus imprisoned, whereat he became saddened; and after he revealed the cause of his sorrow to his father, the father allowed him to ride out and go wherever he wished.

### The Fifth Chapter.

When the King’s son, of whom we spoke before, was held thus enclosed in his palace, and having completed his childhood youth, became a young man, well-instructed in all the customs, habits, and teachings of the **Moors and Persians** <note>historical terms for the peoples of North Africa and the Middle East, used here to set the legendary Eastern scene</note>—not only well-formed and handsome in spirit and body, but also understanding and wise in all things good, so that his tutors and **preceptors** <term>preceptor: a teacher or instructor, often a private tutor in a royal household</term> wondered at his wisdom—indeed, even his father was struck by the beauty, clarity, and loveliness of his face, and the constancy of his spirit. He therefore commanded those who were always around him that they should bring nothing before his son that might cause sadness regarding this earthly life, nor mention that the pleasures of this world might be cut off by death. He had, however, conceived a vain hope through this (and as they say, "shot arrows into the sky or the air" <note>an idiom for a futile or impossible effort</note>), for how can death remain hidden from the human race? This knowledge did not escape this youth either, especially because he was endowed with such

Yet he feared to ask his father about it, thinking it was not the thing to do, as he knew well that his father did nothing that was not for his benefit; besides, because of his father’s command, even if he did ask, his father would not tell him the truth. Therefore, he resolved to learn this from others, and took one of his **preceptors** <note>original Latin: "Præceptoribus"</note>, whom he loved more than the others, to a private place and asked him for what cause or reason his father kept him thus enclosed and locked away, and said further: "If you tell me this in truth and reveal it to me, I will love you above all others and establish a covenant of eternal love with you." Since the preceptor—who was also an understanding, skillful man and well recognized the youth’s cleverness and intellect—thought to himself that if he withheld it, it might result in disadvantage and danger for him in the future, he therefore began and told him everything word for word: what had occurred, and how the King had caused a proclamation to be made throughout the whole kingdom, issuing a command against the Christians, and especially against the **hermits** <term>hermit: (original "Einsidel") a person living in solitude as a religious discipline</term>, that they should be persecuted and hounded, and that they should be driven out and expelled from the kingdom within three days; and if one were found there and caught in his kingdom, he should be executed with fire and sword without mercy. He also gave orders that a good, glorious music of voices and instruments should always precede him, and that they should arrange all kinds of joys, dancing, leaping, and all kinds of beautiful games, so that he might delight his mind therewith.

While he was riding, one day he saw—without his servants knowing <note>The Prince likely rode ahead or looked where his guardians had not yet cleared the path.</note>—two men, one of whom was blind and the other leprous. When he saw them, he was horrified by them and said to those who were with him: "Who are these, and what does their hideous appearance mean?" The servants could no longer hide what had come before his sight and said to him: "This is human frailty, which flows from corrupt matter and from a bad physical temperament <term>complexion: a medical term referring to the balance of the four bodily humors</term> of man, and it occasionally happens to people." 

Whereupon the youth desired to know whether such a thing happened to all people. "No," they said, "not to all, but only to those who have a ruined nature and are burdened with many evil, superfluous vapors and fluids <term>humors: the bodily liquids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—once thought to dictate health</term>." At this, the youth said: "So can one recognize those who are subject to such frailty, or does it happen to them commonly and unexpectedly?" Thereupon the servants answered further: "Who among men is he who knows or recognizes future things? For such is beyond human nature and is inherited by the immortal gods alone." At this, the young king asked no further of these things he had seen, but was moved to compassion; he was horrified and turned pale at these unusual things.

After many days, the young king rode out for pleasure again; there he found a very old, aged man, his face all wrinkled and deformed, his head ice-gray or white as a dove, no teeth in his mouth, a lisp on his tongue, lame in all his limbs, and he went along with a bowed back. At this he was startled and marveled at him, and ordered him to be led closer to him, and asked concerning his strange appearance. Those who were with him answered: "This is a

<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: the like</meta>

quite old man of many years, and little by little the power of nature has decreased in him, and weakened and sickened his limbs; therefore he has fallen and come into this state as you see him." Then the youth asked: "But what is his end?" They answer: "Death will take him away." The young king asked further: "Does this happen to all people, or only to some?" They said: "Unless death takes one away earlier, it is impossible that a person does not come to this state." 

At this the youth asked: "In how many years does this misery come upon one? Or does death not come at all? Is there also no art available to escape death and to flee this wretched old age?" They answer: "In eighty or a hundred years, a person comes to this wretched old age, after which he dies; for it can be no other way, for death is the debt of nature <note>original: "Schuld der Natur"; the theological and philosophical idea that every living being must eventually "pay" for their life with death</note> which was imposed on man from the beginning; and nothing is more certain than its coming, and nothing more uncertain than the hour."

When the wise and understanding youth had seen and heard all this, he contemplated it within himself and sighed from the bottom of his heart over it, and spoke: "This life is a bitter life, full of all bitterness and pain; since it is so, how can one be safe from death, if no one can escape it, and its coming is as uncertain as you say?" Thus he rode home again and kept these things within himself, thinking of them incessantly, and kept death in his mind; for he said to himself: "When death once seizes me, who will be there who will think of me more after death, since in time everything is placed into oblivion?" He was constantly occupied with such thoughts, so that he nearly wasted away and turned pale from them. But in the presence of his father, whenever it happened that he visited him, he showed himself quite cheerful and merry, for he did not want his father to become aware of his thoughts; for the youth desired with an incomprehensible longing to find a man who could reassure his heart and pour healing words into his ears and heart.

Therefore, he called his <term>Tutor</term> <note>original: "Præceptorem"; a private teacher or instructor</note>, of whom mention was made above, to him and asked him if he perhaps knew or recognized a man who could help him in what he desired and satisfy his mind, which had become weary from many thoughts, for he was not strong enough to resist these burdens. The Tutor remembered their previous conversations and answered him: "I told you long ago how your father has had several of the wise men and <term>hermits</term> <note>original: "Einsidel"; religious recluses</note> who preached and disputed about such things put to death, and has persecuted and driven out the others; I do not know if any of them remain anywhere in the kingdom." When the youth heard this, he was filled with even greater dismay and sadness, like one who has lost a great and precious treasure, and he turned and set all his thoughts upon how he might find and obtain it again. While the good youth was thus contemplating how he might find rest for his soul, He who sees all things looked upon him and did not despise him; rather, He who wills that all people be saved and come to the knowledge of the truth—as He customarily shows His goodness to all—also showed this

### The Sixth Chapter.

At that same time, there was a monk well-versed in divine matters, adorned with a spiritual life and words, and well-experienced in the monastic life and rules. From where he was born, however, and from what lineage, I cannot say; but he had built himself a cell in the borders of the land of <term>Sanaar</term> <note>Shinar, a biblical region often associated with Mesopotamia or the East in medieval legends.</note> and was graced with priestly dignity. This man's name was Barlaam. To him, through divine revelation, all that was happening with and around the King's son became known. He left his cell and his hermit's life to go into the world; he changed his clothes, putting on worldly garments, boarded a ship, and sailed to the kingdom of India. He presented himself as a merchant and landed near the city where the King's son had his palace. He stayed there for many days and made diligent inquiry into the conduct and affairs of the King's young son, as well as his servants. When he learned that the Tutor (of whom mention was made above) was the one most accepted and beloved by the youth among all the others,

he approached him and addressed him with the following words: "Dear sir, you should know that I come as a merchant from distant, far-off lands, and I have brought with me such a precious stone <note>In this religious allegory, the 'precious stone' represents the Gospel or the Word of God.</note> that the likes of it can be found nowhere else, nor can its equal be invented. Until now, I have shown it to no man, nor let it be seen, but I reveal this to you because I see that you are a wise, sensible, and capable man. I ask you to lead me to the King’s son, so that I may let him see this stone and **present** <note>original: "præsentiere"</note> it to him. For in a wonderful way, it surpasses all other goods: it gives the light of wisdom to the blind heart, it opens the ears of the deaf, it brings speech to the mute, it gives health to the sick, and it grants wisdom and understanding to the foolish; it drives away the devils and bestows everything lovely and good upon those who possess it."

The **Preceptor** <note>A title for a royal tutor or teacher.</note> said to him: "I see that you are a man of steadfast and experienced skill, for your words make you worthy of your name. I have indeed seen many costly pearls and gemstones—so many that I cannot count them for you—but never have I seen anything with such virtues <note>The word 'Tugenten' (virtues) here refers to the miraculous powers or properties of the stone.</note> as you describe, nor have I even heard of such a thing. Therefore, I beg you to let me see this precious stone; and if it is as you praise it to be, I will immediately bring it to the King’s son, and you would in return receive great honor and gifts from him. There was once a powerful, mighty King who traveled in a royal carriage beautifully adorned with gold, accompanied by his courtly attendants in a stately manner. It so happened that he encountered two men dressed in torn, worn-out, and filthy clothes; they were quite gaunt and pale of face. However, the King quickly recognized them as men who had consumed and martyred their flesh through the chastening <note>original: "Casterung," referring to the ascetic practice of denying the body's desires</note> of the body and Christian discipline. As soon as he saw them, he leaped down from his carriage, fell to the ground and prayed, then stood up, embraced them, and kissed them heartily. The most noble and distinguished members of his court became indignant at this, believing he had acted against his royal dignity. Yet, they did not wish to rebuke him publicly or reproach him for it; instead, they informed his biological brother that he should speak to the King, so that he would not bring such shame upon the royal crown in the future. When the brother told this to the King and rebuked his unnecessary (as it seemed to him) humility, the King met him with an answer that he did not understand.

This King had a custom that whenever someone was judged and condemned to death, he would send his executioners with a trombone or a trumpet <term>Pusaunen <note>While literally "trombones," in this era the word referred to the trumpets of judgment mentioned in the Bible.</note></term> to the person’s house to sound a blast. By this blowing, everyone understood that the man had forfeited his life. As evening approached, the King sent the "Death-Blower" to his brother's house and had the trumpet sounded. When the brother heard such trumpets, he despaired for his life, and for the entire night—

—the entire night he set his affairs in order and made his will. Early in the morning, his wife and children, dressed in black robes of lamentation or mourning, came before the King’s palace wailing and weeping. The King summoned him inside, and seeing him in such a state of lamentation, said to him: "O you foolish and unwise man, did you so fear the executioners of your own brother, against whom (as you well know) you have committed no crime? Why then did you rebuke me for greeting and kissing in humility the messengers of my Lord and God? They announce to me, with the sound of trumpets, my own death and the terrifying coming of the Lord, against whom I know myself to have often and grievously sinned. Therefore, I undertook this path against you to punish your folly. But now, I will also make manifest to everyone the folly of those who instructed you to punish and reproach me." 

After he had thus instructed his brother, he sent him home again, but ordered that four little chests <note>This is the famous "Parable of the Caskets," a story about judging by appearances that later inspired the casket scene in Shakespeare's *Merchant of Venice*.</note> be made. He had two of them covered with pure gold, but placed foul-smelling dead men's bones inside, and then locked them with golden locks. The other two he had coated with pitch and tar, but filled them with precious gemstones and pearls, as well as fragrant ointments and spices, and bound them with haircloth. When the little chests were thus prepared, he summoned his detractors—the most noble and distinguished of his court—and set these small boxes before them, commanding that they should estimate what each was worth. Thereupon, they valued the two that were gilded as a great treasure, saying they were well worthy of holding the royal crown and jewels therein—

But I saw with the inner eyes of understanding their honorableness, and noted the inner adornment of their souls; therefore I was glorified <note>original: "glorificiert," meaning to be honored or exalted through an action</note> when I embraced them with my arms, for I valued them more dearly than any royal crown, ornament, or purple <note>Purple dye was extremely expensive in the ancient and medieval world, making it a universal symbol of royalty and high status.</note>." ### How Barlaam teaches the King’s son the true Christian Catholic faith.

#### The seventh Chapter.

The King’s son Josaphat said to Barlaam: "You have spoken rightly and well of these things; however, I would gladly know one thing from you: who is your Lord, of whom you spoke and testified at the beginning of your speech, and of His lineage?" 

To this Barlaam replied: "If you wish to know my Lord, then know that He is the Lord Jesus Christ, the only begotten Son of God, the only blessed and powerful, a King of kings, a Lord of all hearts, who alone has immortality and dwells in the everlasting light, and is to be honored with the Father and Holy Spirit. He is my Lord, for I am not among those who call upon many and ill-ordered gods and worship soul-less and ear-less idols; instead, I recognize and confess only one God, who is to be worshiped and honored in the Trinity—I say, the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—of one nature and substance, in equal honor and undivided kingdom, so that in three persons there is but one God without beginning and without end, eternal, unbegotten, unchangeable, who cannot be described, without any physical body, invisible, incomprehensible, who alone is righteous and good, who created all things visible and invisible out of nothing."

And indeed, in the beginning, He created and ordained invisible and innumerable heavenly powers and incorporeal spirits for the service of God’s great majesty; thereafter, He created this visible world, such as the heavens, the earth, the sea, and everything within them, which He adorned with His light: the heavens with the sun, moon, and stars; the earth with all kinds of plants and animals; and also the sea with all kinds of swimming fish and crawling creatures that live in the water. All this He commanded, and it was done; He spoke it, and they were created. He also formed man with His own hands from a clod of earth <note>The German "Erdenklotzen" literally means a lump or clod of soil, emphasizing the humble material of the human body compared to the divine soul.</note> into a body, but He shared with him understanding and a rational soul through a special breathing-in <note>The "breath of life" described in Genesis.</note>; which soul (as it is written) is created after the image and likeness of God—indeed, according to the image because of the intellect and free will, and according to the likeness because of virtue (as much as is possible for a human being). This man He blessed with free will and immortality, and set him as a king over the earth, and also made from his body a woman as a helper like unto him.

He also planted and ordained the Paradise of Delight toward the rising of the sun <note>The East, traditionally the location of the Garden of Eden in medieval cartography and theology.</note>, which was full of pleasure, joy, and delight; and therein He placed the man whom He had formed, and permitted him to make use of all the fruits of the divine plants and trees; only from one single tree did He forbid them to eat anything at all, which was called the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil, <margin>Genesis 2.</margin> and spoke thus to them: On whichever day you taste of

Therefore, he was cast out from his order and dignity, and instead of the blessed glory and angelic name, he is called the Devil and Satan; for God rejected him as one unworthy and useless for the eternal and highest glory and honor. A very great number of the order of angels who were subject to him were also driven out and cast down with him; these, out of their own free will, accepted and loved evil and abandoned the good. For this reason, they are called devils, deceivers, terrifying ones, desperate ones, and outcasts.

As soon as the Devil had renounced the good and accepted evil, he became envious and hateful toward man, seeing that man was exalted to such glory and honor from which he himself had been cast out. He thought diligently and cunningly how he might bring man down from such blessedness. He used the serpent as a tool of his deception, and through it spoke to the woman and persuaded her to eat of the fruit of the forbidden wood, with this consolation: that they would become like God. By this, the Devil took heart and exalted himself in this victory and triumph. And as mankind multiplied upon the earth, he suggested and showed them every way toward sin, evil, and vice. <margin>Genesis 7.</margin> Now, as mankind multiplied again and spread far abroad, they forgot the good deeds of God and fell into the former—indeed, even greater—terrible and abominable sins: For some people supposed and created the delusion for themselves that everything happened of its own accord and was governed without prior consideration, just as if there were no God or Lord. Others attributed it to luck, to <term>fate</term> <note>original Latin: *fato*</note>, or that it simply must happen and be so. Others made and imagined for themselves many gods, even honoring and calling upon those same evil and vice-ridden gods, and held them to be the authors and helpers of their vices and evil inclinations. They made images and forms of these, and set up such images as statues, locking those deaf and useless idols into their temples, worshipping them therein, and serving the creature before the Creator. Others, however, [worshipped] the Sun, Moon, and Stars

Others had images made for them of ugly, hideous men, and held those for gods and called upon them. Indeed, they held as gods male and female persons whom they themselves knew to be adulterers, manslayers, murderers, wrathful, envious, insane, killers of fathers and brothers, robbers, the crooked and lame, sorcerers, hags, the raging and the mad. Some of these passed away through natural death, others plunged into the water and were thought to have been transformed into animals, so that they practiced and accomplished terrifying and abominable things. From these terrifying idols, men took an example and cause to give themselves over to all impurity without any fear or worry; for at that time, darkness ruled our race, and not one was found who understood or sought God.

At that same time and age, one named Abraham was found, who had a pure, unstained conscience and soul, and who, through the contemplation of the creatures, recognized the Creator of the same: for when he considered

...and observed the heavens, the earth, and the sea, as well as the sun and moon, along with everything else; he marveled at the magnificent, well-ordered splendor <note>The original German uses "Ornat," usually meaning ceremonial regalia, here describing the "vestments" or beauty of the created world</note> and adornment. When he also saw the world and everything within it, he understood that such things were not made or maintained by themselves. He did not attribute the creation or the preservation of such splendor and ornament to the elements—the earth, the water—nor to the soulless idols, but through these things he recognized and understood the true God, seeing that He is the Creator and Originator of all these things. He accepted Him willingly and with a right understanding; therefore, God revealed Himself to him—not according to His substance (for it is impossible for human nature to see God in His essence) but through other means and appearances, as He knew best how to do, so that He might pour a more certain knowledge into his soul and mind, glorifying him and making him His intimate friend and servant. This man then left to his descendants, and to those born from him, the devotion of faith, teaching them to recognize the true God.

<margin>g.</margin> Therefore, God caused his seed to multiply and grow abundantly, calling them His chosen people. Afterward, He accompanied this people through the wilderness for forty whole years with excellent, magnificent miracles <note>original: "Miraculn"</note> and divine appearances, feeding them with bread from heaven. This Law contained within itself the form and goal of things to come, so that they might also renounce idols and all evil works, and honor the true God alone, clinging to Him through good works. In order that they might put such things into practice, He led them into a good land, which He had promised long ago to the patriarch Abraham and his seed. It would be far too long, however, if I wished to tell of all the magnificent things and grand miracles He showed them, which are innumerable; all of which was intended so that the human race might be kept from loathsome idolatry and evil conduct, and be restored to its ancient well-being. Nevertheless, self-will and death still ruled in man, and the Devil, through his tyranny, strove to cast everything into Hell.

When we had fallen into such distress and misery, God—who created us out of nothing—did not despise us; nor did He wish the work of His hands to perish utterly. Instead, by the will of God the Father, the only-begotten Son, the Word of God, who is in the bosom of the Father, with Him, and...

...the Holy Spirit, of one substance and eternity, who was in the beginning with God. God Himself, through an unfathomable and incomprehensible humility, descended to become His own servant; and as He is perfect God, He also willed to become perfect man. He was conceived of the Holy Spirit and the Ever-Virgin Mary, without any male seed or mingling, in her untouched virginal womb through the Holy Spirit. This miraculous conception and birth had been previously announced to her by an angel sent to her.

Through the Holy Spirit, without any male seed, the Son of God was conceived and took onto Himself both body and soul in the womb of the Virgin. He came forth with a rational, understanding soul in one Person, but in two natures: perfect God and perfect man. He also preserved the inviolate virginity of His mother <note>The text refers to the "Gebererin," or the God-bearer (Theotokos), a traditional title for Mary.</note> during and also after the birth, and became like us in all things and in our misery (sin excepted). He also took our weakness upon Himself and bore our burdens and frailty. For since death came into the world through sin, it was necessary that the one who wished to redeem humanity should be without any sin, and thus not be subject to death through sin.

For thirty years He walked among this people. He was baptized in the river Jordan by John, a holy man who surpassed all the prophets; at this baptism, a voice from His heavenly Father was heard from heaven: "This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased."

<column-break/>

<page-num>47</page-num>

The Holy Spirit also descended upon Him in the form of a dove. From that moment on, He began to perform great things and miracles: raising the dead, giving sight to the blind, and casting out devils. He made the mute and the deaf, as well as the weak and sick, healthy; He cleansed the lepers and renewed our perverted nature, both inwardly and outwardly. He built us up with good works, taught the way of virtue, turned us away from the path of destruction, and led us toward eternal life. For this reason, He chose twelve disciples, whom He also called Apostles, and commanded them to go out into the wide world to proclaim this heavenly fellowship that had appeared on earth.

On account of such glorious and wonderful divine deeds and signs, the high priests and the people among whom He walked were filled with a senseless, gruesome envy and hatred toward Him. Although He had shown them the aforementioned miracles, they forgot them all and remained ungrateful. They condemned Him to death and bribed one of His disciples with money to betray Him to them. They captured the Lord (who gives life to all) and delivered Him to the heathens <note>Referring here to the Roman authorities.</note>. He took all this upon Himself willingly, for He came for the purpose of suffering everything for our sake, so that He might set us free from suffering. They inflicted much martyrdom, all kinds of pain and evil upon Him, and finally hanged Him on the cross. All of this He endured in the nature of the flesh which He had taken from us; for He possessed two natures, the divine and the human. Therefore, the human nature suffered, for the divine is <term>impassible</term> <note>original: "vnleidentlich"; a theological term meaning incapable of suffering, pain, or change.</note> and un-

### The Eighth Chapter.

And once again he has spoken: "Have you not read what was said to you by God, when he speaks: 'I am the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob'? God is not a God of the dead, but of the living." For in the same way that one binds the weeds <note>original: "Vnkraut" - typically referring to the tares in the biblical parable</note> together and then burns them, so will it also happen at the end of the world: that God will send out his angels to gather the weeds of offense together—that is, those who have done evil—and throw them into the eternal hellish fire, where there shall be weeping, wailing, howling, and gnashing of teeth; but the righteous shall glisten and shine in their Father’s kingdom like the suns.

But as he had spoken only this, he added: "He who has ears to hear, let him hear." With these and many other words, the Lord explained the resurrection of our bodies, and also strengthened the words with deeds,

...of all people. But now Christ has risen from the dead and has become the firstfruits among those who sleep, since death came through one man and the resurrection of the dead comes through one man; and just as they all die in Adam, so shall they all be made alive in Christ. 

And a little thereafter he says: Then the trumpet <note>original: "Pusaunen" - in early modern German, this referred to the trombone or the "last trump" of the Bible</note> shall sound, and the dead shall rise incorruptible, and we shall be changed; for that which is corruptible must put on the incorruptible, and this mortality must put on immortality. Then shall be fulfilled the word that is written: Death is swallowed up in victory. Death, where is your sting? <note>original: "Stahel" - literally steel or a point, used here for the sting of death</note> Death, where is your victory? 

For the power of death will then be destroyed and brought to nothing, and shall work no more; instead, eternal indestructibility and immortality will be given to mankind, and the resurrection of the dead will certainly and undoubtedly follow. And of this I do not doubt at all, but believe it powerfully, for the glory of the righteous and the punishment of the unrighteous will occur on that terrifying <margin>2 Peter 3</margin> day of the coming of Christ. 

On that same day, the heavens will pass away with fire, and the elements will melt with flames; but as a saint says, we look forward according to his promise to a new heaven and a new earth. For there shall be the repayment of good and evil, and nothing shall remain unjudged. For the Lord testifies that one must give an account of all actions, words, and thoughts: Whoever gives even a drink of water to the least among these yours—indeed, even to one in the name of my disciples—the same shall not remain unrewarded.

And further he says: When however the Son of Man <margin>Matthew 25</margin> shall come in his glory, and all the holy angels with him, then he shall sit upon the throne of his glory, and all nations shall be gathered before him, and he shall separate them from one another, just as a shepherd separates the sheep from the goats. For I was hungry and you fed me; I was thirsty and you gave me drink; I was a guest <note>original: "Gast" - implying a stranger or traveler in need of hospitality</note> and you hosted me; I was naked and you clothed me; I was sick and you visited me; I was imprisoned and you came to me." 

Why did he say that? Because he accepts the mercy we show to the needy as if it were done to himself. And he says another thing: "Whoever confesses me before men, I will <margin>Luke 12</margin> also confess him before my heavenly Father who is in heaven."

Through this and much more, God has proven that the reward of the righteous is certain and firm; conversely, however, punishment is reserved for the others and the wicked. He also foretold this through magnificent parables and examples, as he then introduces a rich man who was clothed every day in silk and velvet and costly linen, and lived heartily and grandly, but yet was stingy and unmerciful toward the poor and needy. ...desired; he did not even grant him the crumbs that fell from his table. Now it happened that they both died; the poor man, who was full of sores, was carried into Abraham’s bosom <note>A traditional term for the place of comfort where the righteous dead await the resurrection.</note>, which is into eternal life, but the rich man was buried in hell, which is full of flames, pain, and torment. To him, Abraham also said: Remember that you received good things in your life, whereas this poor Lazarus received evil; now he is comforted, but you are tormented.

In another place, he compares the kingdom of heaven to a king who wished to hold a wedding for his son. By this, he intended to indicate the future joy and delight; because of their coarse understanding, he had to speak with all people—even the wisest of this world—through parables of things they were accustomed to. This was not because there are weddings and many tables in eternal glory, but solely that he might make known and reveal the future state to them. For, he says, this king invited everyone to this wedding and had them called through heartfelt preaching, so that they should come together and be filled with these unspeakable goods.

<margin>Matthew 22</margin> But many of those who were invited despised the invitation and the meal and did not appear; for one went to his village, another to his trade, and the third had taken a wife, and thus they robbed themselves of the glory of the wedding. When these had willingly withdrawn from this lovely joy, others were called, and the wedding was filled with guests. Then the king went in to see the guests sitting at the table, and he saw there a person who did not have a wedding garment on, and he said to him:

But those who do not have the wedding garment on are indeed believers, yet they have soiled the garment of their understanding with all kinds of impurity and filth; therefore they are rightly excluded from the wedding joy.

<margin>Matthew 25</margin> He gave them yet another parable or comparison of ten virgins, five of whom were wise and five foolish. They took their lamps and went out to meet the bridegroom and the bride. Now the foolish took their lamps but took no oil with them; but the wise took oil in their vessels along with their lamps. By the oil is understood the possession of good works. At midnight a cry was made: Behold, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him! (By the midnight is understood the uncertainty of the Day of God). Then all the virgins stood up and trimmed their lamps, but the foolish said to the wise: Give us some of your oil, for our lamps are going out. Then the wise answered and said: Lest there perhaps be not enough for us and you, go rather to those who sell the oil and buy it for yourselves. And as they went to buy, the bridegroom came, and those who were ready went in with him to the wedding, and the door was shut.

...Finally, the other virgins also came and said: Lord, Lord, open the door to us. But he answered and said: Truly I say to you, I do not know you. From all of this, it now clearly appears that punishment will follow evil works, words, and thoughts. For the <margin>Matthew 12</margin> Redeemer says: I tell you that for every idle word that men have spoken, they must give an account on the Day of Judgment. And <margin>Matthew 10</margin> again he says: Even the hairs on your head are all numbered; by the hair, he wishes us to understand even the very smallest and least thoughts and inclinations. Not unlike this, Saint Paul teaches us, <margin>Hebrews 4</margin> saying: The Word of God is living and powerful, and sharper than any two-edged sword, and pierces until it divides soul and spirit, and also the joints and marrow, and is a judge of the thoughts and intentions of the heart; and no creature is invisible before Him, but all things are naked and open before His eyes, to whom we must give an account. <note>original: "in dem wir zu reden haben" — a phrasing of the Latin "ad quem nobis sermo," meaning the one to whom we are accountable.</note>

## Chapter 10.

When Josaphat heard these things, he was deeply struck and troubled in his mind, and he went into himself; and overflowing with tears, he said to the old man: You have revealed and made all things known to me freely, and have spoken most elegantly of this wonderful narrative. Since these things lie before us, what shall I do so that I may escape the punishment for the sins I have committed, and become worthy to enjoy the joy of the righteous?

Whereupon Barlaam gave him this answer: We <margin>Acts 2</margin> read in the Holy Scripture, when Peter, who was called a Prince of the Apostles, taught the people, they were moved to the heart with repentance and spoke to Peter and to the other Apostles: Men and brothers, what shall we do? # Regarding the Life and Conduct

...that he has poured out his mercy abundantly upon you and called you from afar—while you were still far from him in your will, serving silent devils and senseless gods <note>The author refers here to "idols," which were often described in medieval literature as "dumb" or "silent" because they could not answer prayers.</note>. Therefore, go before all things to the one who has called you, so that you shall receive from him the true knowledge of visible and invisible things. But if you would not come after being called, or should postpone it, you would be disinherited by the righteous judgment of God. Thus the Apostle Peter also said to one of the disciples <note>Likely an apocryphal reference or a general summary of the apostolic call to "take up one's cross."</note>, even though he ignored the calling—and he heard it even more clearly—that you must take your cross upon you and follow the ruling God, who calls you from death to life and from darkness to the light. For ignorance of God is the true darkness and the death of the soul; and to serve idols is the greatest folly.

To whom, or to what, should I compare these indiscreet people and truly represent them to you? I hardly know. Nevertheless, I will set an example before your eyes, which a learned and wise man told me, speaking thus: Idolaters and those who honor them are like those who go about with hunting and bird-catching. For once, one of them caught a nightingale, and as he wanted to kill it and prepare it for a meal, the nightingale began to speak and said: "O man, what use is my death to you? You cannot even satisfy your belly with me. But if you let me go free again, I will give you three lessons, from which you will receive and create much benefit your whole life long." He, however, was terrified by this speech from the bird and promised her that if he heard something new from

<column-break/>

## The Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

her, he would let her go at once. The nightingale turned to the bird-catcher <note>Original: "Vogler"; a fowler or someone who catches birds.</note> and gave him these three lessons or commandments as an example. The first: You should never again trouble yourself, nor labor, to grasp the things that cannot be grasped. The second: Do not grieve or fret, nor be too anxious, over a lost thing that cannot be recovered. The third: You should not believe a speech or word that is unbelievable. Keep these three commandments, and it will go well with you. The man marveled very greatly at these high and sensible lessons and speeches, and he immediately let the nightingale go free.

When the little bird had flown away, she wanted to test and inquire whether this man had rightly understood her teaching and what benefit he had drawn from it. While flying, she spoke to him: "Woe to you, you unwise and foolish man! What a foolish and unwise mind you have had; for behold, I have a fine little pearl <note>Original: "Berlin," a diminutive of "Perle" (pearl).</note> as large as an ostrich egg within me." When the man heard this, he became very distressed, and he was sorry that he had let the nightingale fly out of his hands. He labored to see if he could catch the nightingale again and said to her: "Come to me in my house, and I will show you all friendship and honor, and let you fly away again afterward." The nightingale answered him and said: "Now I truly recognize that you are a great fool. For you heard the words that I told you gladly and received them willingly, but you have drawn no fruit from them. For I told you that you should not grieve or trouble yourself too much over a lost thing that can no longer be recovered, and that you should not set out to desire the things that you...

...you cannot comprehend and obtain; and though you have labored to set me free, you then tried to see if you could catch me again, even though you can neither walk nor fly the path that I fly. After that, I also told you that you should not give belief to an unbelievable word; but behold, you believed that within me there was a fine pearl <note>original: "Berlin," an archaic diminutive of "Perle" (pearl)</note> that was larger than my body, and did not understand that in my entire substance I am not as large as an ostrich egg; how then can I have such a large pearl within me? So also are those unwise and foolish who place their hope in idols and false gods, which they themselves have made and formed with their own hands and fingers, and say: "These are our gods, who created and made us." And how can they even set their thoughts to the idea that their idols, which they made and formed with their own hands, are their creators? They even lock their idols in a chest <note>original: "Behaltnus," meaning a container or strongbox</note> so that they are not stolen by thieves, and call them protectors and guardians of their souls, and of their salvation and welfare. But is it not a folly not to recognize that those who cannot help themselves are hardly sufficient to provide the same for others? They exhaust their treasures so that they may erect images and idols to the devils, and they consider those to be the givers of goods which they do not possess nor have ever possessed; therefore it is written: *They shall become like those who do such things.* <note>A reference to Psalm 115:8, suggesting that those who make idols become as spiritually "dead" as the objects they worship.</note> They also hire artists and craftsmen to make such things for them, and they fall down and worship them; they even take them upon their shoulders and carry them about; but when they set them down in a place, they remain there immovable, and at whatever time they might call to them, they will not be heard by them, nor [delivered] from their—

---
