—distress; they therefore involve themselves in all manner of disorder, saying to the carved and cast images and idols: "You are our gods." They also offer sacrifice to new idols and fresh devils that have only just arrived, and not to God; which is a cursed, evil generation in which there is no faith.

Out of this evil and unfaithful generation, God has summoned you and spoken: "Go out from their midst, and separate yourself from them; touch nothing unclean, for you shall be preserved out of this generation." Look up and depart from them, for with them there is no sense; with them are many disorderly, riotous, and discordant gods. But with us, there are not many gods and lords, but only one God and Father, in whom all things are, and we in him; and one Lord Jesus Christ, through whom all things were created, as were we. Indeed, He is the invisible image of God, the firstborn of all creatures and of all the world; for in Him all things are created that are in heaven and on earth, visible and invisible—whether they be Thrones, Dominions, or Principalities, or Powers—all this is through Him, and without Him was nothing made. And one Holy Spirit, in whom all is one Lord and God who makes all things living and divine; a good Spirit, a righteous Spirit, a Spirit of comfort, a Spirit of the adoption of children. Of these three Persons, each one is true God, and yet they are not three gods; just as the Father is, so also is the Son and the Holy Spirit. In these three Persons is one nature, one kingdom, one virtue, one glory, and one substance; there is only the distinction of the Persons, but they are one in the Godhead: a Father, whose characteristic is to be unbegotten; an only-begotten Son, whose charact—

# Of the Life and Conduct

...creation is the birth, and a Holy Spirit, whose attribute is proceeding forth. <note>Barlaam is explaining the complex theology of the Trinity: the Son is "begotten" or "born" of the Father, and the Spirit "proceeds" from them.</note> And thus we are enlightened through the light of the Father by the Son with his light, and in that light we are made alive through the Holy Spirit: and we honor in these three persons only one Godhead, for he is recognized in the Trinity as only one God; for from him, through him, and in him is everything. Through his grace I have a knowledge of you and have been sent so that I should teach you that which I have learned and kept until this my old age; so far as you believe and are baptized, you will be saved; but if you do not believe, you are damned. For the things that you see today and in which you rejoice and exult—such as temporal glory and honor, wealth, the pleasures of the world and its seductions, and of life—all these things pass away and throw you out of your kingdom even if you do not want it. Nothing remains for your body but a small grave; you will be left there and shut in alone, abandoned by all your blood-relatives and good companions. Also taken from you and cast away will be all the pleasures of this world; and in their place, your body will suffer a stinking and foul-smelling decay instead of the glory and honor you possessed just now. And the devils will lead your soul into the eternal damnation of Hell until the Last Day of the Resurrection, when your body and soul will come together and the righteous Judge will damn body and soul together, etc.

But if you do not despise or neglect him who called you, but run to him with a willing mind, joy, and great desire, so that you might be

<margin>Psalm 4</margin>

<column-break/>

## the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

...enlightened by his light, follow him with all your heart and no longer turn back, casting all worldly things from you and despising them, and clinging to him alone—then hear what joy and comfort you shall draw from it. Sitting, you will be safe; when you sleep, you will have a lovely, gentle sleep. You will not fear the nightly terrors and the mighty disturbances of the devils; then you will step forth like a steadfast lion and live in joy and eternal exultation. For your head will be surrounded with exultation, praise, honor, and joy, and all pain, sadness, and sighing will depart from you. Then your light will break forth, and health will soon appear; your righteousness will also go before your face, and the glory and honor of the Lord will gather you. Then call upon him, and he will hear you; cry to him, and he will say: "Behold, here I am." For I am he who has a sorrow because of your unrighteousness and sins, which I will no longer remember. But you, be mindful of the judgment; recount your wickednesses so that you may be justified. For though your sins were scarlet-colored, they shall become as white as snow; and though they were as red as a <term>little worm</term><note>original: "Würmlein." This refers to the kermes insect, from which a deep crimson or scarlet dye was historically derived.</note>, they shall become as white as wool; the mouth of the Lord has spoken this.

# After receiving baptism, one must perform the works of faith and those things necessary for repentance.

### The 11th Chapter.

At this, Josaphat said to Barlaam: All your words are good and wonderful, and I have believed and do believe them; for my whole mind has always shown itself doubtful and reluctant toward the service and worship of idols and false gods <note>The German "Abgötter" literally means "off-gods" or false deities.</note> even before you came to me. But now I hate the idols with a perfect hatred; and because I have learned from you the vanity and folly of those who serve and honor them, I desire from now on to be a servant of Christ. May he not reject me, unworthy as I am, because of my misdeeds, nor turn his merciful face from me; yet I have a confidence and hope that he will forgive me all my sins, for he is kind and very merciful (as you have taught me). I am also now ready to receive holy baptism, and to do and keep all that you command me, and whatever else I should do after receiving holy baptism. But whether it is enough that I be baptized and believe, I wish for you to make me understand, and to make it known and revealed to me.

To this, Barlaam said to him: Mark what you should do after receiving baptism: you must abstain from all sin and vice, and you must build the works of virtue upon the true faith; for faith without the works of virtue is dead, just as good <margin>Galatians 5</margin> works without faith are dead; for the Apostle says: If

<column-break/>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>
<meta>catchword: our</meta>

you are led by the Spirit, then you are not under the law. Now the works of the flesh are obvious, which are: fornication, impurity, immodesty, unchastity, the honor of idols, sorcery, enmity, strife, envy, anger, quarreling, discord, sects, hatred, murder, drunkenness, gluttony, and the like. Of these I tell you, and have told you before, that those who do such things will not attain the Kingdom of God. But the fruit of the Spirit is love, joy, peace, patience, generosity, goodness, long-suffering, gentleness, faith, moderation, abstinence <note>Original: "Abbruch," referring to the act of "breaking off" from worldly desires or fasting.</note>, chastity, and the like. These are the rungs and the assembled ladder <note>This likely refers to the "Ladder of Divine Ascent," a popular medieval metaphor for spiritual progress.</note> that increase daily and lead the soul to heaven. Take note: these things we command you to hold fast after baptism, and to shun and abstain from their opposites.

But if, after receiving baptism and the recognized truth, we wish to take up the old dead works again and turn backward, like a dog to his vomit <note>Original: "Vmbdewung." A reference to Proverbs 26:11 and 2 Peter 2:22 regarding those who return to their old sins.</note>, then that which Christ says in the Gospel will happen to us: When the unclean <margin>Luke 11</margin> spirit departs from a man, he wanders through dry places seeking rest; and when he finds none, he says: "I will return to my house from which I came." And when he comes, he finds it swept with a broom and decorated. Then he goes and takes seven other spirits with him, who are more evil than he himself; and when they enter, they dwell there; and the final state of that man becomes worse than the first. And although baptism covers and completely removes and extinguishes the marks <note>Original: "Merckzeichen," likely referring to the stains of original and previous sins.</note> through the water, and henceforth is a strong wall and tower and a sharp arrow against us— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence, likely continuing to describe the protection baptism provides against the enemy.</note>

<page-num>76</page-num>

is to fight our enemy, yet it <note>Referring to the struggle of the soul</note> does not at all take away or abolish free will; just as the sin committed after baptism is not forgiven a second time <note>Early Christian doctrine often emphasized that while baptism washed away all prior sins, subsequent serious sins required a difficult process of public penance rather than a second baptism</note>, for we confess only one baptism. Therefore, we should guard ourselves with great diligence, so that we do not fall into new sin and filth, but rather that we grasp the command of God; for he said to his Apostles: Go forth and teach all nations, and baptize them in the Name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. But do not let it stop there, but add to it: And teach them to keep all that I have commanded you, etc. For he has also commanded that we be poor in spirit, whom he considers blessed, and he mentions that the Kingdom of Heaven is their seat: he also adds that we should mourn in this life, so that we may be worthy to be comforted in the future; we should also be gentle, and constantly have a hunger and thirst for righteousness; we should also be merciful, and show mercy to our neighbor, being of a pure heart and keeping ourselves pure from all fleshly uncleanness; peaceful with our neighbor, we should subject evil to good, and decide an offensive war or quarrel between them with just judgment and bring them to peace; we should also endure and patiently suffer all persecution, tribulation, and shame inflicted upon us for the sake of righteousness and for the sake of his Name, so that we may be worthy to behold the eternal joy and his manifest presence. He has also commanded that in this world our light should shine before people in such a way that they see our good works, so that they might glorify and honor our Father who is in heaven, etc.

In the Law of Moses, which was given to the Israelites in former times, it was said: You shall not kill; you shall not commit adultery, nor practice unchastity; you shall not steal, and give no false testimony. But Christ says: Every one who says *Raca* <note>original: "Racha"; an Aramaic term of contempt found in the Gospel of Matthew, meaning "empty-headed" or "worthless person."</note> over his brother shall be guilty of the council; but whoever says "you fool" shall be guilty of hellfire. Therefore, when you offer your gift upon the altar and there remember that your brother has something against you, leave your gift there before the altar and go first and reconcile yourself with your brother, and then come and offer your gift. Whoever looks at a woman to desire her has already committed adultery with her in his heart. Furthermore, you have heard that it was said to the ancients: you shall not swear falsely, but shall keep your oath to God; but I say to you that you shall not swear at all. You have heard that it was said: an eye for an eye, and a tooth for a tooth; but I say to you that you should not resist evil, but if someone strikes you on the right cheek, offer him the other also; and if someone wants to sue or quarrel with you in court and take your coat, let him also have your cloak; and if someone forces you one mile, go with him another two. But whoever asks something from you, give it to him, and do not turn away from him who wants to borrow from you, etc.

You have heard that it was said: you shall love your neighbor and hate your enemy; but I say to you: love your enemies, do good to those who hate you, and pray for those who persecute and offend you, so that you may be children of your Father who is in heaven, who lets his sun rise over the good and over the evil, and lets it rain over the righteous, and

the Savior <note>original: "Seligmacher"</note> commanded his Apostles, that they should teach it to the believers; all these things we must faithfully keep and follow, if we wish to attain perfection and deserve the incorruptible crown of righteousness, which the Lord, the righteous Judge, will give on that day to those who love Him, etc.

But Josaphat said all this: "As far as I can perceive and understand from your heartfelt speech and teaching, if I, after my innocence received in baptism, should fall again into sin—even through the transgression of only one or two commandments—would I then lose all my good will, and would all good hope be in vain?"

To this Barlaam answered him: "You should not think of these things in such a way, nor take them upon yourself; for for the sake of our salvation, God the eternal Word became flesh, who then fully knew our human frailty and weakness and had experience of it; therefore He did not leave us without medicine, but like a wise physician <note>original: "Artzet"</note>, He ordained for our weakness the Sacrament of Penance <note>original: "Sacrament der Buß"</note> and proclaimed or preached it for the forgiveness of sins. According to this, once the truth is known and one is washed clean of the impurity of sins through water, should it happen that a person later falls into certain sins, these are no longer extinguished through water and the Holy Spirit <note>A reference to the doctrine that baptism cannot be repeated</note>, but must be repented through a strict penance, the shedding of many tears and lamentation, and also through much sweat, toil, and labor; and the guilt of the same be forgiven through the bottomless mercy of God, just as He then saves many from many kinds of sins; for no hour surpasses the goodness of God, if we only [seek] the same..."

believers <note>The text begins mid-word ("bigen"), likely completing "Gläubigen" (believers) from the previous page.</note> come to help with repentance and tears before death overtakes us and seizes us in sin; for in Hell there is no more repentance nor confession. But while we live and persist steadfastly in the foundation of the right faith—even if a moment of madness or foolishness happens to us and we fall back into the mire—such things can be made new again through repentance. For it is impossible to recount the greatness of His compassion or the abundance of His mercy; though the sins be as many and as great as they will, and even if they were innumerable, the overflowing mercy of God surpasses and overwhelms them.

Therefore, we should not despair or lose hope in the multitude of our sins, because through the goodness of Christ, forgiveness for them is given to us, He who shed His blood. For everywhere in the Scripture we learn of the virtue of repentance, and especially from the recounted words and parables of our Lord Jesus Christ; for thus the Scripture says: Jesus began to teach and spoke: Repent, for the Kingdom of Heaven is at hand; and He specifically pointed to this parable:

# Of Life and Conduct

[gain] of every capacity; therefore I would gladly find a way that I might diligently keep the commandments of God and no longer deviate from them, so that I do not again provoke my God and Lord to anger after having received **indulgence** <note>In this context, the remission of temporal punishment for sins through the Church’s authority.</note> and **penance**.

Upon this Barlaam said: O Lord and King, you speak rightly and well of it, for this is also my wish and desire. But it is very dangerous and impossible to sit by the fire and not be blackened by smoke: so it is also no less dangerous and difficult to be burdened with worldly business, to throw oneself into it, to deal with it, to live in pleasures, and yet to walk irreproachably in the way of God and preserve oneself from sin, for the Lord says:

<margin>Matthew 6.</margin>
No one can serve two masters, for either he will hate the one and love the other, or he will hold to the one as good and despise the other; you cannot serve God and **Mammon** <note>A term used in the New Testament to describe material wealth or greed, often personified as a false god.</note>. His beloved disciple and Evangelist, also the Theologian John, writes:

<margin>1 John 2.</margin>
Love not the world, nor what is in the world; if anyone loves the world, the love of the Father is not in him, for all that is in the world is either the lust of the flesh, or the lust of the eyes, or the pride of life, which is not of the Father but of the world: and the world passes away with its lusts, but whoever does the will of God remains for eternity, etc.

When our godly ancestors heard from the Apostle that one must enter the kingdom of heaven through much tribulation, they applied themselves and worked so that they might keep the garment which they received through baptism unstained by the defilement of sin; therefore some of them not only re-

<column-break/>

### The Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

ceived the baptism of water, but the baptism of the shedding of their holy blood; for this is also called a baptism, which is very precious and laudable, and cannot be stained a second time by the filth of sin. The Lord also accepted this baptism and worthily called it a baptism. Its followers were first the Apostles and twelve disciples of the Lord, and thereafter the entire choir of holy martyrs, who set themselves against the idol-worshipers and image-servers, standing before kings, tyrants, and judges for the sake of the name of Christ. For this reason, they suffered and endured many kinds of pain and martyrdom, for they were thrown to wild beasts to be torn apart and eaten, burned with fire, and judged with the sword. These had the true knowledge, finished their good race, kept the faith, and earned the reward of righteousness; therefore they are joined with the angels and have become co-heirs with Christ. Their virtues shone in such a way that their fame resounded throughout the entire earth, and their speech was spread throughout the whole wide world. Not only these words and works, but also their blood, flesh, and bones are full of all holiness, for they powerfully drive the devils out of people and make the sick whole who flee to them in right faith and hope; whenever people come upon their clothing or anything else precious belonging to them, they should always honor it, etc. To recount all their virtues would be far too long.

<meta>This page continues the speech of Barlaam to Prince Josaphat. Barlaam is explaining how, after the age of physical persecution ended, devout Christians sought a "bloodless martyrdom" through the ascetic life of the desert fathers.</meta>

# Of Life and Conduct

...ceased, and when believing kings ruled throughout the whole world, some of the kind-hearted and zealous Christians—in order to keep their conscience and innocence pure from sin, because they well knew that they would not accomplish or maintain such things in the world—found another way to make themselves like the **martyrs** <note>Refers to the "desert fathers" who practiced extreme self-denial as a form of spiritual struggle.</note>: some lived in the wilderness and deserts in the manner of pilgrims; and according to the voice of Christ, who says, "If you would be perfect, go and sell all that you have and give it to the poor," they left the world, father, mother, blood-relatives, and other friends, suffering great want of food and drink, as well as other bodily necessities; of whom the world was not worthy to have them dwelling within it. Others supported themselves under the open sky in all kinds of heat, cold, rain, and storms, being poorly clothed. Others lived in huts, caves, and clefts of the rocks and mountains, and there suffered want and **chastened** <term>to discipline the body, often through fasting or discomfort, to strengthen the spirit</term> their bodies, thus gathering one virtue upon another and renouncing all bodily comfort and rest. They ate only wild, rough herbs, or at times a piece of hard bread only once a day; they also contented themselves with poor clothing and practiced these two things so that they might flee the causes of sin and tear out their desires by the roots, cores, and stems from their hearts, so that they might plant divine and heavenly things within themselves. Furthermore, they also tormented their flesh through deprivation and willingly made themselves into martyrs, so that they might not be deprived of the glory which those obtained through the shedding of their blood, being followers of the suffering of Christ as much as lay within them,

---

# the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

and might be partakers of the light of the eternal kingdom. Now that they had attained these virtues, they led a solitary, quiet life, thus piling up and increasing virtue upon virtue, so that they renounced all worldly comfort and **consolation** <note>original: "Consolation" — here meaning physical or worldly relief.</note>.

They also let themselves be satisfied with little and meager food, some with only a piece of hard, dry bread, and of that very little and meager, and they ate and took it only for the maintenance of life. Some among them fasted the entire week and took food only on Sundays; others ate twice in the week; others always tried and bit into a little something in the evening every other day. They were devoted to prayer and vigils, waiting [upon God], and followed the **angelic life** <note>A traditional term for the monastic life, as monks were thought to live like angels, neither marrying nor seeking earthly wealth.</note>. Gold, silver, and the possession of goods, worldly buying and selling, they completely renounced, and they forgot that they were among people. But envy and pride, which commonly accompany good works, were not among them at all. For even if one among them was perhaps less advanced in his **conduct** <note>original: "Conuersation" — referring to one's manner of living and social interaction.</note> and the other far surpassed him, he was not envious of him for that reason. Conversely, he who was more excellent than the other did not despise him or, through pride, disdain his neighbor, nor did he glory or puff himself up in his holiness, nor boast that he surpassed the others in virtues; for he who was excellent in virtues did not ascribe it to his own toil and labor, but to the **grace of God** <note>original: "Gnad Gottes"</note>, depressing himself in **humility** <note>original: "Demut"</note>, regarding himself and his works as nothing, according to the saying of the Lord: When we...

# Of Life and Conduct /

<margin>Luke 17</margin>
**when we have done all that was commanded of us, we should say: we are unworthy servants; we have only done what we were duty-bound to do.** <note>A reference to Luke 17:10, emphasizing that even the most rigorous asceticism is merely a basic duty to God.</note> But these people have persuaded themselves that they have not even performed that which was commanded, but rather thought they lacked a great deal of what should have been done. They were thus humble within themselves and toward one another. As for ambition—or the vice of seeking to please men—how was it within them? They fled the world for that very reason, went into the desert, and lived there so that their good works would not be revealed to men, but to God alone, from whom they then awaited their good reward. For they knew well that good works performed for the sake of vain glory remain without reward, since they are not done for God's sake, but for the praise of men. From this it follows that such people suffer a double harm: the body is **chastened** <term>to discipline the body through fasting or labor to strengthen the spirit</term> and emaciated, yet they receive no reward for it. But these holy ones strive after eternal glory and splendor, and desire it from the bottom of their hearts; therefore, they despise and reject all human praise.

One group has their dwellings in the outermost and furthest wildernesses, completely removing themselves from human company for all the days of their lives, so that they might be all the closer to God. Another group has their cells and small huts very far from one another, yet they come together in a church on Sundays and receive the **Divine Mysteries** <term>the Eucharist or Holy Communion</term> and secrets—the sacrifice, I say, of the unblemished body and blood of Christ for the forgiveness of the sins of the faithful, and for the enlightenment and sanctification of the soul and body which the Lord has given us. They also instructed one another with good teaching and exercises, as well as divine conversation and good exhorta-

-tions, revealing to one another their secret struggles and wars <note>Refers to "spiritual warfare" or the internal struggle against temptation.</note> so that they might be released and kept from them, and flee from the sorrow of which they otherwise knew nothing. Then each one returned home again, diligently locking the honey of virtue into his heart, and through this they produced a sweet fruit worthy of the heavenly host. Others, however, have gathered their lives together in cloisters and convents; they live separated from others under an abbot or prelate, and have submitted their will to the sword of obedience, and have subjected themselves as purchased servants. They do not live for themselves, but for him to whom they have submitted and made themselves subject for Christ's sake; or to put it better, they do not live for themselves, but Christ lives in them, whom they have followed, having forsaken all. And this is the renunciation of the world: a willing **rigor** <note>Original: "Neid," which in this context means a harsh zeal or severity directed at one's own fleshly desires.</note> against oneself, and a denial or rejection of one's own nature for the sake of the desire for supernatural things. These live on earth like the angels, who praise God with psalms and hymns in unison, and shine with the work and obedience of the **Confessors** <term>those who "confess" their faith through their way of life and suffering, though not necessarily through physical martyrdom</term>, in whom the word of the Lord is fulfilled, where He says: <margin>Luke 17</margin> **Where two or three are gathered in my name, there am I in the midst of them.** <note>Though the margin cites Luke 17, this is a well-known passage from Matthew 18:20.</note> This number (two or three) does not, however, limit the specific number of those who should be gathered in the name of God; rather, He intends thereby to mean any unspecified number. For whether there are many or few gathered in His holy name who serve Him with fervent desire, we firmly believe that He is with or in the midst of His servants.

# Of the Life and Conduct /

...actions and deeds, their earthly life followed the heavenly one: through fasting, prayer, vigils, hot tears, an unyielding sorrow <note>Original: *unversöhnlichen Leyd*, referring to a state of constant, deep repentance for one's sins</note>, a pilgrimage toward death, in gentleness and goodness, in silence and the taming of the lips, in poverty and infirmity, in chastity and wisdom, in a humble and tranquil mind, and in perfect love toward God and neighbor. Those who lead such a life shall become like the angels; therefore, God has adorned them with wonders and every kind of virtue, and has spread their miraculous way of life throughout the whole world. And now, were I to tell you the life of but a single one who was the founder of the monastic way and life—as it is said—whose name was **Anthony** <note>Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356 AD), an Egyptian monk hailed as the "Father of All Monks."</note>, you would recognize from this single tree what lovely and sweet fruit the other trees of the same kind and character produce. You would see what a foundation this **hermit** <term>original: *Einsidel*, a person living in religious solitude</term> laid, and what a structure he built upon it; likewise, how many graces he received from our Savior, whom many have followed in the struggle, and have thus earned and received the same crown and reward.

Blessed are they—yea, thrice blessed are they—who have loved God and, for the sake of His love, have forsaken and despised all things. They have lived in weeping and mourning day and night, so that in the life to come they might possess eternal consolation. They have willingly humbled themselves so that they might be exalted there; they have tormented their flesh with hunger, thirst, and vigils, so that they might behold the delight of sweetness and the joy of Paradise. They have become a temple and dwelling-place of the Holy Spirit through the purity of their hearts, as

then it is written: *I will dwell in them and walk among them* <note>A citation of 2 Corinthians 6:16.</note>; for they have crucified themselves to the world so that, on the Day of Judgment, they might stand at the right hand of the Crucified. They have girded their loins with virtues and kept their lamps ever ready, longing for the coming of the Immortal One, whom they beheld with their inner eyes. They have constantly contemplated that fearsome hour <note>The hour of death or the Last Judgment.</note>, for the meditation of things to come was always in their hearts, that they might obtain eternal glory. They have become as impassive as the angels and now rejoice with those whose lives they imitated. Blessed are they—yea, thrice blessed—who with fixed eyes have seen the vanity of present things and the unstable and uncertain prosperity of man; they have despised these things and cast them away. Instead, they have planted within themselves eternal goods and have chosen and won a life that does not die, nor can ever again be harmed by death.

These wonderful and holy men we also desire to follow, though we are weak and cast-off; not that we are able to reach even the slightest height of heavenly **conversation** <term>original: *Conuersation*, here meaning "way of life" or "citizenship," from the Latin *conversatio*</term> and fellowship with them, but rather, as much as our weak nature and wretchedness allows, we set their life and virtue before us as a model. We are covered with their habit <note>Original: *Tracht*, referring to the distinctive clothing worn by monks as a sign of their vocation.</note>, even if we do not fully live up to it; for this Divine promise draws us away from the will to sin and binds us to **incorruption** <term>the state of being free from spiritual decay or mortality</term>, which was given to us in the **mystery** <term>original: *Mysterio*, referring to a religious sacrament</term> of holy baptism. Therefore, following the speeches and words of these blessed ones, we have a terrible loathing for the destructibility of this mortal life,

# Of the Life and Conduct /

<margin>Ecclesiastes 1</margin> <note>A reference to the biblical book of Ecclesiastes, which famously states "Vanity of vanities; all is vanity."</note> ...in which nothing constant or enduring is to be found; for all is vanity and vexation of the spirit, bringing many changes in the blink of an eye. It is more contemptible than dreams and shadows, or even the dust that is tossed to and fro by the wind. There is no grace or loveliness in this world, but only error and seduction, which we are commanded not to love, but much rather to hate with all our hearts. And in truth, this world is to be hated; for what it gives to its friends, it takes back from them in anger, stripping them of all goods, clothing them in shame, overwhelming them with great hardship, and consigning them to eternal sorrow. Those whom it now exalts, it soon humbles with great poverty, casting them under the feet of all their enemies. And these are but its "graces" and "gifts," for it is an enemy to all its friends, and a sworn foe to those who do its will. It miserably tears apart those who hope and boast in it, and bleeds dry <note>Original: *äderet die auß*, meaning to drain the very lifeblood or resources</note> those who place their trust in it; for it has made a pact with those who wander and has given them false promises to draw them to itself.

To those who obey it, the world is ungrateful and deceitful, and it does not keep what it promises. For today it fills them with precious food and treats them with outward splendor; tomorrow it rejects them entirely. Today it makes one a king; tomorrow, a rejected servant. Today it makes one rich; tomorrow, a beggar. Today it sets the crown of honor upon one’s head; tomorrow it throws him to the ground. Today it adorns and burdens his neck with precious stones; tomorrow it throws him into irons and

Those who serve such evil hearts, turning their whole minds away from the gentle and kind heart of God to cling to worldly affairs, are entirely bound to them. I compare such a person to a man fleeing from a cruel **unicorn** <term>original: *Einhorn*</term> <note>In medieval parables, the unicorn often represents Death, which pursues all living things relentlessly.</note> that seeks to swallow him. and saw a terrifying dragon that spat fire and looked at him with cruel eyes, also opening a terrifying mouth, desiring to devour him. After this, this man looked at the turf <term>turf<note>original: Wasen; refers to a patch of grass or sod, here symbolizing the fragile physical foundation of the human body.</note></term> upon which he stood, and saw four snake-heads gnawing at the turf. And he looked up again and saw that a little honey dripped from the small shrub, and the drops fell upon his tongue. Then he neglected to consider the misery that surrounded him: the mad, devouring unicorn <note>In this medieval context, the "unicorn" (Einkirn) is not a gentle creature but a symbol of the relentless pursuit of Death.</note> that desired to swallow him and raged above him; the cruel dragon beneath him that likewise desired to swallow him; that he had placed his feet on a slippery turf; and that the shrub was almost entirely gnawed away by the mice. He forgot all these things and dangers and cast them to the wind, surrendering himself to the little honey that flowed from the shrub and its sweetness. And thus, he fell into the pit and was swallowed and consumed by the hideous dragon.

This parable applies only to those who cling to and serve this world. I will also tell you the interpretation of this parable. The unicorn is a figure and representation of Death, which constantly persecutes the human race and exerts itself so that it may consume it. The pit is this world, which is full of all evil and deadly snares. The small shrub that he grasped, which is gnawed without ceasing by the two mice, is the span of our life, which is consumed and diminished by the hours of the day and the night, which have nearly run their course and passed away. The white mouse signifies the hours of the day; the black signifies the hours of the night. But the four snakes beneath the turf signify the four

four unstable elements <note>The four elements: fire, water, earth, and air, which medieval science believed composed the human body (the four humors).</note> from which the human body is joined together. When these four elements—fire, water, earth, and air—are not joined in equal order within the person, and one surpasses the other, then the body is altered and destroyed; then the turf falls—that is, the human body. The most cruel dragon in the pit signifies the cruel, terrifying belly of Hell, which desires to receive and swallow those who prefer and love the sweetness of this world over heavenly things. ## The 13. Chapter.

When Josaphat had heard this parable, which he received gladly and with joy, he said to the old Father: "How true this speech is, and how finely it compares to the lovers of this world! I pray you that you would not find it burdensome or let yourself be annoyed to tell me more of these examples, so that I might learn what this present life is, and what evil and disadvantage it brings to those who are its friends and love it—those who also love their friends, children, temporal goods, and pleasure more than God, etc."

<meta>This page continues the teachings of the hermit Barlaam to Prince Josaphat. Following the parable of the man in the pit, Barlaam now introduces the "Parable of the Three Friends," a story intended to illustrate the vanity of worldly attachments and the importance of good works.</meta>

<header>On the Life and Conduct</header>
<meta>catchword: expect</meta>

To this the old Barlaam answered the King’s son: Those who love worldly pleasures and are led astray by the sweetness of this world, and who love worldly, fleeting benefits more than those that are to come and remain eternal, are like a man who has three friends. Two of these he loves and honors from his heart, even desiring to risk his life, soul, and goods for them. But the third he does not love or honor nearly as much, doing only as much as honor required to show himself friendly toward him, acting as though he loved him. 

It happened one time and day that the Emperor’s horsemen came terrifyingly to this man and informed him they had an order to lead him before the Emperor so that he might give an account before him, for he owed him ten thousand pounds <note>The "ten thousand pounds" represents an insurmountable debt of sin that a person must account for at the end of their life.</note>. Then he was greatly terrified, for anxiety and need surrounded him on all sides, and he looked for a friend who would help him make the accounting and stand by him before the Emperor, and also help pay the debt. 

In this thought, he went to the first friend, whom he loved above the others, and spoke to him: "O my dearest friend, you know that I have always put my soul on the line for you, but today I need your help, for anxiety and need have surrounded me. If you will only help me, O dearest one, then promise me your aid." 

To this he answered him and said: "O man, I am not your friend, nor do I know who you are, for I have other friends with whom I must enjoy myself and be merry today, whom I will henceforth have as my friends. I will, however, give you two small cloths <note>The "two cloths" (shrouds) symbolize material wealth, which can only provide a person with burial garments at the time of death.</note> that you may have on the way you will go, but they will be of no use to you; you have no other help to expect from me."

When this distressed man heard such an answer from his friend, he despaired entirely of his help and lost all hope that he had in this, his best friend. Thus, he departed from him without any comfort or hope, taking only the two small cloths with him, and went on and came to the second friend and spoke to him: "O friend, remember how you received honor, loyalty, and good things from me. Now I need help today, for I have fallen into great anxiety and adversity. Therefore, I beg you to tell me with how much you are willing and able to help me now." 

To this, this friend answered him: "I have no leisure or time today to go with you and make an accounting for you, for I myself am burdened and troubled with much work, business, and care. However, I will go a little way with you and escort you, although it will not bring you much benefit or use, for I must soon return home to attend to my trade and household." <note>The second friend represents one's family and kinsfolk, who can only accompany a person as far as the grave before returning to their own lives.</note> 

Then this man also departed from this friend with empty hands and was now robbed of all comfort, and lamented weepingly that all his friends, upon whom he had placed his hope, had abandoned him, and that all hope was in vain and for nothing—everything he had shown these ungrateful friends in honor, love, and goodness. And he thought to himself: "Now I will go to the third friend, whom I never regarded, nor allowed to be a companion of my friends, nor invited to my good living." And he went to him with downcast eyes and bowed his head to the earth, and out of shame he spoke to him: "O friend, I am ashamed that I should come to you or address you, for I well recognize that I never did you any good, honor, nor..."

Then the man struck himself <note>A traditional gesture of repentance and self-reproach.</note> and said with hot tears: "Woe to me, poor wretch! Or do I more rightly mourn my small friendship and service which I showed and demonstrated to this, my dearest friend and brother, as I rightly should have done?" And this last and third friend pleaded with the Emperor for him, and obtained so much that he forgave him all his debt and declared him free, etc.

you should, O King Josaphat, understand it thus. The man who owed the Emperor ten thousand pounds <note>original: "zehen tausent Pfund"; representing an insurmountable debt of sin.</note> is the sinner, who is obliged to give an account to God, the highest Emperor, for all his misdeeds and guilt which he has committed here against Him. Then come the terrifying horsemen—Death—and the bailiffs <note>original: "Schörgen"; the officers or henchmen of a court.</note> of this Emperor, and they demand the sinner before the Emperor to give an account for the ten thousand pounds; that is, for his sins, his deeds, and his omissions. He then becomes terrified and is surrounded on all sides by anxiety and distress, and then comes to the first friend—that is, to the possession and love of great goods and money, for the sake of which man throws himself into many kinds of danger, anxiety, distress, sweat, and labor. Then, when the hour of death creeps near, he says to his friend: "O my dearest friend, for whose sake I have always staked my body and soul, now a need presses upon me today, that I must pay the Emperor ten thousand pounds; come and help me, and make an accounting before the Emperor for me, and release me from eternal imprisonment." Then the friend will say: "O man, I do not know you, nor am I your friend; I have other friends, who are the lovers of this world, of temporal goods, gold, and silver, with whom I wish to enjoy myself and be of good cheer, for these are my friends. However, I will give you two small cloths: one for your head, the other for your body. These are the burial shrouds, which bring you little benefit for your accounting; you have no other hope, comfort, or help to expect from me." Then this friend will also say: "Friend, I confess that you have done much good for me and shown much friendship, and even pledged body and soul on my behalf; but I cannot help you, for I have my own affairs to attend to—with my wife and children, with my work, temporal goods <note>In this context, 'temporal goods' refers to worldly, material possessions that do not last beyond this life.</note>, and other bodily cares—so much to think about that I cannot help you at all. When they have accompanied him to the grave, and he is scraped in <note>original: "gescharret" — a blunt, physical term for burying a body.</note> and covered with earth, they depart again, each to their own trade, household cares, and the needs of their bodies, caring little how their deceased friend fares with his accounting, or who pays his debt. And thus the poor sinner is abandoned by father, mother, wife, children, sisters, brothers, and other friends. But the third friend, whom the man little esteemed and to whom he showed no or little friendship, are **good works**, such as the six works of mercy <note>Based on Matthew 25, these traditionally include feeding the hungry, giving drink to the thirsty, clothing the naked, sheltering the stranger, visiting the sick, and visiting the prisoner.</note>, faith, hope, and love toward God and neighbor, and other good works and virtues, of which the man does no more than he is obliged to do by the command of God and the Christian

Church. These offer themselves to go before us and give an account for us, and to pay the debts for us to the highest Emperor. A person should serve this friend here and keep and show himself friendly toward him; that is, that he should do good here and flee evil, and leave father, mother, children, sister, brothers, and all worldly joy, pleasure, and goods. Thus this friend brings him into the grace of the highest Emperor and pays all his debt—that is, his sins. Therefore, O King Josaphat, consider which is better: to serve the world with all its joys and pleasures of worldly goods and friends, and to love them above all things, which brings nothing else but eternal damnation and imprisonment; or to do good works and apply oneself to them, to serve and fear God, to renounce and speak against the world and everything in it, and through this friend to remain in the eternal friendship of God and pay all debt before Christ the Judge. I tell you, this is the right and true friend, who keeps our good works in memory and gives them back with interest—that is, in eternal life.

"...and sweet speeches have gladdened my heart; therefore, please continue to depict and illustrate the vanity <note>In this context, 'vanity' (German: *Eytelkeit*) refers to the emptiness or fleeting nature of worldly things, a common theme in medieval moral literature.</note> of this world, so that I might lead this life of mine in peace and security."

Upon this, the good old father Barlaam spoke further to King Josaphat and said: "I have heard that there was a great city whose inhabitants and citizens had this custom from of old: they knew nothing of their rights, statutes, commands, and prohibitions, and they would take a man and make him king, giving him all power to rule, to act, to command, and to do everything according to his will—but only for the duration of one year. Now, this king would imagine he was entirely secure in his position, believing he would henceforth always live and remain in such royal dignity, honor, pleasure, and sweetness. But when the year came to an end, the citizens rebelled and rose up against him; they stripped him of his royal robes and led him naked and bare through the whole city, and sent him away to a far-off, desolate, and desert island. On this island, he found neither food nor clothing, and (as they say) nothing to bite or gnaw upon, nor anything to wear; thus, in place of royal finery, honor, and pleasure, he had to perish in bitterness from hunger, thirst, frost, and cold, and die a wretched death."

"But it happened at one time that they called another king, a man of no small understanding. When he unexpectedly came to such royal dignity and honor, he did not forget his own nature; he considered day and night how he might ensure his own welfare. For he was warned by one of his innermost counselors <note>The 'innermost counselor' represents the conscience or divine wisdom that warns a person of their mortality.</note> that these..."

[The previous kings] paid no attention, nor did they seek after it, and sent none of our goods ahead, such as gold—which is love toward God and our neighbor—and the money and silver—that is, alms—which makes us pure before God from all our sins, as well as other virtues and good works of mercy. These the Lord and strict Judge shall eternally reward on the Last Day with His Father’s kingdom. The precious stones, however, are other good works and examples that we should demonstrate before men and practice, sending them on ahead. For this path leads you into eternal and everlasting goods, if you follow this way, through which you are also withdrawn from the vanity <note>The German *Eytelkeit* refers to the biblical concept of "vanity"—the fleeting, hollow nature of worldly life.</note> of this world. In this vanity, I, a miserable man, was once sent and loved the world, and was long held captive by its sweetness, pleasure, and loveliness, until I saw and considered with my inner eyes that through this vanity the lives of all men are consumed. From this I have perceived that such things are all vain, and that there is no profit in them at all; therefore one should despise them all, forget them, and cast them aside. Although one might say: it is a magnificent, apparent glory and dignity <note>original: "Dignitet"</note>, a magnificent power of the mighty, or the dreadfulness of tyrants, or whatever else may be brought forth that a person may achieve in this world—it is all nonetheless useless and fruitless. Just as my ancestors did not fail to rejoice in temporal things, so it would have happened to me, had I not recognized how the tyrant and the sorrowful world trouble and lead people astray. One person she shifts from wealth into poverty, the other from poverty into wealth; from one she takes life, to another she gives it; the wise and understanding she rejects, the lowly and discarded she exalts; the fools and simpletons she sets in the seat of honor and glory, while the honorable and respectable she disdains and belittles.

Therefore, it is plain to see that the human race has no lasting city nor anything permanent before these tyrants. A person is just like a dove fleeing before a bird of prey—now onto one tree, then onto another, now under a bush, soon into a thorn hedge, or hiding in caves or rocks; and yet

[A person] finds no secure place before [death], and is thus driven back and forth without ceasing: So it is with those who fear losing temporal things, yet work for them with irrational violence and toil, and yet possess nothing certain or lasting. They do not even know what end they will meet, or that this life of toil and labor—to which they have subjected themselves—will leave them. For they desire evil instead of good, and instead of good works, they practice evil works. They do not know what "cold fruit" <note>The "cold fruit" refers to the useless or unsatisfying results of worldly labor that does not benefit the soul.</note> the heavy labor they have taken upon themselves will bring, whether it be a domestic or a foreign fruit—and often it benefits neither friend nor acquaintance, but much more their adversary and enemy.

I have brought many more such things before the judgment seat of my heart to be judged, and I found so much that I entirely despised and hated this base world and temporal life, which consumes itself in mere vanity and emptiness. In this vanity, I too was once entangled and devoted before my time; but when I withdrew and renounced it from the bottom of my heart, and set my thoughts further ahead, those things appeared to me which are truly good. That is: to fear God and do His will. And I recognized that to fear God is the beginning and the head of all good; it is also the beginning of wisdom—indeed, the most perfect wisdom and life itself. For such a life is without any mourning or resentment, and is safe and free from all uncertainty. It rests upon and within itself, as if upon the most upright and beautiful path of the commandments of the Lord, which recognizes in its gentle and subtle way that there-

It is smooth, and for those who walk through it, it happens as if they were taking a pleasant stroll; it fills their eyes with its sweet, visible contemplation, and directs their feet onto the path of peace.

As I now set my affairs in order in this way, and helped my slippery spirit and sought improvement, I heard the voice and words of a very wise and understanding Master, who cried out to everyone: "Come forward, all you who desire to walk the path of salvation <term>Seligkeit: a state of eternal happiness and union with God</term> and renounce the world’s vanity; for it passes away along with its form, and even now—yes, right now—it vanishes before your eyes. Therefore, depart from it, yet do not depart without a dowry <note>original: *Morgengab*—traditionally a gift given by a groom to a bride, here used metaphorically for the spiritual 'gifts' one takes to heaven.</note> and provisions for the journey that will serve you for eternal life. For you must walk and wander a long and far way, and such provisions (good works) will be highly necessary until you come to the eternal Fatherland." In that Fatherland, there are two kinds of regions or landscapes containing many dwellings. One He has prepared for those who fervently love Him with their whole heart and keep His commandments; it overflows and is full of every good thing. Those who have earned the right to dwell therein will remain eternally in a constant life, where there is neither pain nor sadness. But the other landscape is full of darkness, sadness, displeasure, aching, and woe, prepared for the Devil and his angels, where those who follow him and subject themselves to him shall be cast down.

To forsake death, to hate the world and its rulers, also to fear and hold your corruptible flesh as your enemy, and to run toward the incomprehensible light, and to lie beneath your cross, following him, so that you may be glorified with him and become an heir of eternal life.

Luke 18.</margin> rich man, who asked him and said: "Good Master, what must I do that I may inherit eternal life?" But Jesus said to him: "Why do you call me good? No one is good except God alone. You know the commandments: you shall not kill, you shall not commit adultery, you shall not steal, you shall not bear false witness, you shall honor your father and mother." When he heard this, he became sad, for he was very rich. When Jesus saw that he had become sad, he said: "How hard it shall be for those who have money to enter the kingdom of God! It is easier for a camel to go through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of God." Now when the saints heard this commandment, they forsook everything,

<column-break/>

everything, <note>The text repeats the word "lassen" (to leave/forsake) across the column break for continuity.</note> and distributed their wealth among the poor, so that they might gather eternal treasures for themselves; and they took up his cross and followed Christ—some through martyrdom, others through a spiritual, hermetic, <term>einsidlisches</term> <note>From "Einsiedler," meaning a hermit's or anchorite's life of solitude.</note> lowly, and humble life. # On Life and Conduct

Lies? Even in former times, a wise, understanding, learned, and modest man <note>Likely referring to King David in the Psalms.</note> looked down from above as if through a mirror and, observing this misery, cried out to all: "O you children of men, how long will you be of a hard and stubborn heart? And why do you love vanity, and seek after lies, and value this temporal life, its sweetness and pleasure, its small and perishable honor, its lowly power, and its uncertain welfare as blessed? For these do not belong to those who see them or think they possess them; instead, they pass away and are driven here and there like dust in the wind. They are like a smoke or a dream, and thus those who pursue them possess neither the temporal nor the eternal."

Since our Lord Jesus Christ commanded such things, and the Prophets and Apostles preached them, and the Saints exhorted others by word and deed to the righteous and true way of virtue—and though very few walk this narrow path, while many choose the broad path that leads to damnation—the value of this Divine Wisdom is nevertheless not diminished. For just as the sun illuminates everything when it rises, sharing its rays with all without any envy, so also does true wisdom. She illuminates those who desire and seek her, just as the sun does, making herself visible and revealing herself. And even if some should close their eyes and cannot look into the clear sunshine, or despise its brilliance and light as well as its truth, the sun is not to be blamed; rather, it is to be attributed to those themselves who deprive themselves of the light. And just as the blind go about bumping into walls, falling into pits and thorns, and tearing their 

<column-break/>

# Of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

faces: In the same way, the sun remains in its brilliance and illuminates everything with an open face. So too the Light of Christ shines for everyone and shares its brilliance most abundantly; everyone becomes a partaker of it according to what they desire and will. For it lies in the will of each person to behold the Sun of Righteousness <note>original: "Sonn der Gerechtigkeit"—a reference to Malachi 4:2, often interpreted by Christians as a title for Christ.</note> or else to choose the darkness; for the choice is left to the free will of each person as long as they are in this world.

Upon this, Josaphat asked: "What is free will and choice?" To this, the elder answered him: "Free will is the will of a rational soul, which no one forbids, which moves itself toward what it wants—toward the good or the evil—and such is granted by God. Conversely, free will is the understanding of a rational soul, and choice <term>Erwehlung: choice or election</term> is a desire or the counsel <note>original: "Raht"; refers to the process of deliberation.</note> of man, which he gives to himself; and what is judged from this counsel, that we choose and keep. Counsel, however, is a desire that searches within us for what we ought to do. Now, every counsel regarding what should be done or left undone is called the Judgment <note>original: "Iudicium"</note> or Understanding. Thus, before every choice there is counsel, and before every action there is pre-choice <term>Vorerwehlung: the internal decision or intention made before the physical act occurs</term>. Therefore, not only the action of evil or good, but also the things which the pre-choice offers according to the will—that is, the things the will pre-chooses to finally do, whether evil or good—even where the deed does not happen, these are a cause of the eternal crown or pain. For the beginning of sin, and the pre-choice of the work of righteousness, comes from the things which..."

<summary>Barlaam uses the analogy of the sun to explain that Divine Wisdom is available to all, though many choose to ignore it. He then defines free will and the process of moral choice (counsel, judgment, and intention) as the basis for eternal reward or punishment.</summary>
<keywords>Free Will, Divine Wisdom, Sun of Righteousness, Choice, Counsel, Judgment, Barlaam, Josaphat, Virtue, Sin</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the hermit Barlaam’s instruction of Prince Josaphat. Having previously discussed the vanity of the world and the "narrow path" of the righteous, Barlaam now explains the mechanics of the human will and the nature of moral choice.</meta>

On Life and Conduct

...have been introduced by us. And so it is concluded from this that choice is a desire united with deliberation regarding those things that are within our power. For that which we resolve through counsel, we then desire to choose. Thus, counsel is undertaken for the sake of the resulting action; counsel precedes the choice, and the choice precedes the action. Therefore, not only the action itself, but also that which is moved within the mind—which then causes the choice—brings either the crown <note>The "crown of life" or "crown of righteousness," a common biblical metaphor for the reward of heaven.</note> or eternal pain. 

For the choice of those things placed within our power is the origin of either sin or righteousness. For whatever inclinations are within us, their actions are also within us. If the actions of the virtues are within us, then virtue itself is within us, so that we become what we have deliberated and taken to heart. In this way, men may freely deliberate and choose what they will through their own free will. For whatever anyone has chosen up to this point in order to attain the eternal light, he is that thing, and he spends his time in the study of philosophy <note>In this context, "philosophy" refers to the "true philosophy" of a Christian life and the pursuit of divine wisdom.</note>. 

For there are many kinds of choice. Just as water springs from the innermost springs of the earth—some high, some deeper, some very deep, some springing quite near—and some are sweet while others flow from the depths and are salty or taste of sulfur; some flow abundantly and others only drip: in this same way is choice. For some, it is very quick and hot; for others, lazy and cold. Some are very quick and eager for virtue, while others are quite inclined toward all vices and sins. Therefore, as the choices are, so also are the deeds and omissions.

of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.

# Barlaam gives the King’s son 
### a hope of eternal life, and instructs him in the Christian faith, which at first appears harsh to look upon, but when it is rightly considered, is sweet and gentle.

## The 16th Chapter.

Josaphat said to the old man regarding this: "Are there others besides you who preach and teach such things? Or are you the only one who introduces this teaching at this time and makes this life so difficult?"

To this Barlaam answered him: "In this, your unhappy kingdom, I know of no one; for through the cruel tyranny of your father, unspeakably many of them have been executed with various gruesome deaths. He still exerts himself and labors to ensure that nothing of divine things is proclaimed or told to you. But otherwise, in almost all lands and languages, one finds those who sing and proclaim this little song <note>An idiomatic way of saying "preach this message."</note>. Some do so according to the right rule, while others—through the enemy of our souls—do so with an adverse understanding; though they are increased by certain passages of Holy Scripture, they do not interpret them according to the correct meaning. However, there is only one truth among the holy Apostles, the holy Fathers, and in the Catholic Church, which shines from one end of the world to the other more clearly than the sun. I have been sent to you as a teacher and doctor to instruct you in this."

Josaphat said to Barlaam: "Did my father never learn anything of these things?"

The old man speaks: With men such things are impossible, but with God all things are possible. <note>A reference to Matthew 19:26, emphasizing the power of divine grace over human stubbornness.</note> Who knows whether you might make your father worthy of salvation, and become a wonderful teacher to your own parent? For I have heard that at one time there was a King who governed his realm very well, showing himself to be gentle and kind toward his people and subjects; but this alone he lacked: that he had no knowledge of divine things, but was instead bound by <term>idolatry</term> <note>original: "Götzendienst" — the worship of idols or false gods, a common theme in conversion narratives.</note>. This King had a counselor who was very pious and good, adorned with all virtues and everything pertaining to the fear of God. This counselor was very troubled and sad that the King persisted in such error; he would have gladly corrected the King for it, but did not dare to do so out of fear that some evil might befall him or his kin, or that the many good works he performed might be hindered. However, he sought an opportunity to draw the King away from such things. At one time, the King said to him: "Come, let us go for a walk outside the city to see if we can find something pleasant." As they were walking through the city, they saw a light shining through a hole; they cast their eyes upon it and saw a dwelling beneath the ground. When this youth listened to this praise, he said: "O woman, what is this you do and your diligence, that you, who are poor, give praise and thanks for your gifts as if He had bestowed great things upon you?" Therefore, I, this poor old man’s daughter, give thanks and praise to God from my heart for the little that I have, and I am certain that He who gave this little can give something more and greater. There, he showed him wealth beyond measure lying in his room—it was such a vast pile and sum of money that the youth had never seen so much wealth and money together in one place. And the old man said to him: "Son, I give you all of this because you have chosen to be an heir to my goods." This youth possessed the inheritance and became more noble, powerful, and wealthy than all others.

is the same for life and its end.

Therefore, from the things that are within me, I have been led to the knowledge of the mighty workings of the Creator. Through these, I have noted, observed, and seen the well-assembled structure <note>Original: "Baw," referring to the "edifice" or architecture of the universe.</note> and the preservation of all creatures. I have seen that all these things are, in themselves, subject to change and destruction—even intelligible things <term>intelligible things: spiritual beings or concepts understood by the mind rather than the senses</term> are subject to free will, for they are moveable from one state to another. And for this very reason, they proclaim with an unspeakable voice that they were made and created by the inexhaustible and unchanging God, by whom they are always enclosed, preserved, and provided for. For how could two opposing natures, such as the physical and the spiritual—that is, body and soul—remain so indissolubly joined to fulfill this single world, if the power of the Almighty had not so mixed them together and thus preserved them indissolubly at all times? How could all of this exist if His Omnipotence did not will and sustain it?

For just as a ship cannot exist without a pilot or captain, but soon sinks, and a small house cannot stand without an overseer, how could the world—such a great good and wonderful creation—exist without the glorified, mighty, and wonderful government, providence, and wisdom of God? Look and perceive how long the heavens have existed without growing weak; the strength of the earth has never grown tired throughout this long time

since they were created; the springs have not ceased to flow as long as they have existed; the sea, which receives so many great rivers, does not overflow its bounds or end; the course of the sun and the moon do not change; the order of day and night does not reverse. From all this, the unspeakable power and greatness of God is explained and made manifest to us, which the prophets and apostles have testified to us; yet no one can sufficiently contemplate or praise His glory and majesty. But He has mercy on everyone. For the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul</note> considers all visible and invisible things and says: Our knowledge is imperfect, and our prophesying is imperfect; but when that which is perfect comes, then that which is imperfect will cease. # Of the Life and Conduct

...the beginning, middle, and end of times, and the distinction and change of the hours, and that He has ordered all things in number, measure, and weight. For He has always shown His mighty power, and who can withstand His mighty arm? For the world before His face is like a moving grain <note>Original: "Kor," likely a truncated form of "Körnlein" (grain). Then Josaphat said to him: "O most wise one, even if you had considered for a long time how you might explain the questions I asked of you, I believe you could not have done better than to explain and relate these things to me as you have just done. For you have taught me that God is the Multiplier and Giver of all things and all good deeds; that His goodness is unspeakable and the memory of Him is incomprehensible to men. You have also shown me with clear words the glory of His great power, which I have heard gladly, and I have marveled greatly at His wisdom."

### What the right and true life is, also what the right and true death is; and what the monk’s clothing and food may be.
### The 18th Chapter.

"We shall let these things pass for now; but tell me, you blessed man, how many years old are you? In what place and region do you dwell, and what kind of 

---

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>
<note>A "Confessor" is a title for a saint who suffered for the faith but was not martyred.</note>

companions do you have in this wisdom of yours? For I tell you, my soul clings strongly to your soul, and I do not wish to be separated from you at any time."

The old man Barlaam spoke: "I am (as I believe) forty-five years old, and I lead a wretched life in the desert of Sennaar <term>Sennaar: Also known as Shinar, a biblical region often associated with Babylon; here it refers to the desert where the hermits lived.</term>, and I have companions with me who fight and contend in a spiritual struggle <note>The "spiritual struggle" refers to the ascetic life of monks fighting against temptation.</note>, and who labor in the course of the highest path that leads to eternal life."

Josaphat said: "How can you say that? For I look upon you and see that you are more than seventy years old."

Barlaam answered him: "If it is the case that you ask about the years since my birth, then you have estimated correctly that I am more than seventy years old. But these years which I spent in the vanity of this world are not counted by me in the number and measure of my life. For when I lived according to the body and the pleasure of the flesh, and served sin, I was dead in the inner man; therefore, I shall never again call those years of death the years of life. But since the world has been crucified to me, and I to the world <note>A reference to Galatians 6:14.</note>, I do not live now in the flesh, but Christ lives in me. That I live in the faith, I live for the Son of God, who gave Himself for me; these years I rightly count as the years of life, and call them the days of salvation. This time I reckon to be about forty-five years, and these are the years I have told you of. You should therefore make this calculation for yourself, and realize that those who are dead to good works are in no way alive, but rather live for sin and for the ruler of this world, and are thereby separated from the Good..."

# Of the Life and Conduct/

drawn away, they consume their life in pleasure and excess; you may well consider them as dead and erased from this life. For eternal Wisdom has most elegantly called sin "Death": for He speaks through the Apostle:
<margin>Romans 6</margin>
When you were slaves of sin, you were free from righteousness. But what benefit did you have at that time in the things of which you are now ashamed? For the end of such things is death. But now, since you have been set free from sin and have become servants of God, you have your fruit in being made holy, and the end is eternal life; for the wages of sin is death, but the grace of God is eternal life, in Christ Jesus our Lord.

To this, Josaphat said: "Since this fleshly life cannot properly be called a life, then this temporal death may also not be called a death."

The elder <note>Referring to Barlaam</note> answered him: "Doubtless, I also understand these things in this way, and I do not fear this temporal death in the least, nor do I call it a death; for when it takes me while I am keeping the Divine commandments, it is much more a departure from death into a better, more perfect, or more certain life, which is hidden in Christ—which all the Saints have desired, having carried a weariness toward this temporal life. <margin>2 Corinthians 5</margin>

And therefore we groan, and long for our habitation which is from heaven, and desire to be clothed with it, provided that we are found clothed and not naked.

I know certainly that if your father were to learn of these things, he would (if it were possible) subject me to a thousand deaths: but I place the word of God before all of this, and I am not terrified by temporal death, indeed, I do not even deem it worthy to be called a death. I hold to the word and teaching of the Lord, who says: Do not fear those who kill the body and cannot kill the soul; but fear him much more who is able to destroy both body and soul in hell.

# Of Life and Conduct
<meta>catchword: like</meta>

...to you the clothing/ and what your garments are/ I pray you/ that you would clearly and truthfully show and explain this to me.

Barlaam answered him: Our nourishment by which we live/ comes from the fruits of the trees and herbs that grow and are found in the desert/ which are also sustained and made fruitful by the dew of heaven/ and obey the command of God. Concerning this food, we are troubled by no one/ and no one fights or quarrels with us over it/ this food is prepared for all of us without any envy/ and the table is always ready. But if it happens that one of the believing brothers who dwell around us/ gives and shares with us the blessed or dear bread/ we take and receive such bread/ as if it were sent and blessed for us by Divine Providence. But our clothing is made of hard wool/ badger and camel skins/ and hair/ with these we wear down and bruise our tender bodies/ and that is our clothing and covering summer and winter/ in heat/ cold/ rain and snow/ which we also never take off (as it is by no means proper for us to do)/ but keep it on for so long/ until it entirely rots/ and falls away: for when we are thus troubled and killed by the heat and cold in such garments/ we deserve to be clothed with the garments of immortality.

Josaphat said to him: Where then do you get this garment/ which you have on now?

But I hope in the mercy of God that you shall soon become rich: yet you will not then be merciful <note>Barlaam is likely using "merciful" here in the sense of giving alms; if Josaphat becomes spiritually rich by giving everything away, he will have no material goods left to give as "mercy" or charity.</note>.

Upon this Josaphat spoke: Tell me only, and explain this speech to me: how the least among your people surpasses me in riches, though they live in the highest and most extreme poverty and want, as you previously indicated; and why you also call me poor; and why you believe that if I were to become rich, I would not be merciful.

Barlaam answered him: I did not say that they were saddened by their earthly poverty, but rather that they are abundantly gifted and showered with imperishable riches. For to constantly increase money with money, and not to hold oneself in check, but to proceed insatiably and struggle for it—this is eternal poverty and the most extreme misery. Therefore, those who despise the present things because of their desire for the Eternal, and at the same time hold them to be as dung <note>original: "Kot" — an allusion to Philippians 3:8, where worldly gains are counted as refuse or dung compared to Christ</note>, so that they might win Christ alone, and regard neither food nor clothing, and have no worry for them, but leave all that to the Lord—they rejoice in poverty, just as the lovers of this world sometimes rejoice in riches, honoring and loving money. They gather the "virtues" of wealth without any hatred, living in the hope that it will never slip away from them; and being fattened in this manner, I have rightly called them richer than you and all earthly kingdoms. For through the help of God, you too shall receive such spiritual riches, which, if you guard them quite diligently and increase them through merit, they shall

never be exhausted. And this is the true wealth. Temporal, immoderate wealth corrupts its lovers more than it benefits them; for which reason I have rightly called it extreme poverty, which the lovers of heavenly goods completely renounce and flee from as if from the face of a serpent. one kingdom, one power, and one authority: for it is common to the Son and the Holy Spirit that they are from the Father: for the Father’s unique property is that he is unbegotten, while the Son is begotten, and the Spirit proceeds from them both.

Therefore, you should believe this and not ponder or over-analyze too much how this birth and proceeding happened, for it is impossible <note>The author suggests that the mechanics of the Trinity are a divine mystery beyond human reason.</note> to grasp; rather, take it to heart and hold it as certain and without any deviation that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are one thing in all respects, except for the specific properties (of which a little was said before). And also that the only-begotten Son is the Word of God, and that God descended to the earth for the sake of our salvation, and through the will of the Father, with the cooperation of the Holy Spirit, was conceived without male seed in the body of Mary the Virgin and Mother of God <note>original: "Gottes Gebererin," a translation of the Greek "Theotokos," meaning the one who gave birth to God.</note> through the Holy Spirit, and was born from her as a true, perfect man, out of and in two rational natures, and in one Person. And this you should hold as certain and firmly believe; nor should you investigate how the Son of God humbled himself and became man from the purest drops of the Virgin's blood, without any male seed, and without violating her maternal purity. For trust and believe me, that we are taught to believe and hold such things through divine inspiration and Holy Scripture, but the "how" is unknown to us, and we are not even able to speak it.

You should also believe that the Son of God became man out of his innermost mercy,

For the dead shall rise, and those who are in the graves shall come forth; and those who have obeyed and kept Christ’s command, and have also departed from this life in the right, true faith, they shall possess eternal life forever. But those who allow themselves to be corrupted by sin and have turned away from the true faith, they shall fall and come into eternal punishment. For believe that sin has its origin neither in God, nor in any kingdom or principality, but from the Devil and ourselves, out of our free and own will; for we have acted out of our own discretion, or have chosen out of our own will, either evil or good. The image of the Divine Word, who for our sake became flesh, you shall honor with a kiss, and let it seem to you as if you are seeing the Savior in his own image. For a holy man says: <note>This is a reference to St. Basil the Great, often quoted by St. John Damascene: "The honor paid to the image passes to the prototype."</note> the honor shown to the image passes to the one whom it signifies; for through the figure of the image that we see, we raise the eyes of the mind to the true vision of the one it represents to us. Also, in adoring such an image of him who became flesh for us with devotion, we do not make it into a god; rather, we honor and kiss the image of him who became our flesh because of the heartfelt desire and love through which he humbled himself for our sake, even to the form and shape of a servant.
