You should also, in the same way, adore the sign of the life-giving and venerable Cross, and hold it in high honor because of him who hung upon it in the flesh for our sake—namely, Christ the Savior of the world. He gave this to us as a protection against the malice, deceit, and snares of the devil; for the devil is terrified by this sign and is overcome with trembling and fear, and he cannot look upon its power. Now, if you are baptized with and in such teaching, and in such faith, and keep it free from all heresy and unadulterated until the departure of your body's last breath, and reject all teaching and pretension that is contrary to such unadulterated and spotless faith—remembering that such things separate one from God. For the Apostle says: "If an angel from heaven should preach otherwise than what we have preached to you, let him be accursed" <note>original: "im Bann," referring to anathema or being cast out from the community.</note> or separated from the community. For there is no other Gospel, nor any other faith, than that which was preached by the Apostles and confirmed by the holy fathers through many synods <note>A "synod" is an official council of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine.</note>, and which has been handed down to the Catholic <note>In this historical context, "Catholic" refers to the "universal" or "orthodox" Christian church before the later denominational splits.</note> Church.

When Barlaam had spoken these things and explained the Nicene Creed <note>original: "Nicenischen Synodi Glaubens Symbolum," referring to the statement of faith composed at the Council of Nicaea in 325 AD.</note> to the King’s son, he baptized him in the fishpond that he had in his pleasure-garden, in the name of God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit; and the grace of the Holy Spirit came upon him. When he returned again to his chamber, and Barlaam had performed the mystery of the Holy Mass, he also made Josaphat a partaker in this mystery of Christ <note>Meaning he administered Holy Communion to Josaphat for the first time.</note>. Whereupon he leaped up in spirit and praised God with laud and honor.

<header>->Of the Life and Conduct /<-</header>

Upon this Barlaam said: Blessed be God, the Father of my Lord <note>The original German "Herzen" (heart) is likely a transcription or printing error for "Herren" (Lord), as this passage closely paraphrases 1 Peter 1:3.</note> Jesus Christ, who through his bottomless mercy has reborn you into a living hope, and an unbreakable, unstained, and everlasting inheritance, which is kept for you in heaven through the Holy Spirit in Jesus Christ; for on this very day you have been freed from sins and have received the eternal pledge, have been made a servant of God, and have left the darkness and put on the light, having been accepted as an eternal son of glory. For John says:
<margin>John 1</margin>
But as many as received him, to them he gave power to become children of God, to those who believe in his name. Therefore, you are now no longer a servant of sin, but a son and heir of God, through Christ in the Holy Spirit. Therefore, most beloved, strive to be found pure and unstained, and build good, wholesome works upon the foundation of faith; for faith without works is dead, just as works without faith are likewise dead, as I have told you these things before.

Therefore, put aside and cast away all evil works; forget and hate all the works of the "old man" <note>A biblical term referring to one's former sinful nature before spiritual rebirth.</note>, which advise only toward destruction, and be like a new and just-born child, and desire without any fear the milk of reason and virtue, so that you may grow therein, and come to the knowledge of the commandments of the Son of God, into a perfect man, and into the full age of the grace of Christ; so that you are not driven hither and thither like a child by childish things, but rather wish to be a child in malice, yet toward the good [to have] a future steadfast
<meta>catchword: mind</meta>

---

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>->of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.<-</header>

mind, so that you may walk worthily in the calling (to which you have been called), in the keeping of the commandments of the Lord, and put away the fellowship and practice of your former life and conduct—not like the heathens who walk in their vanity and are thereby blinded, and are far from the glory of God, for they are led astray in their desires and fleshly dealings. But you have stepped forward and come to the true living God; so live and walk also as a son of light: For the fruits of the Spirit consist in all good virtues, righteousness, and truth. Therefore, do not let the "new man" whom you have put on be corrupted henceforth by the old perishability, but rather cast such things away from day to day, and increase and grow in righteousness and true holiness: for it is possible for him who now wills it, as you have heard, for he gave them power to become children of God. Therefore, we cannot say now that it is impossible for us to possess virtue, for the way is easy and level, even though it is called narrow and sharp because of the emaciation and chastening of the body; yet it is desirable and right because of the hope of future things, for those who do not walk unwisely therein, but diligently pay heed to the will of God, and put on his weapons and armor to fight against the malice of the Adversary <note>Original: "Widersachers" — a common title for the Devil.</note>, and are watchful in prayer against him, in all patience and hope. As you have heard and learned such things from me, draw from them a firm and strong foundation, that you may grow and proceed in the same, and fulfill the service of a brave and good soldier in

<meta>This page continues the sermon of the hermit Barlaam to Prince Josaphat. The previous page concluded with an exhortation to have a steadfast mind and walk worthily in one's calling. # On the Life and Conduct

<page-num>146</page-num>

a steadfast faith and conscience, in the pursuit of righteousness, godliness, faith, love, patience, and gentleness, so that you may attain eternal life, to which you are called. On the other hand, however, you must not only put away from you through your deeds all lust, and the sweetness and flattery of vice, but you must strike them from your thoughts, so that you may present a spotless soul to God. For not only our works, but also our thoughts serve us either toward the eternal crown or toward punishment, and are a cause thereof; for we believe that Christ, together with the Father and the Holy Spirit, dwells in pure hearts. On the other hand, just as smoke drives away hornets <note>original: "Hurnnaussen"</note> and flies, so do useless thoughts drive away the grace of the Holy Spirit.

Therefore, you should apply all diligence to remove evil thoughts from your heart, and on the contrary, apply yourself to good ones, so that you may become a temple of the Holy Spirit. For through thoughts we come to the work itself; for all works follow through the thoughts and incitements of the mind. At first, these may seem small, but afterward they increase silently and finally become great. Therefore, you should in no way allow an evil habit to rule within you; rather, while it is still new, tear the perverse, evil roots out of your heart, so that they do not grow up. For if they take deep root, they can only be rooted out again with greater difficulty and pain. For heavy and great sins overcome us much more, and burden our souls, if we do not avoid them—such as evil thoughts, indiscreet speech, and useless, vain conversation,

<column-break/>

<page-num>147</page-num>

# the Holy Confessor <note>A "Confessor" is a title for a saint who suffered for the faith or lived a life of great piety without being martyred.</note> Barlaam and Josaphat.

which then stand in need of considerable, sharp punishment and correction. Just as those who perhaps do not much heed small bodily weaknesses and toss them to the wind, often entirely poison the body and bring it to death; so also small sins are the soul's harm and disadvantage, and cause one to fall into heavy sins and vices. If the soul brings these into a habit, she laughs at them and considers them trivial. For <margin>Proverbs 8 <note>Though the margin cites Proverbs 8, the quote "When the wicked comes into the depth of sins..." is traditionally associated with Proverbs 18:3.</note></margin> as the wise man says: the unjust and godless, when he comes into the depth of sins, he despises them, <margin>2 Peter 2</margin> and wallows like a sow in the filth <note>original: "Vnflat"</note> and delights himself therein. Unfortunate, therefore, is the soul that is bound and captured by evil habits, for she does not taste the stench of sin, but delights and rejoices therein, and mistakes evil for a good work. Even if such souls eventually turn back and come to their senses, they are only released with great effort, labor, and sweat from those things they committed and the service of evil to which they subjected themselves.

Therefore, you should, with all your strength, divest and withdraw yourself from the habit of sin, useless thoughts, and all evil vices; practice virtues, and bring good works into a good custom. For when you apply small and simple diligence and labor well, and bring it into a good habit, you will overcome the greater things without any effort or labor through the cooperation of God. For the habit of virtues, once impressed upon a soul, has—as it were through the working of the Almighty—a natural recognition in the soul; for she remains immovable and constant, as you then see, that

<meta>This page continues the hermit Barlaam’s spiritual instruction to Prince Josaphat. Having previously discussed how thoughts are the seeds of actions, Barlaam now explains the psychological nature of virtue and vice, arguing that while evil habits are hard to break, virtuous habits—being more natural to the soul—become an inseparable part of one's being. He transitions into Chapter 20, which focuses on the transformative power of prayer.</meta>

# On the Life and Conduct

<page-num>148</page-num>

...the strength of wisdom, temperance, and justice are inseparable virtues, for the characteristics and works of such a soul come from the very foundation of the soul into which they are planted. Since the nature of evil is not natural to us, but comes from the outside and is brought into practice, it is difficult—indeed almost impossible—to root it out once established. How much more, then, will virtue, which is naturally part of our being and is firmly ingrained <note>original: "eingewurgt," literally "strangled in," used here to mean deeply or inextricably fixed</note> in our soul through a little effort and labor, be impossible to root out?

## At the end of this chapter, the King’s son Josaphat is instructed on the power and effect of pure prayer, and encouraged to contemplate both the temporal and the eternal.

### Chapter 20.

A man practiced in such virtues also told me: "When I had brought divine contemplation and the practice of good works into a constant habit, and had entirely filled my soul with such reflections, I sometimes wanted to set this habit aside; however, my mind held me back from doing so. It would not allow me to cease these contemplations nor turn away from them. I found that my conscience was heavily burdened and troubled by the omission, and it desired the habit again with incomparable longing, and could in no way be brought to any other focus. And if I ever 'let loose the reins' <note>A metaphor for relaxing one's spiritual discipline</note>, it quickly and swiftly returned...

# On the Life and Conduct

<page-num>150</page-num>

<margin>Psalm 33 <note>original: "Psal. 33." In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 34:15-17.</note></margin>
...to worship, as the Prophet says: The eyes of the Lord look upon the righteous, and His ears are open to their prayers. Therefore, the holy Fathers have said that through prayer, people may be united and devoted to God; they have also called it a work of the angels, a reward of future joy, and an approach to the kingdom of heaven. Above all other things, it is in prayer that a contemplation and beholding of the Holy Trinity occurs, to which the intent of devotion leads the mind. Therefore, it is not unfittingly called a reward and, as it were, a foreshadowing of blessedness <note>The German "Seligkeit" refers to both happiness and eternal salvation.</note>. However, not all prayer is called such, but only that which is worthy of the name—of which God is the master—which then elevates the one praying above everything on the earth, and is offered and presented to God the Lord with a pure heart.

I would wish that you acknowledged this, and strived to continue in it, for this prayer has the power to pull you from the earth toward heaven. But you should not proceed thoughtlessly or superficially in it; rather, you should cleanse your soul of all vices and wash away all evil thoughts, so that it becomes like a pure and clear mirror. You should also remove all anger and resentment from your heart, for such things most of all prevent our prayer from ascending to God. Therefore, erase all stains of sin in your heart, and through your almsgiving and mercy toward the poor, give your prayer wings. And thus, with tears <note>original: "Zehern," an archaic word for Zähren (tears), signifying deep repentance.</note>, offer it up to God. Then you may well say with the holy David—who, although he was a king burdened with many cares and affairs, nevertheless cleansed his soul from all

When you call out in this way, God will hear you, and while you are still speaking with Him, He will say: "Behold, here I am." When you receive such a prayer, you will be blessed. For it can never be that one who worships and asks God with such a free and joyful mind does not daily make new progress in the virtues and overcome all the snares of the evil enemy. For whoever warms their mind so, as a holy man says, and directs and lifts their soul into heaven—calling upon God with their heart, bringing their sins to mind, speaking with Him for forgiveness, and asking for the same with hot tears—there is no doubt that God will show Himself gracious to them according to His great goodness. From this daily practice and habit, one also achieves the setting aside of all the cares of this life and rules over human desire, and is granted as worthy to have fellowship with God. Above this, there is nothing more blessed or secure; may God make you worthy of such blessedness.

For all flesh is like grass, and the beauty and glory of man is like the flower of the field; the grass withers and the flower will rot, but the Word of God remains eternally. Meditate on this always, and may the peace of the heart be with you. May He enlighten you and grant you a good understanding, lead you also on the path of salvation, and drive all evil will far away from your mind. And may He mark your soul with the sign of the holy cross, so that no malice or evil befalls you, but that through good virtues you may become worthy to receive the unspeakable fruit of the soul and the unfailing kingdom, and be enlightened

# Barlaam has come under suspicion by the Prince’s tutors <note>original: "Præceptoribus" (Latin for teachers or instructors).</note> due to his frequent visits; therefore, he was permitted by the King's son to depart.

## The 21st Chapter.

When the venerable old Barlaam had instructed the King’s son with such wholesome teachings and speeches, he went back again to his lodging. But the young King’s servants, his tutors <note>original: "Zuchtmeister," referring to those responsible for the Prince's moral and formal education.</note>, and instructors wondered greatly at the constant entry and presence of Barlaam in the King’s palace. One among them, who was the most prominent and very much loved by the King—and who had also been highly commended by the old King so that he placed great trust in him—was named Zardan. He spoke to the King’s son: "O my Lord, you well know what a great fear I have of your father, and also what great trust he places in me; therefore he commanded and ordered me to serve you as one of his most faithful servants. But now I see, Lord, that this stranger man speaks with you very much and often, and I worry he is one of the faith of the Christians, against whom your father shows himself so hostile and tyrannical. If it is the case that this is so, then I must die a miserable death. Therefore, do one thing: either make these matters known and manifest to your father, or refrain further..."

When Barlaam came again to the King's son, he led Zardan with him behind the fore-curtain or hanging, and spoke to the old Barlaam:
> Dear old father, now tell and explain your divine and salvific teachings to me once more, so that they may be all the better and more deeply imagined and imprinted upon my heart.

Then Barlaam began and spoke much of God and His goodness, and the faith, and how one should love Him with the whole heart, the whole mind, and the whole soul, and keep His commandments in fear and love, for He is a Creator and Multiplier of all visible and invisible things. He also called to mind the creation of the first man, and recounted what commands and laws He had given him; he also depicted heaven and hell, and how the Creator took care for our salvation and shared His goodness with us. He told how He also sent His prophets to announce the Incarnation <term>Menschwerdung: the Christian doctrine of God becoming human in Jesus</term> of His only-begotten Son, to show the manifold benefits of His descent and His becoming man, the miracles shown to us ungrateful people, the Cross, suffering, His voluntary death and resurrection, the call to goodness, the Kingdom of Heaven, and how to avoid the well-deserved punishment of the wicked, such as unquenchable fire, incessant darkness,

take for myself. For that which I have refused to my brothers/ and though it were good to have and possess much money/ I would have more rightly granted it to them than to myself/ and taken it for their sake. Since I then recognize the danger/ which lies in the possession of the same/ I will subject neither them nor myself to such snares.

As he would not grant such a request/ Josaphat placed another plea before him/ that he might not entirely despise his request/ and leave him something as a legacy/ and he begged him for his hair shirt/ and hard mantle/ so that he might have and use such things as a memorial of the strict/ hard and holy life of his master/ and against the evil temptations of the Devil: In return/ he wished the pious Elder would also take something from him/ so that when he looked upon it/ he might also be mindful of his <note>Josaphat’s</note> humility.

The Elder gave him such an answer/ and said: It would not befit me/ that I give away my old torn clothes/ and take new ones in their place/ lest I receive a recompense and a reward here in this time for my meager labor. But yet/ so that I may satisfy your desire and temptations/ let me seek old torn hair clothes/ which have no difference from my own clothes/ those I will take in place of my clothes.

As the holy Barlaam was soon to depart/ he gave the young King the final teaching/ and said: You/ my dearest brother/ and son/ whom I have birthed through the Gospel/ and to whom you have entrusted your secrets/ you shall know/ under what kind of King you fight/ and perform your service of knighthood/ <note>Barlaam uses the metaphor of "spiritual knighthood" (militia Christi) to describe the Christian life.</note> and whom you have recognized. Therefore it is necessary/ that you

hold this confession firm/ and perform your knighthood devoutly/ which you have promised to the Lord of all things/ in the handwritten record of your confession/ in the presence of all the heavenly host/ who shall then give testimony thereof/ and have recorded this your confession/ which you made in holy baptism/ preserve/ and keep the same unbroken/ so you shall be blessed. Therefore be mindful/ that you do not value or prize temporal welfare and fortune above God/ His grace/ and eternal goodness. For what is more terrifying in this life/ than the hellish fire/ which burns eternally/ and has absolutely no radiance or light/ also always torments/ and nevermore ceases? What on the other hand among all the goods of this world/ gladdens a man so highly/ as God/ who gives Himself to those who love Him/ whose beauty is unspeakable/ also His glory eternal/ which no one can uproot or destroy/ whose benefits which He reserves for His friends and lovers/ are inexpressible/ further and more exalted/ and better/ than all visible things/ yes/ which also no eye has ever seen/ no ear has ever heard/ nor has ever entered into any man's heart? Should I have comfort and joy in place of your love? For see/ you have reconciled me/ a wicked and apostate servant/ to God; and you have appointed me as an heir and son of eternal life/ and of the kingdom to come. You have sought me out as a lost/ wandering sheep in the desert of this world/ which had been cast as food to a wild beast—that is/ to all the evil spirits—and you have torn me away/ and joined me to the little sheep of God/ who have not gone astray. You have shown me a short path of truth/ which leads me out from the darkness and shadow of death/ into the light of eternal life.

But Barlaam/ wishing to still and soothe the King's son’s lamentation and weeping/ stood up/ and with uplifted hands performed his prayer toward heaven/ and said: Lord God Father of our Lord <note>Original: "Herzen," likely a transcription error or archaic variant of "Herrn" (Lord).</note> Jesus Christ/ who enlightens the darkness/ and has created and made the visible and invisible creatures out of nothing/ and has transformed your creation into yourself/ and has not let us sink into our ignorance and folly; to you we say ever praise and thanks/ and also to the virtue/ power/ and wisdom of our Lord Jesus Christ/ through whom the world was made/ and who has raised us up again who had fallen/ and awakened us who had sinned/ forgiving us such things/ bringing us wanderers back onto the right path/ redeeming us prisoners/ and making us/ who were dead/ alive again through the precious blood of your Son.

Therefore I call upon you/ and your only-begotten Son/ and the Holy Spirit/ that you would look upon this your <term>rational sheep</term><note>A traditional theological term for humans, distinguishing them from "unreasoning" animals because they possess a soul and intellect.</note>/ who through me/ your unworthy servant/ has come to your sacrifice/ and sanctify

his soul through your divine power and grace/ and preserve this your vineyard/ which has been planted by the Holy Spirit/ and grant him the true fruit of righteousness; strengthen him/ and make him fit for your testament; tear him out of the Devil's power/ and teach him the wisdom of the Spirit/ also to fulfill your will. The King sent a physician to him and begged him to apply himself to the utmost to cure him once again.

Therefore, the physician (seeing how dear and pleasant he was to the King) visited him very diligently. And when he had quite rightly and well observed his condition, he went immediately to the King and informed him that he could find nothing in him that might cause a sickness; and he held, moreover, that he had fallen into this illness out of grief and sorrow. Now, when the King heard this, he suspected at once that he might perhaps have been unkindly received by his son, for which reason he had withdrawn from him out of grief. But because he wished to know more thoroughly how matters stood, he sent word to Zardan that he would visit him the next day, to be informed of the cause of his arising illness.

But when Zardan heard this message, as soon as day broke, he put on his clothes and went to the King; and as soon as he stepped inside to him, he threw himself to the earth and worshiped him. But the King spoke: Why have you made the inconvenience for yourself to come to me? For I was of the will to go to you myself, and to show everyone the love I bear for you. To which Zardan answered: My sickness, most gracious King, is not one of those with which men are usually afflicted, but after I was sad in my spirit and anxious in my heart, my body also became sick. It would, however, be counted as a great and gross lack of understanding on my part if I did not (since such an opportunity presents itself) go to you as befits a servant,

When the King questioned him again as to what kind of negligence it was into which he had fallen, and what kind of danger it was of which he spoke, he said: In the guarding of my heart—your son—I have been un-diligent. Now, when all this came to the ears of the King, he was surrounded by such a sorrow that he burned entirely with anger, and by this message was almost beside himself. These men were of such a nature, and so strong-minded, that they were not in the least distressed or disturbed, nor did they show any sign of despondency or sadness. "So you know him then?" spoke Araches. "Rightly," answered the hermit, "do I know him whom one calls a deceiver, namely the devil, who dwells in the midst of you, and is also worshipped and honored by you."

"is [worshipped]." Then Araches spoke: "I am asking about Barlaam, where he may be." To this the monk replied: "Why," said he, "have you spoken so backwards, asking me about the one who deceived the King's son? The monk gave him this answer: "If he had a desire to see you, he would have come to meet you himself. But it is not fitting for us to show you his house."

At this, Araches became violently enraged and gave him a fierce and unkind look, saying: "Now I will kill you with new and unusual <term>tortures</term> <note>original: "Peinigungen."</note> if you do not immediately hand Barlaam over to me." "And what do you see in us," answered the hermit, "for whose love we would be unwilling to lose this life, or why we should fear your threatened death? Indeed, we would rather give you fit thanks for robbing us of this life while we are diligent in virtue. For the uncertain time of our departure causes us no small fear, in that we do not know at the very least in what state or being death will seize us: so that neither the instability of a good will, nor the storminess of the evil enemy, might change the constancy of our purpose, or persuade and talk us into doing or thinking something else (which is not in accordance with the covenants we have made with God). Therefore, since you are now altogether [ready] to seize that which we have promised ourselves for certain,"

## The Abbot, after he has held a long disputation with the King / is at last martyred along with his fellow brothers.

### The 23rd Chapter.

After the passing of a few days / he brought them thus to the King / to whom he revealed their nature / and presented them to him (who was enraged beyond measure). When he saw them / he became / because of

<column-break/>

<page-num>171</page-num>

his excessive wrath / like an insane person. And after he had them whipped with gruesome things / and then saw them so miserably hacked to pieces / his fury was yet hardly stilled / and he ordered the executioners to stop striking. But to them, the hermits, he spoke: For what reason do you carry these dead bones with you? If you carry them for the sake of those to whom they belong / then I will set you with those very same ones in this hour: so that / once your wish and desire is satisfied / you might give me thanks for it. That godly leader / however / and superior of the divine little house <note>A humble way of referring to their monastery or community.</note> / regarded the King's threats as nothing / just as if nothing adverse had ever happened to him / and answered with a free voice / and a brave countenance (which sufficiently showed the grace with which his soul was endowed).

These holy and pure bones we carry around with us for this reason / so that we might partly soothe the longing / which we have for those wonderful men to whom they belong / and remind ourselves of their spiritual exercise / as well as their godly conduct / and to encourage ourselves to pursue a similar life. And as we imagine for ourselves that rest / and those delights / in which they now live / we call them blessed: and stir each other up to follow in their footsteps. In addition / we also cause ourselves hereby the remembrance of death / which is powerfully useful / and makes us quite quick / and as if established / for the spiritual struggle: and then / we also draw holiness from the touching of the same.

Upon this the King spoke again: If then the remembrance of death / according to your opinion / is a benefit

<summary>A group of captured monks refuses to betray Barlaam's location despite torture and is brought before the King. The Abbot explains to the King that they carry the relics of deceased saints to inspire their own virtue and to keep the reality of death in mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King and the monk (the Abbot) regarding the nature of faith and the practice of carrying holy relics. The previous page ended with the King questioning the monks' devotion to the dead.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>Of the Life and Conduct/</header>
<meta>catchword: Monk</meta>

provides; why do you not use such a memorial more appropriately in your own bones, which are yours and will return to the dust tomorrow, rather than in these foreign and now decayed ones?

To this, the monk replied: Though I have shown five reasons why we carry these relics <note>original: "Heyligthummer"</note> about, you mock us by responding to only one. But believe me, these bones represent the memory of death more suitably and poignantly <note>original: "anstruckenlicher," meaning more impressively or touchingly</note> than those who are still in this mortal life. Since you speak of such things, and since your own bones, which are within your flesh, bring the memory of death to your mind, how is it that even now, being mindful of your own future death, you do not immediately seek the right counsel in your affairs? Instead, you have burdened your soul with all kinds of heavy vices. Furthermore, why do you kill in such an inhuman and tyrannical manner those who honor God and apply themselves to godliness—those who have never done you any harm, who have nothing in common with you in worldly matters, and who desire to take nothing from you?

To this the King spoke: I torment and kill you as harmful people and deceivers of the people. For you lead everyone into deception and turn them away from the pleasures <note>original: "Wollüsten"</note> of this life. Instead of a pleasant life and sweet desires, you drive them to take upon themselves such a filthy, hard, and laborious way of living. You also persuade them to attribute the honor that belongs to the gods to Jesus. Therefore, so that the people are not led astray by you, leaving the land desolate, falling away from the gods of their fathers, and worshipping a stranger, I have deemed you worthy of martyrdom and death. ### Nachor is secretly instructed by Araches to pose as Barlaam. The King scolds his son for having embraced the Christian religion.

## Chapter 24.

AS these had now departed in the form of a blessed death, the King addressed his chief advisor, **Araches**, in this way: "Since our first plan did not succeed for us, consider now the second, and bid **Nachor** be called here." Araches set out in the dark of night and went to Nachor's cavern, for he lived in the desert and practiced the <term>black arts</term> <note>original: "schwarzen Kunst," referring to magic or sorcery</note>. When he had revealed to him the entire plan they had begun, he returned to the King as soon as day broke. Following this, Araches took to his horse again, under the pretense that he wished to search for **Barlaam**. As he traveled on and wandered through the desolate, waste places, he saw a man coming out of a cave. When he commanded that the man should be captured,

---

<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>

his companions used a particular swiftness so that they seized the man and led him to **Araches**. When he then questioned him as to who he was, what religion he followed, and what his name was, he confessed he was a **Christian** and was named **Barlaam** (just as he had been instructed). For this reason, Araches acted as if he were mightily rejoiced, took him, went as quickly as possible to the King, and delivered him to him. Then the King spoke to him, so that those who were present heard it: "Are you **Barlaam**, a servant of the devil?" The man gave answer: "I am a servant of God, and not of the devil. Therefore, do not scold me so much. For you should thank me most highly that I have brought your son out of error and seduction, and taught him the **Word of God**, reconciled him again with the true God, and instructed him in all kinds of virtues." At this, the King acted as if he were once again very incensed, and spoke to him: "It would in truth not be unjust if I, taking away all your excuses and defense, had you killed immediately without any further inquiry. But out of my goodness, I will tolerate you until I have arranged an inquiry with you on a certain day. And if you will show me obedience, you shall obtain forgiveness. If not, you shall die a shameful death." When he had said this, he delivered him to **Araches**, with orders to guard him most diligently.

The following day thereafter, he returned to his usual palace. Thus, the rumor immediately went out that **Barlaam** had been captured: so that this also came before the King's son, who was therefore beyond measure terrified and saddened...

# On the Life and Conduct /

...to exert yourself and to attempt to turn me away from this excellent confession. For just as it would be an impossible and useless undertaking if you were to try to touch the heavens with your hands, or to drain the entire sea: so too would this be equally impossible for you. Therefore, either follow my counsel and surrender yourself to Christ, so that you may receive those goods which surpass all human thoughts, and so that we may both be companions in faith just as we are in nature: or, believe me in this, I will no longer be your son, but will serve God with a pure conscience.

When the King had heard all of this, he was immediately inflamed with grim wrath; he spoke angrily to him until his teeth gnashed together like those of a madman, and he spoke to him with these words: "Who else is the author of all these and such great evils of mine but I myself, in that I loved you so very much and did those things for your sake which no father has ever done? Because of this, your perverted mind and obstinacy—indeed, your <term>stubbornness</term> <note>original: "Hartnäckigkeit"</note>—strengthened by my goodness and indulgence, have brought you to the point where you oppose me. Therefore, the <term>astrologers</term> <note>original: "Astrologi"</note> and star-gazers did not predict in vain or unjustly that you would be an evil, presumptuous person, disobedient toward your parents. But if you persist in this now, so that my plans are thwarted, I will show myself as an enemy toward you, and punish you in such a way as no fierce man has ever done toward his enemies."

To this he spoke in return: "For what reason, O Ki—

Considering it would be far easier for you to follow the eagle's path and fly in the air than to turn me away from the Christian faith and the excellent covenant I have made with Christ. Instead, understand, O Father, cast away the blindness and darkness of your eyes; take up and look upon the light of my God, which enlightens everyone everywhere: and may you yourself finally be enlightened by His most lovely radiance. # On the Life and Conduct /

Why then do you embrace and hold onto that glory and majesty in such an indefinite and perverted way? It fades and is wiped out like fresh flowers. So too the cursed and stinking pleasures and desires of the belly, and those things <term>below the belly</term> <note>A common historical euphemism for sexual or carnal lusts.</note>, which indeed delight the foolish senses for a time, but are later digested more bitterly than gall. This happens precisely when the shadow and the dreams of this vain life pass away, and the lovers of it—as well as those who apply themselves to wickedness—fall into the eternal pain of the unquenchable and dark fire. There, the <term>restless worm</term> <note>A biblical reference to the "worm that dieth not" (Mark 9:48), symbolizing the eternal pangs of conscience in Hell.</note>, which never sleeps, will gnaw at them without end or pause, and the eternal fire will burn and roast them for all eternity. Among these—O, a terrifying and sorrowful thing—you will likewise be included because of your godless counsel. Burdened with heavy <term>penance</term> <note>original: "Buß"</note>, you will greatly desire these present days and will remember my words. But you will draw no benefit from such regret. Consider this: 
<margin>Psalm 6</margin>that in Hell, confession and penance have no place.

For the present time is appointed and ordained for work, but the future for reward. And in truth, even if the present pleasures were in no way subject to fading or ruin, but instead lasted for eternity with their masters, one should still not prefer them to the benefits and goods of Christ (which surpass all human thoughts). For just as the sun is much brighter and more radiant than the dark night, by just as much—and even more—are those goods which have been promised to those who fear God more radiant and 

<meta>catchword: more heartfelt</meta>

***

# of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.

more heartfelt than any earthly kingdom. Furthermore, it would be much more advisable and just to prefer that which is greater and more excellent to the lesser and worse. Since all things in this world are subject to ruin, passing away and being wiped out like a dream 
<margin>Job 14. Psalm 143.</margin>and a shadow, one should sooner rely on the path of a ship floating on the sea than on the unstable happiness of men. What sort of <term>simplicity</term> <note>In this context, it refers to a dangerous naivety or lack of spiritual judgment.</note> is this then—or rather, to speak more accurately, what folly and madness—to prefer that which is weak, perishable, and subject to ruin (indeed, which is nothing at all) to that which is imperishable and eternal? And for the sake of the fruit of these unstable and perishable things, should you be deprived of the enjoyment of those fruits and goods which are infinite? Will you not then understand this, O Father? Will you not let the perishable pass away and go, and betake yourself to that which is constant and certain? Will you not prefer the <term>fatherland</term> <note>original: "Vatterlandt"; here referring to the "Heavenly Fatherland" or the eternal home of the soul.</note> to traveling or wandering, light to darkness, the spirit to the flesh, eternal life to the shadow of death, and the imperishable to the perishable and fragile? Will you not tear yourself away from and flee the harsh servitude of the terrifying prince of this world—that is, the loathsome and seductive Satan—and betake yourself to the kind and most gracious Lord and God of mercy? Will you not withdraw yourself from the worship of many invented gods and worship the one, true, and living God? For even though you have sinned against God and poured out many blasphemies against Him...

The King, however, was overcome with both anger and wonder: the latter because of his son's great wisdom and such speeches, which could in no way be contradicted. And indeed, he could not perceive the light of this speech because of the great density of his inner darkness. Yet, because of the love he naturally bore for him, he could neither punish him nor do him any harm or chastise him. Having also completely despaired of bringing him away from his opinion once again, he feared that if he engaged in further speech against him—and if the son were to speak out freely and confidently, annihilating and insulting the gods—the King might become even more enraged and perhaps commit some hostile act against him. # Of Life and Conduct #

Rebuked as he was, the King was yet again led astray by his evil habits and shameful desires, to which he was already accustomed. These desires controlled him like a bridle and bit, preventing him from seeing the light of truth. And therefore, without moving even a single small stone (as the saying goes) <note>original: "ohne Bewegung eines einzigen Steinleins" — an idiom meaning without making the slightest effort to change or reform.</note>, he persisted in his previous intention, namely desiring to carry out the plan he had previously decided upon with Araches. He therefore spoke to his son: "It would indeed have been proper, my son, that you had lived simply <note>original: "simpliciter" — Latin for "unconditionally" or "without reservation."</note> according to my command in all things. But since you have offered such hard resistance through your stubbornness and self-will, I have come to grant your will more than my own, so that we might set aside all strife and obstinacy and yield to the truth. And since I hold Barlaam, who has thus misled you, captive, I will arrange a great assembly and call together both our own people and the Galileans <note>original: "Galilæer" — a common historical term for Christians, often used by their critics to imply they were merely a provincial sect.</note>, and previously have it explicitly proclaimed that no single Christian shall suffer any harm or distress, but all shall come together without any fear, so that we may weigh and consider the matter by common counsel. And if either you or your Barlaam should bring us over to your opinion, you shall obtain all that you wish and desire; but if, on the other hand, you are brought over to our opinion, you shall voluntarily obey our command."

The wise and courageous youth, however, who recognized the King's deceit through the vision he had received in a divine manner, said: "The will of the Lord be done, and as you have commanded." May that same kind...

<page-num>201</page-num>
# The Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat #

...God grant us the grace that we do not deviate from the right path. For in Him my soul hopes and trusts, and He will have mercy on me. Then the King commanded that both the idolatrous and the Christian people should come together; he sent letters and heralds <note>original: "Præcones" — Latin for public criers or messengers.</note> everywhere, who were to proclaim in all cities that no single Christian person should stand in fear of anything evil happening to them, but that all should come together safely, each according to their lineage and guild, for an unforced and unpressured investigation or inquiry into the truth, together with their leader, Barlaam. In this manner, he also called the idolaters and priests, as well as the Indian and Chaldean sages who were in his entire territory, and no less several soothsayers, sorcerers, and practitioners of demonic arts, so that they might triumph over the Christians.

An immense multitude indeed came together before the King, all of whom were of his accursed religion. Among the Christians, however, there was no more than one single man, named Barachias, who wished to offer assistance to Barlaam (or the one presumed to be him). For some of the God-fearing men were no longer living (having been killed here and there by the insane governors or bailiffs), while others lay hidden in mountains and caves for fear of the impending dangers; still others, fearing the King's threats, did not dare to come into the light, but were "nightly servants of God," worshipers and adorers who honored Christ alone in secret and did not freely profess His faith. But that man alone, being of a brave and courageous spirit...

[He] stepped forward in defense of the truth. When the King had seated himself upon a high throne, he commanded his son to sit with him. However, out of reverence and respect for his father, the son refused and sat upon the ground not far from him. Thus, the men experienced in wisdom—which God has made foolish—stood there; men whose <margin>1 Cor. 1. Rom. 1.</margin> unintelligent hearts (to use the words of the holy Apostle) were rebuked. For while they considered themselves wise, they became fools, and changed the glory of the incorruptible God into the likeness of an image of corruptible men, of birds, of four-footed, and of creeping animals. These men had now come together to oppose the King’s son and those who stood by him. And in them, this proverb was fulfilled: "The goat has entered into a struggle with the lion." For he <note>Josaphat</note> had placed his refuge in the <margin>Ps. 91. Ps. 57. Eph. 6.</margin> Most High and had his confidence under the shadow of His wings. But they had placed all their hope in the princes of this earth, who perish, and in the prince of this world <note>Satan</note>, to whom they had wretchedly subjected themselves.

Thus was Nachor brought forward, who pretended to be Barlaam. The King had indeed planned this for him; but the wise and heavenly providence of God steered the matter otherwise. In the presence of all these together, the King spoke to his orators and <term>Philosophos</term> <note>original: "Philosophos" — Philosophers; the text here suggests they are "misleaders" of the people</note>, or more fittingly, misleaders of the people and of a foolish heart: "Look, here a struggle is set before you, and a great one indeed. For one of two things shall happen to you: specifically, if you strengthen our teaching and convict Barlaam and his companions of their error—

<meta>catchword: the</meta>

# of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat #

—then you shall obtain the highest glory and honor, both from us and from the entire council, and be crowned with wreaths of victory. Or if you, on the other hand, are defeated, you shall be handed over to the most gruesome death with all mockery and shame, and all your goods and property shall be seized for the people, so that your memory may be entirely removed from the earth. I will even cast your bodies to the wild beasts and place your children into eternal servitude."

After the King finished this speech, his son spoke with these words: "A just and fair judgment you have passed today, O King; may God confirm the same. And I say the very same to my master." He then turned to Nachor, whom everyone believed to be Barlaam, and said: "It is not hidden from you, O Barlaam, in what glory and in what luxuries you found me. But you, with many words, caused me to depart from my father’s laws and ordinances and to worship an unknown God, for you drew and brought my mind to follow your teaching by the hope of certain secret and eternal goods, thus embittering the heart of my father. Therefore, think of nothing else but that you stand in the balance. For if you maintain the upper hand in this proposed struggle, and prove and demonstrate that the teaching in which you instructed me is true—and that those who oppose us today are in error—then you shall indeed carry away and achieve a greater honor than any man has ever had, and you shall be called a herald of the truth. And I will persist in your teaching and [serve] Christ, as you...

->Of the Life and Conduct /<-

I (he said), O King, have come into this world through the Providence <note>original: "Fürsichtigkeit" – God's divine foresight and purposeful guidance of the universe.</note> of God; and as I looked upon and considered the heavens, the earth, the sea, the sun, the moon, and other such things, I marveled, and not without reason, at their beauty. And as I, on the other hand, saw again that the world and everything contained within it must necessarily be moved, I understood that the one by whom all this is moved and sustained is a God. I held the particular consideration that everything which moves something else must be stronger than that which is moved; and everything that sustains and contains something must be stronger than that which is sustained and contained. Therefore, I say that He is God who created and sustains everything together, and who is without beginning, eternal, and immortal, needing nothing, and above all destruction and defects, such as anger, forgetfulness, ignorance, and other such things. Through Him, however, all things are created and have grown. Nor is He in need of any sacrifice or any other thing that comes into sight <margin>Psalm 15, Acts 17 <note>Acts 17 refers to Saint Paul's sermon in Athens about the "Unknown God" who does not live in temples made by hands.</note></margin>; but on the contrary, all these things have need of Him.

# Nachor proves that the pagan gods are false: that the Jews are also in error: and the Christians alone have the right faith.

## The 27th Chapter.

Now let this be enough said of God, through whose goodness it was granted to me to speak such things; let us now proceed to the human race and see who is in the right and who is in

<column-break/>

->of the Holy Confessor <note>A "Confessor" is a title for a saint who suffered for their faith or lived a life of heroic virtue, but was not a martyr.</note> Barlaam and Josaphat.<-

error. For this, O King, is proven to us: that there are three kinds of people in the world: namely, those who are called worshippers of idols among you; likewise Jews; and Christians. And that, on the other hand, there are three kinds of those who worship the gods: namely the Chaldeans <note>Ancient people of Mesopotamia, often associated by medieval writers with the origins of astrology and polytheism.</note>, Greeks, and Egyptians. For these (meaning the Egyptians) have been the leaders, teachers, and masters of many lands in the veneration and worship of many kinds of gods. Therefore, let us now see which ones are right and which ones err.

And the Chaldeans indeed, as they knew nothing of God, have honored the elements and began to worship created things instead of the Creator. Regarding their forms and shapes—since they have made several—they have named such a figure after the Heavens, the Earth, the Sea, the Sun, the Moon, and other elements and stars. They lock such things in their churches and call them gods. They also guard them quite diligently and carefully so that they are not stolen by thieves. And it does not enter their minds, nor do they consider, that the thing which sustains is stronger than that which is sustained, and again, that he who creates or makes something is mightier than that which is created. For if their gods are of such weakness that they cannot even provide for their own safety, how then could they bring safety <note>original: "Heyl" – can mean both safety/well-being and spiritual salvation.</note> to other people? Therefore, the Chaldeans have fallen into great error, in that they worship and venerate useless and dead images. And for that reason, O King, it surprises me not a little why those who are called "Philosophers" among them have not noticed this: namely, that the

<meta>This page continues the speech of Nachor, who is disguised as the hermit Barlaam. Following his introductory remarks about the nature of a single, eternal Creator, he now begins a systematic critique of "element worship"—the practice of deifying natural forces like the sky, earth, water, and fire.</meta>

->Of the Life and Conduct /<-

elements are also subject to destruction. Since the elements are perishable and subject to destruction, how could they possibly be gods? If the elements themselves are not gods, how could the idols built in their names be able to be gods?

Therefore, O King, we wish to turn to the elements themselves, so that we may prove that they are not gods, but are subject to destruction and change, and were created out of nothing by the command and order of the true God—who is indeed without destruction or change, and is also invisible, while He, on the other hand, sees all things and changes and alters them according to His pleasure. Why then do I speak of the elements?

Those who believe the heavens to be a god are in error. For we see that the heavens are altered and moved by necessity, and consist of many things. For that reason, they are called <term>cosmos <note>original Greek: "κόσμος" (kosmos), meaning "order" or "world-system."</note></term>, which is to say, a world. But the world is the construction of a skillful master. Whatever is built has a beginning and an end. Thus, the heavens and their stars are moved by necessity. Consider that the stars are driven with a certain order and in a certain <term>space <note>original Latin: "spacio."</note></term>, from point to point, some rising and some setting, and completing their course in a fixed time so that winter and summer may occur, just as they were commanded by God. Nor do they ever break their law with the heavenly ornament <note>This refers to the fixed "jewelry" or arrangement of the stars in the night sky.</note>, which is imposed upon them according to the unavoidable necessity of nature. From this it clearly appears that the heaven is not a god, but a work of God.

<meta>catchword: Which</meta>

***

->of the holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.<-

Which people, however, believe the earth to be a god are also in error. For we see that the earth is treated badly by men and is subject to their dominion: likewise, that it is dug up, defiled, and made useless. For if it is dried by fire, it dies; for nothing can grow from a pottery shard. If, on the other hand, it is fertilized too much by downpours, then not only the earth but also its fruits are ruined. Furthermore, it is trodden upon by men and other animals, stained with the blood of the slain, dug up, filled, and made into a container for dead bodies. Since this is the case, it is impossible for the earth to be a god; rather, it is a thing created by God for the use of men.

In the same manner, those who believe water to be a god are in error. For it was also created for the use of man and is subject to his dominion. It is also defiled and ruined, and if it is boiled by fire, it is changed; it sometimes takes on other colors, namely when it thickens or freezes from the cold, or becomes ugly and discolored from sweat, and is used for the washing away of all impure and unclean things. From which it again sufficiently appears that water is not a god, but a work of God.

But now, those who consider fire to be a god also fail greatly. For fire is likewise created for the use of men and subject to their dominion; it is used by them to boil and roast all kinds of meat, as well as to burn dead bodies, and is carried back and forth from one place to another

<meta>This page continues the systematic critique of pagan beliefs delivered by Nachor (posing as the hermit Barlaam). Having previously discussed the heavens and stars, he now moves through the remaining physical elements—specifically fire, wind, the sun, and the moon—before addressing the deification of human beings and the myths of the Greeks.</meta>

# ->Of the Life and Conduct /<-

carried around. <note>In the preceding text, the author was likely discussing fire, which is "carried around" in lamps and torches.</note> Additionally, it is corrupted in many ways and extinguished by men. Thus, it cannot be a god, but must be a work of God.

In the same way, those who believe the blowing of the wind to be a god are also in error. For it is known and manifest that the wind serves another, and was created by God for the bringing in of fruits and for other such uses. Beyond this, it is increased and diminished according to God's command and bidding. Therefore, it cannot be that the wind is a god, but only a work of God.

Furthermore, those who hold the sun to be a god err in no small way. Considering that we see it must necessarily be moved, and that it runs from one point to another and is changed: it rises and it sets, so that it may warm the trees and plants for the benefit of men, and that it has a distinction or division from the other stars, and is much, much smaller than the heavens; it also occasionally diminishes in its brightness, nor does it have any singular dominion or Lordship. From this it is now manifest that it cannot be that the sun is a god, but rather a work of God.

In the same manner, those who believe the moon to be a god are in error. For we see that it likewise is necessarily moved and changed: it also runs from one point to another, and for the benefit of men it now rises, then sets, and is smaller than the sun: it also waxes and wanes, and occasionally suffers an eclipse <note>original: "Abbruch," referring to the "breaking off" of light during an eclipse or lunar phase.</note>. Therefore, it cannot be that the moon is a god, but rather a work of God.

<meta>catchword: Lastly-</meta>

<column-break/>

# ->of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.<-

Lastly, those who believe man to be a god are also in error. For we see that he is likewise necessarily moved, nourished, and even against his will reluctantly grows old; that he is sometimes joyful and sometimes saddened, and stands in need of food, drink, and clothing. Likewise, that he is angry and envious, is inflamed by unchastity, performs repentance and penance, and has many other defects. So too is he corrupted and destroyed in many ways, namely by the elements and animals, and lastly by approaching death. From this it appears that man cannot be a god, but only a work of God.

Therefore the <term>Chaldeans <note>The author uses "Chaldeans" to refer to the ancient Babylonians and other Eastern peoples who practiced astrology and the worship of natural elements.</note></term> have fallen into a massive error, in that they follow the fictions of their own false opinions. For they honor elements which are perishable, and pray to dead images: and they are not ashamed to count these among the number of the gods.

Now we wish to come to the Greeks, so that we may see whether they perhaps hold a right view of God. Thus the Greeks, while they wish to have the praise of wisdom, have behaved even more foolishly than the Chaldeans: considering that they have introduced many gods, some of the male gender, some of the female gender, as the master-architects of all kinds of shameful <term>affections <note>In this historical context, "affections" refers to uncontrolled passions or emotional states.</note></term>, desires, and vices. For the Greeks, O King, have brought forth ridiculous, foolish, and indeed godless things, and have named those people "gods" according to their own perverted lusts and desires—people who never were, nor are gods: they did this so that, having these as protectors and patrons, they might commit fornication, robbery, murder, and in short, all kinds of heavy vices and evildoings.

<meta>This page continues the speech of the monk Barlaam (or a philosopher in the story) to the King, systematically dismantling pagan beliefs. <page-num>212</page-num>
# Of the Life and Conduct

For since their gods displayed such vices, how could they have not committed them themselves? From these errors and laws, therefore, wars, battles, and gruesome imprisonments have often arisen for many people. Thus you will also nearby, when we wish to tell specifically of their gods, hear and see an unspeakable absurdity.

In that they namely set up Saturn as a god above all others, and even slaughter their own children to him. He indeed had many children by Rhea, whom he likewise devoured out of anger and fury. But they say that Jupiter cut off his member <note>Original: *Geburtsglid*, referring to the castration of Uranus or Saturn in various mythologies</note> and threw it into the sea, from which they imagine that Venus came forth. Jupiter, however, supposedly bound his father and cast him into the abyss. Do you now see the error and the lust <note>Original: *Geylheit*, meaning lasciviousness or wantonness</note> which they attribute to their god? Can it then happen that one binds God and cuts off his member? Oh, what a singular madness! Who, being sensible and in their right mind, would say such a thing?

Secondly, they bring forward Jupiter, whom they say rules over all their gods, and how he changes himself into many forms so that he may lie with mortal women. For they report that he once transformed himself into a bull for Europa, into gold for Danaë, into a swan for Leda, into a satyr or a little man of the woods <note>Original: *Waldmännlin*</note> for Antiope, and into a lightning bolt for Semele, so that he might have his way with them and enjoy them. And that many children sprang from this afterward: namely Bacchus, Zethus, Amphion,

<meta>catchword: Hercules/</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>
# the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

Hercules, Apollo, Diana, Perseus, Castor, Pollux, Helen, Minos, Rhadamanthus, Sarpedon, and nine other daughters, whom they call the Muses.

And finally, they also bring forward something regarding a certain Ganymede. Therefore, O King, it has happened among men that, following their god, they imitated all these things: they were fornicators, adulterers, and were occupied with an insane love for boys <note>A reference to the myth of Ganymede, often used in historical polemics against pederasty</note>, and committed other terrible vices. How can it then happen, and how is it possible, that God should be a fornicator, a lover of fantasy, or a father of murderers?

Furthermore, they also speak of a god called Vulcan, who limps, has a hammer and tongs in his hand, and practices the blacksmith's trade to support himself. Thus he must ultimately suffer want. And it is a loathsome thing to say of God that he limps and that he needs the help of men.

In addition, they introduce Mercury to us as a god, who is said to be greedy, a thief, covetous, a black magician <note>Original: *Schwartzkünſtler*</note>, a deceiver, and an interpreter. Which indeed does not belong to the nature and character of the gods at all.

Moreover, they also have Aesculapius among the number of the gods, who was a physician, brewed medicines and purgatives <note>Original: *Purgatzen*, medicinal cleanses</note>, made plasters, and supported himself in this way, for he was needy. But subsequently, they say, he was struck by lightning by Jupiter on account of the son of Tyndareus the Lacedaemonian <note>Likely referring to the myth where Asclepius raised the dead, leading Jupiter/Zeus to kill him to maintain the natural order</note>, and thus killed. If Aesculapius then, while he was a god and was struck by lightning, could not help himself at that time, how would he be able to help other people now?

<meta>This page continues the speech of the monk Barlaam, who is systematically deconstructing the Greek pantheon to prove their unworthiness of worship. Having already criticized Saturn and Jupiter on the previous page for their cruelty and lust, he now moves through the other major deities—Mars, Bacchus, Hercules, Apollo, Diana, and Venus—pointing out their very human failings of anger, drunkenness, and immorality.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>
<header>Of the Life and Conduct</header>

Mars is likewise considered by them to be a severe, diligent, and wrathful god of war, and one who is greedy for cattle and other things. Later, they say that after he had defiled Venus, he was bound with chains by the boy Cupid and by Vulcan. <note>The author refers to the famous myth where Vulcan, the blacksmith god and husband of Venus, traps his wife and her lover Mars in a fine bronze net to shame them before the other gods.</note> How then can he be a god who is full of desire, who delights in wars, commits adultery, and is bound with chains?

Again, they count Bacchus among the gods, who holds nocturnal feasts, is a master of drunkenness, and a stealer of other men’s wives; likewise, they say he was an insane, fugitive man who was eventually hacked to pieces by the Titans. <note>Bacchus, or Dionysus, was famously torn apart by the Titans in one version of his myth before being reborn.</note> If Bacchus could not help himself when he was being killed, but was instead driven to madness by his own frenzy, devoted himself to gluttony, and was a fugitive, how then could he be a god?

Furthermore, they also introduce Hercules, who overloaded himself with wine and became insane because of it, strangled his own children, and subsequently perished and was consumed by fire. <note>In Greek tragedy, Hercules is driven to a temporary madness in which he kills his family; later, to escape the agony of a poisoned shirt, he builds his own funeral pyre and is consumed by flames.</note> But how can he be a god who overloads himself with wine, kills his own children, and is burned by fire? Or how will he be able to help other people if he cannot help himself?

Once more, they introduce Apollo, who is said to be envious, and carries a bow and quiver of arrows, and sometimes also holds a cithara <note>Original: *Zittern*, a zither-like instrument</note> and a transverse flute, and tells fortunes to people for the sake of profit. Thus he must have been needy. But it cannot be so, and it is impossible, that God should be needy, or envious, or a flute-player or zither-strummer.

<meta>catchword: In like</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>215</page-num>
<header>of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>

In like manner they also introduce his sister Diana, who devotes herself to the craft of hunting, has a bow and a quiver of arrows, and wanders about alone with dogs so that she may catch stags and wild boars. In what way can a huntress be a goddess, one who roams about with dogs?

Then they say that Venus is also an unchaste goddess. For at times she allowed herself to be slept with by Mars, at times by Anchises, and at times also by Adonis, whose death—as he was her lover—she mourned. Furthermore, they say Venus descended into hell <note>The author uses "hell" to refer to Hades or the Underworld.</note> so that she might release the same Adonis from Proserpina. Have you ever seen such a folly, O King, as surpasses this? Namely, to hold one for a goddess who is stained with the vice of unchastity, who weeps, and who laments.

Finally, they also introduce Adonis as a god, who was a hunter and was struck by a tusked boar, and thus perished miserably, nor could he help himself in such distress. How then will an adulterer, a hunter, and one who perished miserably be able to care for mankind?

All of this, O King, and much else besides—even more hideous, shameful, and vicious—the Greeks report and bring forward concerning their gods, things which it is right neither to speak of nor to bring to memory in any way. Therefore, men have taken the <term>occasion</term> <note>Original: *occasion*; the author means the "pretext" or "opportunity" provided by these divine examples.</note> and opportunity from their gods to commit all kinds of vice, unchastity, and godlessness, and have defiled the earth and the air with their terrible sins.

The Egyptians, however, have acted with even more foolishness and...

...surpassed in folly and strayed more grossly than all other peoples. For they were not content with honoring the Greek gods, but they also placed irrational animals—both those living in water and those on land—as well as trees, fruits, and branches among the number of the gods. They also defiled themselves with all sorts of madness and unchastity more than any other people on the entire earth. For in ancient times, they worshipped Isis, who had her own brother Osiris as a husband; he was subsequently slain by his brother Typhon. <note>Typhon is the Greek name for the Egyptian god Set.</note> And Isis fled with her son Horus into Syria to Byblos, mourning Osiris and weeping bitterly until Horus grew up and killed Typhon. Thus, neither could Isis help her brother and husband, nor could Osiris help himself when he was slain by Typhon, nor finally could Typhon, his brother’s murderer, save himself from death when he was pursued by Isis and Horus. And yet, though they became known and famous among men because of these miseries, the Egyptians regarded them as gods.

Furthermore, they were not satisfied with these and other pagan gods, but also brought forth wild animals. For some of them [worshipped] a sheep, some a goat, some a calf, some a sow, some a raven, some a hawk, some a vulture, some an eagle, some a crocodile, some a cat, some a dog, some a wolf, some a monkey, some a dragon, some a snake; some [worshipped] garlic, some onions, some thorns, and some

<meta>other</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>217</page-num>
<header>of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>

other kinds of created things they worshipped and honored. And these wretched people do not notice that there is no power in such things. For even though they have seen how their gods were eaten, roasted, burned, strangled, and destroyed by other men, they have still not understood or concluded from this that they are not gods.

Therefore, both the Egyptians and the Chaldeans, as well as the Greeks, have fallen into great error by introducing such gods, setting up their images, and regarding these mute statues—which have neither sense nor understanding—as gods. And I marvel that they did not notice they are not gods, seeing how their gods were planed, sawn, cut, and trimmed by artists and craftsmen, and grew old and changed over a long time, and were cast through the fire. For if one has no power in that which concerns his own salvation, how then could he oversee the salvation of men? But their own poets and philosophers—both of the Chaldeans and the Egyptians—in their diligence to exalt their gods through their verses and written works, have instead revealed their shame even more and made it manifest to everyone. For while the human body (though it consists of many parts) does not cast off any of its members, but has a very firm connection <note>original: "Conjunction"</note> and joining with them, thus uniting itself: how then could there be such great conflict and discord in the nature of God? For if there were only one nature of the gods, then one god should not have persecuted, or slain, or otherwise done any evil to another god. Thus...

<sig>e 3</sig>
<meta>the</meta>

<summary>Barlaam critiques the Egyptians for their worship of animals, plants, and mortal, flawed figures like Isis and Osiris. He argues that the physical creation and vulnerability of idols prove they lack divine power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Barlaam’s apology for the Christian faith. Following his critique of Egyptian and Greek idolatry, he argues that the stories of the gods are logically inconsistent with human laws and concludes by examining the beliefs of the Jews and the origins of Christianity.</meta>

# Of the Life and Conduct

<page-num>218</page-num>

the gods were attacked by other gods, killed, robbed, and struck by lightning; thus they do not possess a single nature, but rather conflicting wills, and all of these are indeed harmful and destructive. 

From this, one must conclude that none of them is God. It is therefore obvious, O King, that all their disputation regarding the nature of the gods is full of error. How is it that those who are praised among the Greeks as wise and understanding learned people have so completely failed to understand that the very ones who made the **laws** <term>original: "Gesäß", an archaic variant of "Gesetze" (laws)</term> are judged by their own laws? For if the laws are right, then their gods are entirely unjust, namely those who, contrary to the order of the law, killed one another and fell into **witchcraft**, **adultery**, **theft**, and **unchastity**. 

But if they acted rightly, then truly the laws must be wrong, as they were established against the gods. However, the laws are excellent and just, as they praise virtue and forbid vice: but the works of the gods whom they worship are unjust and godless. Therefore, their gods are also unjust; indeed, they are all godless and should be punished with death, as they set up such gods. For if that which is written of them is invented and mere fables, then they are nothing but empty and pure words. If it is according to the truth, then they are not gods who have done and suffered such things. But if such things are reported with an *allegorico sensu* <note>original: "allegorical sense"; a method of interpretation that looks for hidden spiritual meanings rather than literal facts.</note>, then they are fables and nothing more.

Thus it has been proven, O King, that all these honors which are shown to these many gods are

<meta>catchword: nothing</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>219</page-num>
### of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.

nothing but works of error and destructive harm. (It is also not fitting to call those "gods" who are seen but do not see; rather, one should worship the One who Himself cannot be seen, yet sees all things). On the contrary, the God who created everything is the one to be worshipped.

Therefore, O King, let us now come to the **Jews**, so that we may likewise see what they hold concerning God. For they descended from Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and dwelt in Egypt. From there, God led them with a mighty hand and an outstretched arm through their lawmaker **Moses**, and revealed His power to them through many signs and wonders. 

But as they also showed themselves to be unfaithful and ungrateful in like manner, they frequently practiced idolatry and killed the prophets and righteous men sent to them. And afterward, when it was God's will that His Son should come into this world, they inflicted all kinds of shame upon Him, handed Him over to the Roman **governor** <term>original: "Pfleger", referring here to the procurator or prefect</term> **Pontius Pilate**, and condemned Him to the death of the **Cross**, regardless of the many benefits and countless miracles He had shown and performed among them. Thus, they perished through their own vice. For even now, they do indeed honor the one almighty God, but not according to knowledge. For they deny that Christ is the Son of God, and they are like the heathens, although it seems as if they happen upon something closer to the truth, from which they have nonetheless strayed far. And let this be what is reported of the Jews until now.

But the **Christians** trace the origin of their teaching from our Lord **Jesus Christ**. And He is...

believed in the Son of the most high God in the Holy Spirit. Who, for the sake of human salvation, descended from heaven, and was born of a holy virgin, and without seed and without violated virginity took human flesh upon Himself, and appeared to men, so that He might turn them away from the false worship of many gods. And as He suffered death through the cross willingly and by a [divine] counsel: following this, after three days, He did rise again and ascended into heaven. Whose presence, glory, and majesty you, O King, may perceive from the Scripture, which among them is called <term>Evangelical</term><note>The author refers here to the Gospels (Evangelium), the primary records of the life of Jesus.</note>, if you are minded to read the same. This one had twelve disciples, who after His ascension into heaven distributed themselves into the provinces of the world and spread His beautiful majesty. Then also one of them came into our land and preached the teachings of truth. Therefore, those who now devote themselves to the righteousness of their preaching and apply themselves to the same are called Christians. And these are they who have found the truth before all the peoples of the world. In that they recognize God—whom they acknowledge as a Creator and <term>Master Craftsman</term><note>The German "Werckmeister" implies God as the divine architect or builder of the universe.</note> of all things—to be in His only-begotten Son and Holy Spirit: also worshipping no other god besides Him. But they have the commands of the Lord Jesus Christ engraved in their hearts and keep the same, awaiting the resurrection of the dead and the life of the world to come. They are not afflicted with any vice of unchastity or adultery: they speak no false witness: they desire no stranger's goods: they honor father and mother: love their neighbor: pass just judgment: what they

<meta>catchword: not</meta>

# of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

do not want to happen to themselves, that they do not do to others: those by whom they are offended, they pray for and reconcile them to themselves again. They strive to show good to their enemies: they are gentle and well-willing: they abstain from all unchaste cohabitation and from all impurity: they do not despise widows: and they do not grieve orphans: he who has, shares generously with the needy. When they see strangers, they take them in: and they rejoice over their arrival no differently than if it were their own physical brother. For they do not call themselves brothers after the flesh, but rather for the sake of the spirit. They are ready to lose their lives for Christ's sake. For they keep His commandments steadfastly, holily, specifically, and justly, living just as their heart and God commanded them: and also giving thanks to Him for all food, drink, and other goods. For this is the right way of truth, which indeed leads those who walk the same to the eternal kingdom, which was promised by Christ in the future life, as if with an outstretched hand. 
And so that you understand, O King, that I do not speak this in the least out of my own head or by invention, if you look into the writings of the Christians for that reason, you will find that I have reported nothing that is against the truth. Therefore, your son has well understood and perceived such things, and has quite billingly and rightly learned to worship the living God, so that he may attain salvation in the world to come. For great and wonderful is that which is spoken and acted upon by the Christians. For they do not report the word of a man, but the Word of God.
