### Chapter 28.

Now when Nachor had concluded his speech in this manner, the King burned with fierce anger. But his orators and priests were all silenced at once and could replicate and bring forth nothing in return but some poor, worthless, and useless little words. But the King's son <note>Josaphat</note> rejoiced

<column-break/>

<page-num>223</page-num>
# of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.

himself in the spirit, and with a joyful face praised the Lord, who grants a good outcome in uncertain and confused matters to those who set their hope in Him; who also, through one of His enemies, has confirmed the truth and brought it about that even he who was previously a ringleader and prominent defender of error should show himself to be a protector of the right teaching.

Now the King, although he was otherwise violently enraged at Nachor, yet he dared not do him any harm, because he had previously commanded and permitted him, in the sight of everyone, to speak freely on behalf of the Christians. Yet he sometimes opposed his speeches and gave him to understand through certain signs that he should drop the sharpness of his arguing and allow himself to be overcome by the disputation of his orators. But he <note>Nachor</note> was only strengthened all the more thereby, and rejected all their propositions and arguments by uncovering the deception of their error with which they were possessed. When this disputation had extended almost until evening, the King commanded the same to be concluded, as if he wished to treat the matter again on the following day.

But Josaphat addressed the King in this manner: Just as you gave the command at the beginning, O King, that everything should be handled justly, so you should now also satisfy justice and agree to one of two things: namely, that you either let my master <note>Nachor, whom Josaphat is pretending is his teacher Barlaam</note> remain with me tonight, so that we may confer with one another regarding the matter which we are to treat tomorrow with those who have challenged us; and that you, in turn, take your own people to yourself and discuss with them according to your pleasure

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;So the King's son retired to his palace, no differently than one who had triumphed over his enemies, and had Nachor with him. But it is not said in vain in the proverb: The fool's heart thinks upon vanity. And indeed, these thoughts of yours, and this your counsel, were in truth quite rotten, void, and foolish. But the work you have accomplished is full of all wisdom and sharpness of mind. Therefore rejoice, O Nachor, and be glad.

and did not defile your lips with any cunning hypocrisy <note>original: "Gleiſnerey"</note>, or shameful words: but rather cleansed them from all manner of impurities, in that you destroyed the error of the false gods and confirmed the truth of Christian doctrine. I have, however, endeavored to bring you here for two reasons. First, so that the King does not secretly lead you away and torture you, because you did not say that which was pleasing to him. And then, so that I might thank you for the kindness shown to me today, and might in part repay it. In what manner, though? Namely, by admonishing you to turn away from the base and dangerous path that you have held until today, and to set yourself upon the right and salutary road: which you knew very well, but with diligence you took the evil path and thus drove yourself into the terrible depths of godlessness. Understand it therefore, O Nachor, since you are so wise and highly intelligent, and strive to win Christ alone, and a solitary life with Him, with contempt for all things temporal and transitory. For you will not live forever: but since you are mortal, you will in a short time be summoned away from here, as well as all those who were before you. And, oh, how miserable you will be if you carry the heavy burden of your sins with you thither, where the righteous judgment and the righteous reward of works is, and do not cast them from you beforehand, while you are still able to do so.

Now, when the godly youth mentioned this and much more regarding repentance to Nachor—who had grown old in nothing but evil—and promised and vowed to him the forgiveness of sins, and assured him that Christ would be most kind and gracious to him, confirming it as certain that He, according to His goodness, is always ready to receive the penance of the sinner, he refreshed his sick soul, as if with various medicines, and made him entirely healthy.

Wounded with Divine love, he desired only Him who is truly to be desired, who is entirely sweet and lovely, and of such a nature that He is desired without any satiety or weariness.

### Theudas brings the King to his way of thinking by telling a fable. The King therefore sends the most beautiful women to his son. However, Josaphat overcame such temptation through the help and means of prayer.

## Chapter 30.

There once was a king who was greatly troubled that he had no heirs, and he did not consider this a small affliction. While he 

---

<meta>The catchword "lebte" confirms continuity between the fable's introduction and the description of the prince's upbringing.</meta>

lived in this state, he gained a son: at which he was then overjoyed beyond measure. However, several distinguished and famous <term>physicians</term> <note>original Latin: "Medici"</note> reported that if this little boy were to see the sun or a fire within twelve years, he would become stone-blind; for they could see this from the shape of his eyes. When the father heard this, it is said that he had a completely dark room carved into a rock and locked his son away there with several wet nurses, where he saw no light for twelve years.

When that time had passed, he led his son (who had seen nothing at all in the world until that day) out of this dark room. Then he had everything shown and presented to him in an orderly fashion, each according to its kind or nature: in one place, namely, the men; in another, the women; in the third, the gold; elsewhere, the silver; in other places, the pearls and precious stones; likewise, elegant and impressive clothes, beautiful wagons with royal horses bridled in gold, purple velvet blankets, and armed riders upon them; likewise, herds of oxen and other animals. To put it briefly, he let the boy see everything in proper order.

When the boy asked what each thing was called, the King’s <term>guards</term> <note>original: "Trabanten", refers to personal guards or attendants</note> and servants identified everything specifically for him. But when he desired to know the name for the women, the King's armor-bearer or herald said to him mockingly that they were called "Devils," because the minds of men were led into error by them. But the boy's heart was filled with love and desire for them more than for any

...captured and taken by other things. When, after investigating all things, they returned to the King, and the King desired to know from him: what among all that he had seen pleased him the most? "What else," he answered, "than those devils who so lead people astray with deceit?" <note>In the preceding fable, the prince—having never seen women before—was told by his attendants that they were "devils who deceive men."</note> For my spirit, among everything I have seen this day, has been kindled by nothing more than by their love. Then the King marveled greatly at this speech from the boy, and gathered from it how turbulent and tyrannical a thing the love of women is. "Therefore," <note>Theudas the magician is speaking to the King.</note> "make your calculation entirely: that you will be able to subdue your son and bring him into your power in no other way than this."

The King accepts this advice and immediately orders the most select of the most beautiful young maidens to be led to him. When he had dressed them in the most beautiful and ornate manner, and arranged everything so that they might entice the young man's spirit to themselves, he removed all his servants from the palace and placed the maidens in their stead. These now clung to him everywhere, embraced him, and incited him to ungodly impurity. In short, they provoked him with all their words and gestures to harmful pleasures. He had no one else he could look at, or with whom he could speak and eat, for they performed all his services. And the King indeed arranged all this.

He did this only so that the enemy might not perhaps catch his sluggish and careless soul, and easily suggest evil and base thoughts to it, and thereby stain and infect the purity of his mind.

—and he also completely despaired of bringing the brave, steadfast youth to a fall, he resorted to yet another and more cunning deceit (as he is at all times full of malice and never ceases to inflict disadvantage and harm), striving his utmost to fulfill that which was imposed and commanded of him by Theudas. Therefore, he then prepared and took in hand the following means.

He went to one of the most beautiful young maidens, who was also a king's daughter and had been abducted from her fatherland into captivity and presented to King Avenir as a magnificent and noble gift: she, as a paragon of a beautiful human being, was sent to his son to cast down and pervert his mind. To her stepped the evil perverter, and whispered into her such speeches with which she gave sufficient sign of the wisdom and understanding of her mind: for the devil knows how to practice all skills suited for sin and vice quite nimbly and easily. Subsequently, he stepped to the right of the King's son, and likewise whispered to him to love the maiden, namely on account of her wisdom and gravity, and also for the reason that she was of such a lineage and royal descent, even though she was now robbed of her fatherland and dignity. Furthermore, he suggested such thoughts to him as to how he might convert her from miserable idolatry <term>idolatry: the worship of idols or false gods, here referring to the King's pagan religion</term> to the Christian faith.

The leaves on the trees also made a quite pleasant rustling from a very gentle breeze: and when they were moved by it, they gave off an insatiable, good fragrance. There were also places <note>original: "Stell", possibly referring to thrones or ornate pavilions</note> standing there, adorned with gold and exceedingly precious stones, which gave off a bright radiance. Likewise, lovely beds were seen there, furnished with unspeakably beautiful covers. Furthermore, little streams ran through it, which were quite bright and clear, delighting the sight beyond measure. Subsequently, those same hideous men led him across this wonderfully large field into a city of likewise unspeakable beauty and radiance. For the walls were of pure, clear, and most brilliant gold; but the battlements and bastions <term>bastions: "Pasteyen", defensive fortifications of a city</term> were of stones that no man had ever seen before. And who is there who can speak of the beauty and radiance of this city? The light, however, which shone and gleamed down from above with its rays, filled all its streets; and there were several throngs and great multitudes of those who dwelt therein, all winged and quite radiant, who sang a song that no human ear had ever heard. Afterward, he heard a voice that spoke: This is the rest of those who loved righteousness.

# Of the Life and Conduct

This is the delight and joy of those who please God the Lord in their lives, actions, and conduct. The formidable men <note>The "formidable men" are the angelic guides accompanying Josaphat through his vision of the afterlife.</note> now led him out and wished to escort him back again. But he was entirely entranced by the delight and joy of the spirit and said: "Oh no, oh, I say, do not deprive me of this joy, which cannot be expressed with any words: rather allow me to remain and dwell now in a small corner of this exceedingly great and glorious city." They gave him an answer, however: that there was no way he could remain there at this time. But after much labor and sweat, he would attain such a thing, provided he subdued himself and did violence to his own will <note>original: "jhm Gewalt anthũ" — a reference to the biblical concept that the kingdom of heaven suffers violence, meaning it requires great spiritual effort.</note>.

When they had reported this and went back across the great field, they led him into a completely dark place, which was full of all sorrow and suffering, and was entirely contrary to that light and joy he had seen before. For there was an exceedingly great darkness, full of anguish, tumult, misery, and necessity. There, a furnace was lit with fire that burned quite fiercely, and also exceedingly hideous snakes and worms, which live in the midst of the flames of fire and martyr and torment the wretched souls: and many other hideous things besides. Some were seen being pitiably consumed in the fire, and this voice was heard: "This is the place of the wicked and the perverted; here is the pain of those who defiled themselves with shameful vices." Afterward, however, he was led back out by those who had escorted him in. Then he immediately came back to himself, trembled in his whole body, and tears flowed as copiously as streams of water from his eyes; and it seemed to him that all the beauty of the aforementioned shameful maiden, as well as

### Chapter 31.

The King was now once again overcome by intense sorrow and began to lose all hope for his life; and as he weighed many bitter thoughts in his mind, he returned to his palace. Meanwhile, the evil spirits who had been sent against this godly youth by Theudas returned to him in great shame; and although they usually delight in falsehood, they were forced to admit how they had been completely defeated. Indeed, they bore the visible signs of their defeat upon their faces. Theudas spoke: "Are you then so weak and wretched that you cannot overcome a single youth?" Then the unclean spirits (having been tormented by Divine power) began to reveal the truth with these words: "We cannot resist in the slightest the power of Christ and the sign of his suffering, which they call a Cross. For whenever that sign is made, no matter how many of us <margin>Ephesians 6 <note>The spirits reference St. Paul’s letter to the Ephesians, which discusses the "spiritual armor of God" needed to fight against evil forces.</note></margin> are present, we must all hastily flee and are driven away before it is even fully formed. Therefore, although we attacked this youth fiercely and greatly disturbed and confused him, he immediately drove us away with an angry spirit (after calling upon the help of Christ and marking himself

<column-break/>

with the sign of the Cross), creating for himself a strong help and fortification. Consequently, we did not stop there, but immediately took up the very instrument and tool with which our prince <note>The leader of the fallen spirits, or the Devil.</note> once attacked the first man and brought him under his power <note>This refers to the use of temptation—specifically the maiden sent previously—to try to cause Josaphat to fall, mirroring the temptation of Adam in Eden.</note>. To tell the truth, we considered the youth's hope to be nothing; but when he again called upon Christ for assistance, He burned us with the fire of Divine wrath and put us to flight, so that we no longer dare to go near him." In this manner, the wicked spirits gave Theudas a detailed account of what had occurred.

The King, however, became utterly distressed, confounded, and void of both comfort and counsel. He therefore summoned Theudas back to him and said: "Even though everything you commanded has been diligently followed, O most wise man, no beneficial result has come of it. If you have another plan, let us try it. Perhaps we shall yet find a means to drive away this evil."

Since Theudas desired to speak with the King's son, the King took him along early the next morning and visited his son. Once he had sat down with him, the King began to address him, scolding his stubbornness and defiance with harsh words. Since the prince would not be moved by this, but continued to defend the true doctrine and say that one should consider nothing more pleasant or precious, nor more important, than the love of Christ, Theudas stepped forward with these words: "What is it that you have..."

"O Josaphat, you have been found blameworthy in the eyes of our gods, for you have fallen away from their worship! Have you so enraged the mind of the King, your father, and cast yourself down in the sight of all the people? Did you not receive your very life from these gods? Did they not give you to your father after he petitioned them and they released him from the bonds of infertility?" When this man, grown old in malice, had finished employing many such vain speeches and useless questions—even patching in several arguments and proofs from Christian doctrine with the intent to belittle it and, conversely, to confirm the worship of idols—the son of the supreme King and citizen of that city which was built by no human but by God Himself <note>A reference to the "New Jerusalem" or the "city with foundations, whose architect and builder is God" (Hebrews 11:10).</note> (having restrained himself for a moment) spoke to Theudas in this way:

"Listen, you abyss of deceit, you who are darker than palpable darkness! You vain and wretched old man, I say, you who are burdened with heavier sins than the five cities consumed by divine fire <note>The five "cities of the plain," including Sodom and Gomorrah, destroyed by fire and brimstone.</note>: Look, how do you dare to so greatly defame and mock the proclamation of salvation? For through this salvation, that which was surrounded by darkness has been enlightened; through it, those who wandered have found the right path; through it, I say, those who were perishing and oppressed by heavy servitude have been restored to their former state and freedom! only-begotten Son and the Holy Spirit—the Almighty God, I say, who is uncreated and immortal, a fountain of all good, whose power and dominion can neither be imagined nor His glory expressed? Are you not ashamed, O you wretched ones, who are food for the eternal fire and who resemble the Chaldean race <note>Historically, the Chaldeans were associated with astrology, magic, and the origins of idolatry in the ancient Near East.</note>? Are you not ashamed, I say, to pray to dead images made by human hands? For you call a hewn stone or a piece of wood, worked and carved by human skill, a 'god.' And then you take a beautiful bull, or perhaps some other of the most handsome animals of whatever kind it may be, and you sacrifice it to the dead idol. Are you so mad and senseless? The sacrifice itself is more precious than your god! For humans made the idol, but God created the sacrifice—namely, the animal. ### The 32nd Chapter.

Thereupon Theudas said to him: See, it is certain and obvious/ that many great and wise men/ who were wonderful for their virtue as well as their teaching/ established our religion/ and also all kings and princes of this world accepted it as that which is excellent/ and freed from all danger. They did this even though these men were just as subject to all hardships, tribulations, and mortality as other people. Now when Theudas was instructed in this manner, he departed from the Court immediately, betook himself to his godless cave <note>Original: "Spelunck"; used here to mean a den or a hideout for his former magical practices.</note>, and burned all his books of sorcery, as they were the beginning of all his vices and the treasury of devilish secrets. And after he had subsequently instructed him in the teachings of the Christian faith and imposed many days of fasting, he cleansed him with holy Baptism. And thereafter, for all the days of his life, Theudas performed heartfelt remorse and penance for his committed sins, and applied himself to the highest degree to reconcile himself with Almighty God through constant sighing and weeping.

# 268 Of the Life and Conduct /

# The King divides his kingdom into two equal parts with his son, by the counsel of Araches, and sends him away to rule his own. In which he converts countless people to the faith.

## The 33rd Chapter.

When all this had transpired, and the King was altogether bereft of any good counsel as to what he should do, he was encompassed by great sorrow and heartache. He therefore called his council together again to deliberate on what further actions to take regarding his son. But as they brought forth many differing opinions, Araches stood up (of whom we have made mention before, who was the most prominent among the princes and the first in the council) and spoke to the King: "O King, what more could have been started with your son than what we have already done to bring him to the obedience of our commands and the veneration of the gods? But I see clearly that we are attempting impossible things; for he is endowed, either by nature or perhaps by fate, with a stubbornness of spirit. If you are therefore minded to torture or torment him, you will not only become an enemy of nature and lose the name of a father, but you will also be deprived of him entirely, since he is clearly ready to die for Christ’s sake. Therefore, I know of nothing better than that you should do this: divide your empire with him, commanding that he rule his own portion himself as a king. And then, if the nature of business and worldly

<column-break/>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<header>of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>
<sig>M</sig>
<meta>The catchword "chen" at the bottom of page 268 and top of page 269 completes the German word "weltlichen," meaning "worldly."</meta>

worldly cares <note>Original: "weltlichen Sorg"; the catchword "chen" completes the word from the previous column.</note> bring him back to our way of thinking and to our faith and way of living, then all has gone well for us. For the habits, or the life, which have already grown strong and been fortified in a person's mind, can only with difficulty be eradicated and diminished; they are changed and talked away sooner with good and affectionate words than with force. But if he should persist in the Christian religion, you will at the very least have a lesser pain or sorrow than if you were entirely deprived of your son."

When Araches had finished this speech, all the others agreed with his opinion. And subsequently, the King consented to live by this plan.

Therefore, early the next morning, he sent for his son and addressed him in this manner: "This, O son, is my last speech that I shall make to you. If you do not immediately abide by it, and yet at least in this matter soothe the pain of my spirit, I will henceforth (you may boldly believe me) deal with you with no more kindness." When the son asked what he meant by this speech, the King answered: "For," he said, "although I have expended much toil and labor upon you, I have found you altogether rebellious and stubborn in everything, in that you never wished to obey my commands. Well then, I will take this path: I will divide my kingdom so that you may rule separately. Now you may safely take whichever path pleases you."

But this divine soul <note>Referring to Prince Josaphat.</note>, although he otherwise sufficiently perceived that the King did this only to weaken his resolve, nevertheless considered it good—

...looked to see to following it, and namely for the reason that, once out of his father's power, he might more conveniently fulfill his purpose. He therefore began to answer the King in this manner: "I had indeed intended to seek that divine man <note>referring to his teacher Barlaam</note> who showed me the way of salvation, and after I had renounced and cast off all worldly things, to spend the remainder of my life with him. But since such a pressing matter is not granted to me by you, O Father, I will obey you in this. Considering that in those matters from which no explicit destruction and separation from God can arise, it is fair and right to obey one’s father."

The King then rejoiced exceedingly beyond measure and divided all his land, which he had under him, into two equal parts; he crowned his son with a royal crown and with royal splendor, also with a noble number of guards and servants, and sent him into the part of the kingdom assigned to him. He also gave full power to all the princes, military commanders, and lords of the land who wished to go with him. Likewise, he also designated for him a mighty, large city in which he should have his royal seat, and granted him everything that befits a king.

Now when Josaphat had obtained royal power and authority in this manner and arrived in the city (in which everything necessary for the governance of the kingdom was already prepared), he immediately had the sign of Christ’s suffering—that is, the holy Cross—placed upon all its towers. On the other hand, however, he broke down all the idol temples and altars to the ground and 

<column-break/>

left absolutely nothing of the ungodliness <note>original: 'Gottlosigkeit'</note> remaining.

He also built a mighty, beautiful, and large church for Christ the Lord in the middle of the city and commanded the people that they should visit it often and honor Almighty God through the adoration of His Cross. Then he stepped forward before everyone, performed his prayer with a devout heart, and also admonished and pleaded with all those who were under him; in short, he left nothing undone so that he might bring them from superstitious error and convert them to Christ. He pointed out the deception and seduction of idolatry; he set before them the teachings of Christ; he recounted the great humility of the Son of God, full of mercy and compassion toward us, which caused Him to descend from heaven; he preached the miracles of His advent; he explained the suffering and martyrdom of the Cross through which we obtained our salvation; he reported the power of the Resurrection and the Ascension; and furthermore, he also proclaimed the terrible day of the second coming of Christ <note>the Last Judgment</note>, at which it is right to be terrified; and then also the good which is reserved for the God-fearing and the pious, as well as the pain which awaits the wicked and the ungodly. And all this he reported with very gentle and virtuous words. For he desired much more to gain fear and respect among the people through goodness and gentleness than with great force and royal power. This was also the reason why he drew everyone to himself, because he was just as moral and modest as he was wondrous in his deeds and life. Therefore it followed that because his goodness and gentleness gave great help and assistance <note>original: 'Handreichung'</note> to his power and might...

**Mm 3**

...accomplished, he brought everyone to the point that they obeyed his words.

For in a short time, everyone, both the city-dwellers and the country folk, became subject to him, being trained in the Christian religion through his word and preaching. They renounced the cursed and godless worship of idols, abstaining from all their sacrifices and veneration, and committed themselves to the true faith, which is without error; in this faith they were instructed, and thus led to Christ and reconciled with Him.

Furthermore, all the priests, monks, and several bishops, who had since then hidden themselves in the mountains and caves out of fear of his father, crawled out of their dens <note>original: "Speluncken," referring to the caves where Christians hid during the persecutions of King Abenner.</note> and came to him with joyful spirits. He went out to meet them as those who had been afflicted with so many tribulations and hardships for Christ’s sake; he received them most honorably, led them into his palace, washed their feet, cleaned their unkempt long hair, and performed all kinds of service for them <note>This act mirrors Jesus washing the feet of the disciples, signaling Josaphat's humility despite his royal status.</note>. Subsequently, he had his newly built church consecrated and appointed a bishop (who had suffered much evil for the sake of the Christian faith and had been robbed of his bishopric) as an Archbishop of the same. He was a holy man, quite zealous and experienced in spiritual customs and church statutes. Afterwards, he had a spring or pond prepared in haste and baptized those who had converted to Christ from it. At first, there were the princes and the most noblemen. Following them, those who had been soldiers and knights, and then the common people were baptized. And those who were baptized obtained not only the health of their souls, but also all those who were afflicted with bodily sickness and fevers...

<column-break/>

...came out of the aforementioned spring fresh and healthy again, with pure souls and whole bodies; thus, they regained their health, both in soul and body.

And for this reason, an unspeakable multitude of people came from all ends and places to Josaphat, desiring that he would instruct them in the teaching of the fear of God. All idolatrous temples were destroyed; likewise, all the monetary treasure kept within them was taken away. Conversely, in those same places, holy churches were built to the honor of the Almighty, which King Josaphat richly endowed with all those same treasures, as well as a great multitude of costly vestments and church ornaments. Thus, he put that base and superfluous material to very useful and good purpose.

The cursed devils, however, who dwelt in such godless temples and altars, were beyond measure intensely terrified <note>original: "geueriert," from the Latin 'vereri,' meaning to fear or be in awe/terror.</note> and driven out of them. They also cried out their manifest distress so that many people heard it. And so the entire country was freed from their deceit and seduction, and in contrast, was illuminated with the light of the Christian faith, which is entirely pure and blameless.

For the King himself was an example and model of virtue to all, and thereby he brought many to his will and opinion and inspired them. For such is the nature of a principality or kingdom: the subjects always align themselves according to their ruler, and are accustomed to love and practice that which they see the Prince taking delight in. Therefore, the fear of God increased with the help of the Almighty among them and grew daily. The King, however, devoted himself with all...

<meta>This page continues the description of King Josaphat’s reign following his conversion of the kingdom. The previous page detailed the mass baptism of his subjects and the establishment of the Church; here, the focus shifts to his personal virtues and his immense charity toward the poor and suffering.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>Concerning the Life and Conduct/</header>

Earnest in the keeping of God's commandments, he clung entirely and completely to His love. Along with this, he distributed the grace of the Divine Word, governed souls, and led them to the shore of God. For he was well aware that this is the first and foremost among the obligatory duties of a king: namely, to guide his people toward the fear of God and the upholding of justice. This he also did in his own person, taming the disorderly movements of the flesh and the impulses of the mind, making them subject to reason; he also diligently exhorted his subjects to do the same, and like a good sailor, he guided the rudder of justice. For this is the true measure, rule, and manner of governing well: namely, to conquer one’s desires and to rule over them, just as he did. For he in no way exalted himself because of his noble descent or the royal glory in which he lived (since we all have our origin only from the earth, and both rich and poor are made from a single <note>original: "Leimklotzen", literally a lump of clay or mud, referring to the biblical creation of man</note> lump of clay); instead, he constantly cast his mind into the depths of humility, contemplating with heart and thought the coming salvation, and considered himself only as one who inhabits a house for a rent. On the other hand, however, he valued as his true property that which he would enjoy after the pilgrimage of this life.

Now, when he had laudably accomplished all this, and had made all those who were subject to him—and who were afflicted with the ancient error of their fathers—into servants of Him who redeemed us with His precious blood from evil servitude, he then took up the virtue of beneficence and generosity toward the poor. For since he had already perfectly fulfilled temperance and justice 

<meta>catchword: already (schon)</meta>

<column-break/>

<page-num>275</page-num>
<header>of the Holy <term>Confessor</term><note>A "Confessor" is a saint who suffered for the faith but was not martyred.</note> Barlaam and Josaphat.</header>

already; as one who was adorned with the crown of temperance and clothed in the purple of justice. He therefore noted this: that earthly goods and riches are entirely similar and equal to the instability of flowing water. For this reason, he hurried to keep and use them in those places where neither rust nor moths eat, and where thieves neither dig nor steal <note>A reference to the Gospel of Matthew 6:19-20</note>. Thus, without any sparing, he began to distribute all his money among the poor. For he knew well that it is the duty of one who has come to great power and wealth to apply all diligence so that he might follow—as much as is possible for him—the One from whom he received such benefit and power. And that he would thereby attain the imitation of Christ, if he cared for nothing more than mercy.

Therefore, he gathered spiritual riches and the treasures of godliness and mercy (which in this world delight the mind with the hope of future joy, and in the other world gladden it with the enjoyment of the hoped-for salvation) more than gold and precious stones.

Afterwards, he searched through his prisons and [visited] those who were condemned to the mines to dig for ore, as well as those who were hard-pressed by their creditors; and in short, he shared and gave everything to everyone quite richly and abundantly. He was a father to all the abandoned, the poor, and orphans—yes, a merciful and kind father, I say—and so disposed that he believed whenever he did something good for them, he had done it for himself. For because he was a generous...

<summary>This passage describes King Josaphat's transition from a just ruler to a legendary philanthropist, emphasizing his humility and his view of earthly life as a temporary pilgrimage. He rejects material wealth, comparing it to "flowing water," and systematically distributes his fortune to the poor, prisoners, and debtors to follow the example of Christ.</summary>

# On the life and conduct/

[...he] had a righteous and truly royal spirit, so he gifted all the needy most abundantly: for he hoped specifically through this to attain an unspeakably greater reward when the time should come that every man's work shall be rewarded.

As this praise of him was spread abroad everywhere in a short time, he had a daily influx of people, as if they were drawn to him by the scent of a fine ointment <note>original: "Geruch einer guten Salben"; this is a biblical allusion to the Song of Solomon 1:3, suggesting that Josaphat's holiness had a magnetic, pleasing quality.</note>, to delight themselves in the relief of poverty—both of body and soul—and to be rid of it. Everyone spoke of him. And indeed, the people were moved to this not by terror or tyrannical force, but solely by desire and the love which they generally bore toward him, being driven by God and because of his so pious and excellent life. Consequently, even those who were under his father’s territory betook themselves to him and, after laying aside all error, confessed the truth. And the House of Josaphat indeed grew and increased; on the other hand, the House of Abenner was weakened and diminished, just as is written of David and Saul in the Book of Kings <note>The author refers to 2 Samuel 3:1: "David waxed stronger and stronger, and the house of Saul waxed weaker and weaker." In the historical period of this text, the books of Samuel were often called the First and Second Books of Kings.</note>.

### King Abenner at last becomes right-minded and begins to love the Christians: he summons his son to hear from him the true teaching of the faith.

### Chapter 34.

### King Abenner wishes his dearly beloved son Josaphat happiness and salvation.

Many thoughts, my most beloved son, have surrounded my mind and have troubled and confused it beyond measure. For as I have seen how all our affairs vanish and disappear like smoke, while your religion breaks forth and shines clearer than the bright sun, I have found that which you recently told me to be entirely true. I see that we were so hardened and overwhelmed in the thick darkness of vice and godlessness that we were in no way able to behold the truth or recognize the Beginner and Creator of all things; instead, we prevented ourselves from seeing the light that shines so brightly—which you showed us—by diligently closing our eyes and inflicting much evil upon you, as well as (poor soul!) having most horribly put to death no small number of Christians: who indeed, strengthened with the help of the invincible Omnipotence, fought against our cruelty until their last breath. # On the Life and Conduct /

...a glimmer of the truth: and repentance for the vices we have committed comes upon us.

But another terribly large cloud of terrible despair desires to cover and blot out such a glimmer, in that the great multitude of my sins is presented and imagined to me; and that I am now entirely hated and cursed by Christ, and that for this reason I would not be received by Him, as one who has rebelled and fought against Him. What then is your opinion on this, O my beloved son? I beg you to inform me of this as soon as possible, and teach me what I, your father, must do; lead me, as if with an outstretched hand, to the knowledge of that which will be useful and good for me. Farewell.

When Josaphat had read this letter and its contents, his spirit was filled with unspeakable joy, and no less with wonder. <page-num>284-285</page-num>
# Of the Life and Conduct of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.

[After Josaphat had] then also spoken much to all the people about God and the fear of God, and sung of something excellent and lovely as if with a **fiery tongue**, the grace of the Holy Spirit came over them all and awakened them to praise God in such a way that everyone cried out with one voice: "Great is the God of the Christians, and there is no other God besides our Lord Jesus Christ (who is honored with the Father and the Holy Spirit)."

King Abenner, however, being filled with divine zeal, made a violent assault on his gold and silver idols that were in his palace; he threw them all down, chopped them into small pieces, and distributed them among the poor people. Thus, he turned to a useful purpose that in which no use or fruit had ever been found before. He also, together with his son, tore down and demolished all the temples and altars to the ground, and in their place, they built Christian churches. And they performed this not only in the city but also throughout the entire land with great diligence.

The evil spirits, however, who dwelt within the idols and altars, fled from there with great howling and testified with trembling voices to the invincible omnipotence of God; and in this manner, the entire land and many neighboring peoples were converted to the true faith.

Afterwards, that godly Bishop (whom we mentioned above) arrived, and then King Abenner was instructed in the <term>Catechism</term><note>A foundational summary of Christian principles used for instruction before baptism.</note> and the primary articles of the Christian faith, and in the name of God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, was

<column-break/>

baptized. And Josaphat lifted him out of that same holy baptism <note>Josaphat acted as his father's godfather, "lifting" him from the font.</note>; which was truly as new a thing as had ever been heard. For he became a father to his father and helped him toward a spiritual rebirth, he from whom he had been born in the flesh. For he was a child or son of the heavenly Father, and truly a divine fruit of the divine root; that root, I say, which calls out: <note>original: "Ich bin der Weinstock/ jhr seyt die Reben"</note> "I am the vine, you are the branches," etc.
<margin>John 15.</margin>

In such a manner the King was now reborn through the water and the Holy Spirit, with such delight and joy that it cannot be expressed. So also the entire city, indeed the whole surrounding land, was baptized along with him. And thus those became children of the Light who were previously children of darkness; and every sickness and all tribulation inflicted upon them by the loathsome devils was driven from them after they held the right faith; and they were made healthy in both body and soul. Many other miracles and signs also occurred for the confirmation of the faith.

Likewise, churches were built, and those bishops who had hidden themselves out of fear came forth and took up their bishoprics again. Furthermore, others—both priests and monks—were ordained and appointed to shepherd the flock of Christ.

Now when King Abenner had in this way departed from his evil and shameful life and performed penance for his committed sins, he handed over all his kingdom and power to his son. He himself, however, betook himself into the desert, scattered ashes without ceasing upon his head, uttered deep sighs, and washed himself with his tears.

He offered up such prayers to God the Lord, and in seven days he never once moved away from the grave; he neither ate, drank, nor slept, but continuously shed his tears, and with unspeakable lamentation, he persevered in prayer. Finally, on the eighth day, he returned again to the palace and distributed all his goods and money among the poor: in such a manner that no one

### Chapter 36.

On the fortieth day after the passing of his father <note>The 40th day is a traditional period of mourning and memorial in Eastern Christian customs</note>, he held his memorial again; subsequently, he summoned all the authorities, and a great number of the soldiers and common folk together, sat upon his throne, and delivered the following speech to them, so that everyone heard it.

"Look, as you yourselves have seen, my father, King Abenner, has died no differently than any other poor, simple man. And neither his riches, nor money and goods, nor his royal majesty and glory, nor even I, his most beloved son, nor any other of his friends and relatives could show him help, or relieve him of the unavoidable sentence <note>meaning the judgment of death and the divine trial following it</note>: instead, he travels before that Judge, so that he may give an account of this present life before Him, and he takes no one with him who might be an assistant to him there, except for that (whatever it may be) which he has worked. This happens and occurs to everyone together, as mor—"

### Of the Life and Conduct

Next, the King took Barachias (of whom we have made mention above) by the hand, saying: "This man, O you brothers, this man I make and ordain as your King. And although he strongly resists this, lead him, even against his will and by force, onto his royal throne; crown him with his royal crown, and place the royal ring in his hand." Following this, he stood facing the sunrise <note>The practice of praying toward the East, the direction of the rising sun, symbolized looking toward Christ, the "Sun of Righteousness."</note> and prayed for the King, specifically asking of God that He might give him grace to hold fast to the right and true faith, and to find the paths of God's commandments without any straying.

Furthermore, he also prayed for the entire people, asking help and grace for them from God, and that He might deign to grant them all those things which they would ask of Him for their benefit. After finishing this prayer, he turned to Barachias and said:

<margin>Acts 20</margin>
"See, my brother, I will command you in that which the Apostle has testified: <note>Josaphat is paraphrasing Acts 20:28, though he adapts the biblical address to "Overseers/Bishops" to apply specifically to a "King."</note> 'Take heed to yourself and to the whole flock, in which the Holy Spirit has placed you as King to govern the people of God, which He purchased through His own blood.' And just as you acknowledged God before me and honored Him with a pure mind and conscience, so now apply even greater diligence to conduct yourself in your life, actions, and omissions so that you may please Him. For the greater the territory, government, and dignity you have attained, the more you are indebted to do for Him. And therefore, be thankful to Him who has shown and proven such great benefits to you; keep His holy commandments, and flee from every path that leads you into misery. For just as among those who sail, if a common sailor does something

<meta>catchword: wrong</meta>

<column-break/>

### of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.

wrong, it brings little disadvantage to the others who sail with him; but when he who stands at the oar or the helm does not hold himself correctly, he puts the entire ship in extreme danger—indeed, he even brings it to total ruin and destruction. So it also happens in a kingdom: namely, when a subject sins, he does not inflict as much harm on another as he does to himself; but if the King himself sins, the whole kingdom is harmed thereby. Therefore, maintain yourself in goodness with all diligence and great care, as one who will have to give a great account <note>term: account (Rechenschafft) — refers to the "Final Judgment" where rulers were believed to be held especially responsible for the souls of their subjects.</note> should you neglect those things that are necessary and required of you. Hate all lust that can draw you into sin. For the Apostle speaks:

<margin>Hebrews 12</margin>
'Strive for peace toward everyone, and for holiness, without which no one will see the Lord.' Pay attention to the wheel of earthly and worldly things—how it constantly turns around, moving and tearing the aforementioned things now this way, now another. And endeavor that, in their many-fold changes, you may remain steadfast with a God-fearing mind. For to be changed along with the circumstances is an indication of a doubtful and unstable mind. But see to it that you are steadfast in virtue and fully established. Because of the pride and arrogance of temporal and fleeting honor, do not exalt yourself in the least; rather, with a purified mind, take to heart the insignificance of your base nature <note>original: "Vnachtsambkeit deiner schlechten Natur" — a reminder of human mortality and fallibility despite royal status.</note> and consider the short time of this life, and death also, which we all must die. <margin>Luke 6.</margin>
And truly, he who has received such great power must most diligently follow Him from whom he received it. In this, however, he will follow God most closely: if he lets nothing be more important to him than mercy. Moreover, nothing stirs subjects more to love and goodwill toward their sovereign than the grace of charitable deeds toward the needy. For obedience that springs from fear is much more a secret flattery, which mocks and deceives those who turn their minds toward it with a feigned name of "honor." Also, one who is obedient out of compulsion will gladly incite rebellion if the opportunity arises. But he who is held fast by the bonds of love and goodwill proves a constant obedience from his heart.

Since this is the case, be diligent and ensure that the needy have free access to you; keep your ears open toward those afflicted with poverty, so that you may also find God’s ears open to you. For as we conduct ourselves toward our fellow servants, so

Therefore, let us show mercy before the time of mercy <note>Likely referring to the Final Judgment</note>, so that we may obtain the like through the like.

Furthermore, hear another commandment pertaining to what was just mentioned: Forgive, and you will be forgiven. If you do not forgive men their trespasses, your heavenly Father will also not forgive you your sins. And for that reason, do not keep within yourself the memory of the injury and shame shown to you by others, or by those who have offended you; rather, because you yourself desire forgiveness for your sins, forgive also those who have sinned against you. For to the one who forgives, forgiveness is shared; and if we reconcile with our fellow servants, the Lord will also let His wrath against us subside. Conversely, our unfriendliness and cruelty which we practice against those who have committed something against us causes <margin>Matt. 18.</margin> our own sins not to be forgiven either. Just as you hear what happened to the man there who owed ten thousand talents, or pounds; because of the cruelty he practiced against his fellow servant, he caused the debt to be demanded of him anew. Therefore, we should take care with all diligence that the same does not happen to us; rather, we should much more remit all debt and cast away all anger from our hearts, so that our many debts may also be forgiven us.

When he had recited all of this, he knelt down and prayed again with the shedding of tears. Afterward, he turned to Barachia, whom he had ordained as King, and to all those who were in authority, and kissed them. Then something occurred which is truly to be lamented. For they all stood around him (just as if they only lived by being near him, and as if they would die immediately after his departure), and what did they not say that might move one to mercy? Or what did they leave undone by which their lamentation might be increased? They kissed and embraced him, and were almost out of their minds with pain and sorrow. "Woe unto us," they cried, "with what heavy tribulation are we oppressed?" They called him a shepherd, a father, and the author of their salvation. "Through you," they said, "we have attained the knowledge of God; through you we have been delivered from all error; through you we have been freed from all evil. How will it go for us now

<column-break/>

after your departure? What evil shall befall us?" And while they said such things, they beat their breasts and wept over the tribulation that had overtaken them. But he stilled their howling and great shouting with comforting words, promising that though he could not remain with them in the body, he would nonetheless be with them in spirit.

When he had finished this speech, he went out of the palace in the sight of everyone. Immediately, everyone followed after him, even though they despaired of his return; and they fled out of the city, which they could no longer bear to look upon.

Afterward, as they had run out of the city in this manner, they could hardly be brought back to rest—and only with great effort (for he frequently exhorted them with earnestness, and occasionally let harsh words of scolding mingle with his speech). They returned unwillingly, often looking back behind them, and frequently stumbling as they walked. But some of them, who were endowed with a more zealous spirit, followed him from a distance with great lamentation until the falling night separated them from one another.

traveled about, and afterward returned again to his fatherland. And though he outwardly wore the clothes he was accustomed to wear, inwardly he had a hair shirt <note>A garment made of coarse animal hair worn against the skin as a form of penance and self-mortification.</note> upon his bare body, which Barlaam had given to him. That same night, he went into the small house of a poor man, stayed there, took off his clothes, and gave them to the poor man. And this was indeed the last act of charity that he performed for the needy.

And after he had obtained God's help and assistance through the prayers of that man as well as many other poor people, and had clothed himself with God's protection and grace—as if with a garment of salvation and a cloak of joy—he committed himself to a hermit's life. He took with him neither bread, nor water, nor anything else belonging to sustenance, nor did he wear anything on himself other than the rough hair shirt mentioned above. For he was wounded with a special longing and with a divine love for Christ, the immortal King, and he set his whole mind upon that which he loved. so my soul pants, O God, for you. My soul thirsts for the strong, living God. And just as that soul which was wounded with such love says in the Song of Songs of Solomon: I am sick with love. And again: Show me your face, let me hear

your voice, for your voice is sweet, and your face is beautiful.

With this desire for the inexpressible beauty of Christ, which like a fire one has received in the heart, the holy apostles and martyrs despised everything that can be seen with the eyes. They preferred all manner of countless pain and martyrdom to this wretched and transitory life, for they were encompassed by the love of divine beauty and mastered by the immeasurable love of the Son of God toward us.

Now, as this noble man—excellent in body, but endowed with an even more excellent, noble, and royal mind—received such fire within himself, he completely despised all earthly things and threw away all pleasures of the body. He held riches and glory, as well as all human honor, for nothing. He cast away the royal crown and the royal purple, holding such things to be lesser than spider webs. With a willing and ready mind, he committed himself to all the difficult and harsh exercises of the hermit's life, saying with the Psalmist David: My soul clings

<margin>Psalm 63</margin>
to you, O Christ; your right hand upholds me.

And as he now came into the innermost part of the wilderness, without looking back, he shook off the confusion or turmoil of transitory things like a tedious burden and a heavy chain. He was rejoiced in the spirit and cast his eyes upon Christ, for whom he burned with desire. He cried out to Him—as to the one who is present everywhere and could well hear his voice—with these words: I pray you, O Lord, let my eyes [despise] the vain goods

<meta>This page continues the account of Josaphat’s spiritual journey into the wilderness. Having renounced his kingdom and given his clothes to the poor, he is now traveling into the desert to find his teacher, Barlaam, while battling physical deprivation and spiritual temptation.</meta>

<page-num>302</page-num>
-># Of the Life and Conduct /<-

[...should not] see the things of this world: also let not the present perishability turn my mind away from the journey I have undertaken. But/ O Lord/ fill my eyes with spiritual tears <note>original: "Zéhern," an archaic term for tears (Zähren), often used in mystical literature to describe "the gift of tears" as a sign of true repentance.</note>, direct my way, and show me your servant Barlaam. To me/ I say/ show the one through whom my salvation <term>Heyl (Salvation): a term encompassing both spiritual rescue and wholeness</term> was furthered: so that I may learn the purity of this eremitic and monastic life through him, and may not be led astray by the Enemy, in whose combat I am yet inexperienced. Grant me/ O Lord/ to take the path into my hands by which I might reach you: for my soul is wounded with the desire for you, and it thirsts greatly for you, the wellspring of salvation.

He thought this constantly within himself, and spoke to God through prayer and high contemplation, thus uniting himself with Him. And in this manner, entirely content and satisfied, he completed the journey he had begun, desiring to arrive at the place where Barlaam had his dwelling. He ate nothing other than roots and herbs that were in that same wilderness. For/ as mentioned above/ he had taken nothing with him but his body and his hair shirt.

And in addition to the fact that he could make only a very meager meal—indeed, almost as little as nothing—from the wild herbs, he also suffered a great lack of water: for because he then wandered constantly in the hot sunshine after midday, he suffered all the greater thirst in that same parched and dry wilderness, and was thus vexed and plagued with the utmost and heaviest laborious misery. But desire overcame nature, and his Divine thirst, with which he was possessed, lessened the flames of his thirst for water.

But the Enemy of the Good, and the envious Devil,

<meta>catchword: as he</meta>

---

<page-num>303</page-num>
->## of the Holy Confessor <note>A "Confessor" is a title for a saint who suffered for the faith but was not martyred.</note> Barlaam and Josaphat.<-

as he beheld Josaphat's undertaking and his so vehemently burning love for God with very great annoyance, he inflicted many temptations <term>Anfechtungen: spiritual trials or assaults by the devil intended to make a believer lose faith</term> upon him in the wilderness. Namely, he held up before him his royal glory, his stately guard, and the great number of servants with whom he used to be surrounded, and renewed within him the memory of his good friends and companions. Likewise/ he suggested to him how all souls hung upon his own soul, and finally threw before him all the pleasures of this life. Afterward, he put into his mind the great harshness of virtue and its unspeakable labor: Likewise/ the weakness of the body, the inexperienced and unaccustomed tribulations and hardships: similarly the length of time, the present plague of intolerable thirst, and that he was nowhere to hope for any comfort, nor any end to such great labor and hardship. And finally, he stirred up a mighty, great dust of thoughts in his mind, just as it stands written elsewhere of the holy Anthony <note>Saint Anthony the Great (c. 251–356), the "Father of All Monks," who famously battled demonic visions in the Egyptian desert.</note>.

However, as the evil Enemy found himself far too weak to shake the resolve of his mind (for Josaphat regarded his promptings and evil counsels as absolutely nothing, in that he kept Christ before his eyes, was vehemently inflamed by holy desire, strongly confirmed with hope, and founded upon the true faith), he became entirely red with shame, like one who had been defeated—as they say—at the very first assault. Therefore he began to take another path, for he has many tracks leading to vice: and he undertook to terrify him with various phantoms and turn him away. wilderness/ wandering here and there/ and yet could

# Of the life and conduct

there. In this, he endured many temptations from evil spirits and many a hard struggle; indeed, he also suffered much labor due to the lack of herbs, upon which he was accustomed to nourish himself: for such a wilderness, being mightily dry and parched, had very few of them. But
<margin>Psalm 39</margin>
his invincible and adamantine <note>The original German "diemantiner" refers to a spirit as hard and unyielding as a diamond.</note> spirit, which was so ignited with love and desire, preferred to suffer such annoyance rather than others are accustomed to suffer for pleasure. Therefore, he had no lack of Divine help, but rather the manifold comforts which Christ—for whom he burned with desire—granted him according to the measure of his pain and labor, did gladden his mind both by day and by night.

But when the two years had passed, Josaphat still went about constantly seeking the one he desired, pouring out his tears <note>original: "Zeher," an archaic word for tears</note> abundantly to God, and calling out in this manner:

> Show me, O Lord, show me, I say, the one who shared with me the knowledge of Your name and so much good; and do not deprive me of such a great good because of the abundance of my sins, but grant me that I may also enjoy the sight of him and enter into the same struggle of spiritual practice with him.

Through God's grace and providence, however, he found a cave or grotto <note>original: "Spelunck"</note> by following the footsteps of those who had gone there, and he encountered a monk who led a hermit’s life. He embraced and kissed him with a fervent and zealous spirit, asking where Barlaam was staying. And at the same time, he revealed to him the entire state of his affairs.

When he had now heard where the house of the man he sought

<column-break/>

<page-num>307</page-num>
## the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

might be, he hurried there as quickly as possible, just like an experienced hunter who has found his game. And when he had gone several thousand paces further, upon the advice of another Desert Father <note>original: "Altvatter," referring to the ascetic elders of the Egyptian and Syrian deserts</note>, he ran along quite joyfully and surrounded by the strength of hope, just like a child who has not seen his father for a long time and now hopes to find him. For when Divine love has once taken possession of the mind, it is always much more vehement and zealous than that which comes from nature.

When he now reached the desired cave, he stood before the door, knocked, and said:

> Bless me, O Father, bless me, I say.

When Barlaam heard these words, he came out of his cave and recognized him through the Spirit; for otherwise, because of the massive change he had undergone from his previous form and the blooming beauty of his youth, he could hardly have been recognized, considering he was so burned by the sun that he was entirely black. He also had very long hair, thin cheeks, deeply sunken eyes, and his eyebrows were entirely worn away <note>original: "zerriben," literally rubbed or ground down</note> by the constant tears he shed, and he was quite parched from the great hunger he suffered.

But Josaphat immediately recognized his spiritual father, for he was still endowed with his former features. This old man then turned at once toward the rising sun and prayed with thanksgiving to God. And when he had said "Amen," they fell around each other's necks and satisfied the long-standing desire that had remained unfulfilled.

having [met], they sat down and began to speak with one another. And Barlaam began his speech with these words: "You have done heartily and well to come here, my dear son—yes, a son, I say, of God, and an heir of the heavenly kingdom through our Lord Jesus Christ, whom you have loved, and for whose sake you have not loved in vain all other perishable and fragile goods. And just as a sensible and wise merchant sells all his goods and purchases for himself the most precious pearl <note>original: "Perlein." This refers to the Parable of the Pearl of Great Price in Matthew 13:45-46, symbolizing the kingdom of heaven.</note>, so when you learned that the treasure which cannot be stolen away was hidden in the field of God's commandments, you gave up everything so that you might now buy that same field. May God the Lord deign to grant you the eternal for the perishable, and for that which is subject to decay, that which neither perishes nor grows old."

"But tell me, my dearest son, in what manner did you come here, and what kind of life have you led since my departure from you? And does your father now acknowledge God, or is he still afflicted with his former godlessness and madness, led captive by the ropes of deceit and the seduction of the sorrowful devils?"

As Barlaam inquired into all these things, Josaphat began to recount in order all that had happened from the very beginning—from the time Barlaam had departed from him until the end, when they came together again—and how God had arranged everything so fortunately and well.

When the good old man heard all this, he was overcome with joy and wonder at once; he shed his hot tears <note>original: "Zeher," an archaic word for tears</note> and said: "Honor and glory be to You, O our God, who at all times

When it was now time to refresh the body with some food, Barlaam prepared a stately meal—namely, with spiritual dishes, which nonetheless had nothing of the comfort and sweetness that is felt and tasted only with the external senses of man. For there were raw, uncooked herbs, which he had sown and cultivated with his own hands, and a few palms which were found in that same wilderness, and then also other wild herbs.

# Of the Life and Conduct

Afterward, they stood up again, and when they had performed their night prayers, they began a spiritual conversation and spent the entire night in healing talk full of heavenly wisdom, until the breaking of the dawn called them back to their customary prayer.

In this manner, Josaphat remained with Barlaam for a long time, leading this wondrous life which far surpassed the human condition <note>original: "Condition," meaning the natural state or limitations of humanity</note> and character. He followed Barlaam as a father and preceptor <note>original: "Præceptorn," a teacher or instructor</note> with all obedience and humility: namely, in such a way that he practiced all virtues and learned the manner in which one must struggle against the spiritual and invisible spirits. From this, it followed that he rooted out all evil inclinations and subjected the urges of the flesh to the spirit—no differently than a servant to his master—forgetting all luxury and joy, and ruling over sleep like an evil bondservant. And to say it in few words: he committed himself with such diligence to the struggle of the hermetic life <note>original: "einsidlerischen Lebens"</note> that even Barlaam himself, who had practiced it for so long, wondered at it and yielded to his rigor. For he took only as much of the aforementioned coarse food (in which there was neither taste nor comfort) as was necessary to barely maintain his life, so that he would not, by hastening his own death, rob himself of those rewards one hopes for through the practice of virtue. He also so thoroughly conquered his nature through nightly vigils as if he had neither flesh nor body upon him.

Prayer, however, was his constant practice: and it con-

<meta>catchword: sumed</meta>

<column-break/>

# of the Holy Confessors Barlaam and Josaphat.
<page-num>311</page-num>

sumed all his time in spiritual and heavenly contemplations, so that he did not lose a single hour, indeed, not even a single moment, in vain. And truly, this is what is most required in a proper monastic life: that one never be found idle in spiritual works. This, indeed, this brave man and nimble runner on the heavenly path accomplished perfectly, keeping the fervor and zeal of his mind constant from beginning to end; in this, 
<margin>Psalm 83.</margin> 
he prepared the ascent of his heart, stepping from one virtue to another and higher one <note>This refers to the Vulgate's Psalm 83:6-7, often translated as "going from strength to strength" or "climbing the ladder of virtue."</note>, drawing one desire after another, and applying one diligence after another without ceasing, until he finally reached the hoped-for and desired blessedness.

### Chapter 39.

AS Barlaam and Josaphat lived with one another in this manner, and held a fine and laudable contest with one another—freed from all worldly care and the sorrows of this life, their spirits pure and separated from all distraction, disorder, or confusion—the Elder, after many paths of godliness and endured labor, one day called his spiritual son to him (whom he had fathered through the Gospel) and delivered the following spiritual sermon to him.

->"By now, you should have known for a long time, O my dear friend"<-

<meta>This page continues the deathbed discourse of the elder hermit Barlaam to his disciple, Prince Josaphat. Following their long life of asceticism in the desert, Barlaam senses his end is near and provides final spiritual instructions on perseverance and the nature of the soul's struggle against demonic forces.</meta>

<header>Of the Life and Conduct /</header>
<meta>catchword: For</meta>

Josaphat, [I] have lived in this desert, and the Lord Christ promised me upon my plea that I should see you before my final end. Now I have seen it as I desired: I have seen you, I say, separated from the world and all worldly matters, and united with Christ with a completely steadfast spirit, and I see that you have reached true perfection.

Now, since the time of my passing <note>original: "Absterbens," literally "dying off" or "passing away"</note> is at hand, and I have always possessed that same longing to be with Christ, that desire is now being fulfilled. You shall commit my body to the earth and return dust to the dust. Afterwards, remain in this place and complete the spiritual life you have begun, and remember me in the best way. For I fear that the black army <note>original: "schwarze Heer," a common historical reference to a host of demons or hellish spirits</note> of the hellish enemy might bring some hindrance to my soul because of the multitude of my ignorances <note>original: "Vnwissenheiten," referring to sins or errors committed through human frailty or lack of knowledge</note>.

But you, my son, do not fear the labor of spiritual exercise, and do not slacken in your zeal because of the length of time or the snares of the Devil. Instead, strengthened by the power of Christ, boldly laugh at their weakness all the more. In the hardship of your labor and the length of time, be so prepared in your mind as if you were daily awaiting your departure from this world, and think as if every day were the first beginning and the final end of your hermit’s life <note>original: "einsidlerischen Lebens"</note>. Thus forget what lies behind and <margin>Philippians 3</margin> reach out for that which is ahead, and chase after the goal set before you, for the prize <note>original: "Kleinot," literally a jewel or treasure</note> of God’s calling from above in Christ Jesus—just as the Holy Apostle <margin>2 Corinthians 4</margin> admonishes us with these words: Therefore, we do not give up.

For though our outward man perishes and decays, the inward man is nonetheless renewed from day to day. For our current tribulation (which is temporal and light) creates for us an eternal and exceedingly weighty glory—we who do not look upon what is visible, but upon what is invisible. For what is visible is temporal, but what is invisible is eternal.

When you contemplate such things in your heart, <margin>Psalm 27 <note>original: "Pfal. 26," following the Vulgate numbering</note></margin> then conduct yourself manfully and be comforted; hold fast and, like a brave soldier, <margin>2 Timothy 2</margin> strive to please him who has recruited you as a soldier. Do not be terrified by the snares of the wicked enemy, even if he suggests thoughts of your weakness and inability, seeking thereby to hinder or weaken your resolve; remember this <margin>John 16</margin> admonition of God: In the world you shall have anguish, but be comforted, I have overcome the world. Therefore, rejoice in your heart at all times, for He has chosen you, separated you from the world, and set you before His countenance. He who called you with His holy calling is always near. <margin>Philippians 4</margin> Therefore, do not be anxious, but in all things let your requests be made known to God in prayer and supplication with thanksgiving. For He has spoken: I will not leave you. If you consider all this in the hardship of your life and spiritual exercise, then be joyful and glad, and always keep the memory of God our Lord with you. For I have thought of God, says <margin>Psalm 77 <note>original: "Pfal. 76"</note></margin> the prophet, and I have been gladdened.

But if the wicked adversary should devise a new war against you and suggest high and ambitious thoughts to you, in which he [shows] you the glory of the earth-

And which of us could ever truly repay that <margin>2 Cor. 8.</margin> debt (to which we are bound to God, because although he was rich, he yet became poor for our sake, so that through his poverty we might become rich; and although he was free from all suffering, he yet willed to be martyred so that he might redeem us from evil and wicked inclinations)? While Barlaam spoke thus, Josaphat shed an unspeakable multitude of tears, so much so that they flowed out like an overflowing spring, completely wetting and drenching him and the ground upon which he sat. But he, weeping over this separation, pleaded with him most earnestly that he might take him as a companion on this, his final journey, and that he might not be permitted to live any longer in the world after Barlaam’s passing, saying with these words: "See, O my father, why do you look only to yourself and not likewise to your neighbor? How can you practice perfect—

When Josaphat had spoken this through his weeping, the old man quieted him with kind and lovely words, and said: "God’s judgments, which surpass human sense and reason, we must not oppose in the slightest. For although I have often and many times prayed to the almighty GOD for this reason, and have undertaken, as it were, to compel Him (upon whom no force can be laid) so that we would not be separated from one another, I have nevertheless learned and perceived from His goodness that it is currently not at all useful for you to lay down the burden of the flesh. Instead, you shall still remain in spiritual exercise..."

# Of the Life and Conduct

[continued from previous page] ...so long and so much, until you have earned an even more precious crown for yourself. For you have not yet fought enough for the one that is prepared for you, but must still work a little longer, so that you may enter joyfully into the joy of your heart. For I, now a hundred-year-old man, have already spent nearly seventy-five years in this desert. And even though you have not had to endure for so long, you must still draw closer to the goal that the Lord has set for you, so that you are not less than those who bore the burden of the <margin>Matt. 20.</margin> day and the heat. <note>original: "Last deß Tags vnd der Hitz," a reference to the Parable of the Workers in the Vineyard in Matthew 20:12.</note>

Therefore, O my friend, accept with a willing spirit that which has been so ordained by GOD. For what mortal man will be able to undo that which He has decreed? You, sustained by Divine grace, have patience.

Ensure also that you are watchful in your heart against the thoughts of the Enemy, and keep the purity of your spirit as clean and whole as a most precious treasure, daily ascending to higher virtues and self-denial: so that what GOD promised to his <margin>John 14.</margin> friends might be fulfilled in you, with these words: If anyone loves me, he will keep my word, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him and make our home with him. With these and many other words, that holy soul, worthy of a theological tongue, stilled and softened Josaphat’s mournful spirit.

Subsequently, he sent him to certain brothers, who lived far from there, to fetch what was necessary for the performance of the holy Sacrifice of the Mass. <term>Meßopffer: The celebration of the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</term>

<meta>catchword: Which</meta>

---

## Of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

Which Josaphat then performed immediately with his garments girded. <note>An idiom for being prepared for travel or work.</note> For he feared that perhaps Barlaam might pay the debt of nature <note>original: "Schuldt der Natur bezahlen," a common euphemism for dying.</note> in his absence and give up his spirit to the Lord, whereby a great loss would befall him, considering that he would be deprived of his last words—indeed, of his last conversation and prayer.

Therefore, when he had completed that same long journey and brought back what was necessary for the holy Mass, the divine Barlaam offered the unbloody sacrifice to GOD the Lord. And when he had received the immaculate mysteries of Christ, <note>The consecrated bread and wine of the Eucharist.</note> which he likewise shared with Josaphat, he rejoiced in the spirit; and as he took his accustomed food, he began again to nourish and refresh Josaphat’s spirit with wholesome conversation, speaking thus: We shall no longer, my dearly beloved son, eat at one table or live under one roof in this life. For I am now undertaking the final journey of my fathers. Therefore, you must show your love for me by keeping the commandments of GOD, remaining in this place until your end, arranging your life as you have been instructed, and never neglecting the memory of my discarded and decaying soul.

Live well, therefore; rejoice and delight yourself in Christ: for you have exchanged the earthly and perishable for the eternal and imperishable goods; and the reward for your work is near, and He who gives the reward is now very close at hand. Who indeed will come [to] the vine-

For this is indeed a certain and a most worthy word, <note>original: "gewiß / vnd ein gantz werthes Wort," referencing the 'trustworthy saying' of the pastoral epistles.</note> says the holy Apostle Paul: If we die with Him, we shall live with Him: if we help suffer, we shall also reign with Him in that eternal and unending Kingdom, illuminated by incomparable Light and surrounded by the illumination of the most blessed and holy Trinity.

Barlaam spoke these things with Josaphat until evening and throughout the whole night; Josaphat wept inwardly and was heartily troubled by such a parting. As the day broke again, Barlaam stretched both his hands and eyes toward Heaven, gave God thanks, and spoke thus:

"O my LORD and GOD, you who are present everywhere and fill all things, I give you thanks that you have looked upon my humility and granted me that I have completed the course of this pilgrimage in the true faith and in the right way of your commandments. But now, O my most beloved LORD, who are so inclined toward mercy, take me up into your eternal dwelling and tabernacle, <note>original: "Tabernackel," a reference to the dwelling place of God or a temporary shelter for the soul.</note> and remember no more the sins that I have committed knowingly or unknowingly.

Preserve also this your faithful servant, whom you willed that I, your unworthy servant, should lead. Protect him from all vanity and the deception of the evil Enemy: and grant that he may overstep the rough and knotted ropes (which the Devil spreads and brings forth for the hindrance and harm of all those who are concerned for their salvation) <note>original: "knöpffechtige Strick," referring to the snares or traps set by the devil to catch the faithful.</note>. ty GOD, turn away all power of the wretched Deceiver from the face of your servant: and give him the strength and power that he may tread upon the harmful head of the Enemy of our souls. When he had completed this prayer, he embraced Josaphat with fatherly affection and love, kissed him with a holy kiss, marked himself with the sign of the holy Cross, stretched out his feet, and with inward joy—no differently than if some good friends had come to meet him—he departed on that blessed journey to receive the rewards of that same blessed life, in great age
