Of the Life and Conduct/

## **Josaphat** commits the body of **Barlaam** to the earth: and after he has persevered long in spiritual exercises/ he also passes away in death. Subsequently, the King of India carries both their bodies into his realm with great honor and reverence.

### The 40th Chapter.

**Josaphat** embraced his father with the highest reverence/ and much weeping, and after he had sufficiently washed his body with tears/ and wrapped it in the rough hair-cloth garment/ <note>A "härin Kleid" or hair shirt was a garment made of coarse animal hair worn close to the skin as a form of penance and mortification of the flesh.</note> which he had received from him while still at court/ he spoke the customary psalms over him/ all through the day and the whole night/ also singing at times/ and moistened the entire body of this so precious and blessed man/ with his tears.

On the following day/ he dug a grave next to the cave/ took the holy body upon himself/ and carried it there with great reverence/ and thus the brave/ and most beloved son committed his spiritual father to the earth: he was thus even more vehemently inflamed <note>This "inflammation" refers to a burning zeal or intensified religious devotion.</note>/ practiced himself constantly in prayer/ saying:

For my father and my mother have forsaken me/ but the Lord has taken me up. <note>original: "aber der Herz hat mich auffgenommen." The other is likewise yours: but you must give it to your father, who through your cooperation turned away from his godless life, performed penance with a heartfelt soul, and converted to GOD. Yet it seemed to Josaphat as if he could not well bear this. And he spoke: How can it be that my father should receive just as great benefits and rewards from simple penance as I, who have endured so much and such varied labor?

As he said this, it seemed to him as if he saw Barlaam before him, who rebuked him and said: These, O Josaphat, are my words which I said to you before: namely, that it would happen that once you came to great riches, you would no longer be so generous. And you were in doubt regarding this word. But now you are displeased that your father is equal to you in honor and reward, and do you not rejoice much more that your manifold prayer, which you offered for him, has been heard?

But Josaphat spoke, as he was accustomed to do: Forgive, Father, forgive me. But tell me, where do you dwell?

Thereupon Barlaam spoke: In this glorious, beautiful, and great city I have received a dwelling, in the middle street or alley of the same, which is illuminated with a mighty light. Then it seemed to Josaphat again how he begged him and entreated him that he would lead him into his tabernacle <term>tabernacle; from the Latin 'tabernaculum', meaning a tent or dwelling place, often used in a spiritual sense for a heavenly home</term> and dwell-

## of the Holy Confessor Barlaam and Josaphat.

When Josaphat now woke up again, he found his heart still embraced by that same light and that same inexpressible glory, and he praised GOD with great wonder, showing his grateful spirit.

He remained thus until the end, leading a holy, indeed angelic life on this earth, and applied himself to an even more rigorous spiritual practice after the death of the Elder <note>Referring to the death of Barlaam</note>. For when he left the earthly kingdom in the twenty-fifth year of his age and took upon himself the eremitic life <term>eremitic life; the life of a hermit or recluse seeking spiritual solitude</term>, he led a superhuman life for thirty-five years in that same harsh wilderness, as if he had no physical flesh upon him: so much so that he first rescued many souls from the throat of the harmful dragon of the devil, and preserved them pure and unharmed for GOD the LORD, and through such deeds attained apostolic grace, and with a ready will also became a martyr, confessing Christ before the face of kings and tyrants with an undaunted heart, and showing himself a most clear preacher of His Divine Majesty: likewise in the wilderness he drove out many evil spirits, overcoming them all with the power of Christ, and...

Finally, having lived in this manner, and having shown such diligent service—so well-suited to his calling—to the One who had called him, and having crucified the world to himself and himself to the world, <note>A reference to Galatians 6:14, a common trope in hagiography to describe the ascetic's total rejection of worldly desires.</note> he departed in peace to the God of peace, and journeyed to the Lord, with whose desire he was constantly embraced. In that same hour, however, when he gave up his spirit, a holy man <note>This refers to the monk who had originally helped Josaphat find Barlaam.</note>—who had been warned by GOD and who had previously shown him the way to Barlaam—arrived at his excellent body. And after he had sung over him sufficiently, and shed his tears abundantly over him (which were a sign of the love he had for Josaphat), and had otherwise accomplished everything else that is customary among Christians, he buried him in Barlaam’s grave. For it was right that the bodies of those whose souls were to be together in eternity should also be laid together.

Subsequently, by the command of a terrifying man <note>Likely an angel or a divine messenger appearing in a formidable aspect.</note> who appeared in a dream to the monk who had committed him to the earth, he immediately set out for the kingdom of India, went to King Barachia, and informed him of all the things that had happened with Josaphat.

The King, without further delay, set out on the journey with many people, and finally came to the cave. He viewed the grave, and with the inward shedding of tears, he lifted the lid and saw the bodies of Barlaam and Josaphat (whose limbs were so finely arranged together). They had in no way changed their usual color, but were still clean, whole, and uncorrupted, together with their clothes. These two holy dwellings of holy souls, which gave off a completely lovely fragrance and showed nothing repulsive, the King laid in

As this reached the ears of the common people/ an innumerable multitude of the same came from all the cities/ villages/ and bordering places/ to view and honor the holy bodies. They then also sang many holy hymns and songs of praise over them/ and lit many lamps. And one might then have fitly and well said: here are lights around the sons and heirs of the Light. <note>A poetic reference to the saints as "children of light" (Ephesians 5:8), often used in hagiography to describe those who have attained salvation.</note> Their bodies were thus buried quite honorably/ and magnificently in the church/ which Josaphat had previously built from the foundations. 
God also worked many miraculous signs through his servants/ with the driving out of diseases/ and other things/ while their bodies were still being carried/ and following how they were laid in the earth: also for a long time thereafter. And the King, together with all the people/ saw those workings/ which were done through them. And many from the neighboring lands/ who were still burdened with unbelief/ and ignorance of God/ were converted to the true faith through such signs/ which happened at their graves. And all wondered beyond measure/ who saw and heard of the angelic life <note>term: "angelic life" (German: *engelische Leben*). They praised God in everything/ who never ceases to provide help and assistance to those/ who love him/ and also rewards the same richly.
